“I’ll slap the sass outa you, you mean little coconut-dodger,” cried Banjo, “ef you call me any white man’s nigger,” and he gave Bugsy a poke in his jaw that sent him sprawling.
Bugsy got up frothy at the corners of his mouth, which was always a biological peculiarity with him, but now in his wrath it was more pronounced. He opened a large pocket knife and cried, “I’ll cut you all ways and don’t miss you throat.”
“Try it, nigger,” said Banjo, quietly enough. “Because you lick that theah South African Jew boy, you think youse got a chance against me?”
But the other boys put themselves between them and disarmed Bugsy. In the scuffle Ray’s wrist was cut and bled a great deal.
To Ray the incident recalled another, almost identical affair that happened in London. It was some time after the report of the Amritsar massacre had demonstrated that the mind of the world, shock-proof from the deeds of the great war, nevertheless could still be moved by tragic events. One evening Ray was strolling through a square with two Indians when a one-armed man stepped out of the shadow and begged alms. Evidently he was ashamed, for his hat was pulled down to hide his face. One of the Indians gave a harsh refusal, adding, as they walked on: “It is his kind the British use to make our people crawl before them in India.”
Ray felt ashamed. Ashamed that the man should be forced to beg. Ashamed of the refusal. Ashamed of himself. Ashamed of humanity. Instinctively he felt that the man who begged was not of the hateful type that does the sentry duty of the British Empire. Yet he could not feel that his Indian friend was wrong. He never gave alms in public himself, even when he could afford it. It made him feel cheap and embarrassed. But he would have liked to give something to that one-armed man. And he had not dared.
He hated the society that forced him into such an equivocal position. He hated civilization. Once in a moment of bitterness he had said in Harlem, “Civilization is rotten.” And the more he traveled and knew of it, the more he felt the truth of that bitter outburst.
He hated civilization because its general attitude toward the colored man was such as to rob him of his warm human instincts and make him inhuman. Under it the thinking colored man could not function normally like his white brother, responsive and reacting spontaneously to the emotions of pleasure or pain, joy or sorrow, kindness or hardness, charity, anger, and forgiveness. Only within the confines of his own world of color could he be his true self. But so soon as he entered the great white world, where of necessity he must work and roam and breathe the larger air to live, that entire world, high, low, middle, unclassed, all conspired to make him painfully conscious of color and race.
Should I do this or not? Be mean or kind? Accept, give, withhold? In determining his action he must be mindful of his complexion. Always he was caught by the sharp afterthought of color, as if some devil’s hand jerked a cord to which he was tethered in hell. Regulate his emotions by a double standard. Oh, it was hell to be a man of color, intellectual and naturally human in the white world. Except for a superman, almost impossible.
It was easy enough for Banjo, who in all matters acted instinctively. But it was not easy for a Negro with an intellect standing watch over his native instincts to take his own way in this white man’s civilization. But of one thing he was resolved: civilization would not take the love of color, joy, beauty, vitality, and nobility out of *his* life and make him like one of the poor mass of its pale creatures. Before he was aware of what was the big drift of this Occidental life he had fought against it instinctively, and now that he had grown and broadened and knew it better, he could bring intellect to the aid of instinct.
Could he not see what Anglo-Saxon standards were doing to some of the world’s most interesting peoples? Some Jews ashamed of being Jews. Changing their names and their religion﻿ ﻿… for the Jesus of the Christians. The Irish objecting to the artistic use of their own rich idioms. Inferiority bile of non-Nordic minorities. Educated Negroes ashamed of their race’s intuitive love of color, wrapping themselves up in respectable gray, ashamed of Congo-sounding laughter, ashamed of their complexion (bleaching out), ashamed of their strong appetites. No being ashamed for Ray. Rather than lose his soul, let intellect go to hell and live instinct!

===Chinese===

“他玩的什么把戏，伙计？”班卓问。
“他是个痛快的正经人。俺刚从他那儿弄到一百法郎。”
“怎么会？你怎么不把俺也捎上？是野路子的狠买卖吗？”
“哦不！他们跟咱们一样是游民——他和他朋友。你知道我不跟野路子买卖厮混，尽管我看得起他们。”
“你这黑叫花子真是个怪货，伙计。可你说他们跟俺们一样是游民，是啥意思？你可不是要告诉俺，他们屁股底下没坐着一笔自家的大钱吧？”
“没有，真没有。他们是装阔的游民，跟咱们一样靠一路讨钱过活，没半点找个正经差事的雄心。只不过他们专宰阔人。刚离开咱们的那位摆绅士派头，他那位搭档呢，给他们端出的是生猛的殖民地老爷腔。不过最吃得开的，还是绅士派头这一路。”
“上帝跟他一窝崽子啊！这叫什么世道！可他们穿得那么光鲜体面，是怎么弄到钱的？”
“嗐，班卓，你这根死木头！在阔人堆里讨钱，这才是顶好的路数。你穿得越光鲜、舌头越溜，把握越大。咱们才挣五个法郎的工夫，那号家伙能挣一千。他们哪位宰上一个游客，要是找不出一张五十、一百法郎的票子递过去，就臊得慌——正跟咱们在酒馆里听卖唱的卖完力气、连一个铜子儿都丢不进帽子时那种臊一样。嗐，撞上他弄到这一百法郎，真是走运。俺当时精光。上代理处去，指望美国汇来几个钱，结果一个子儿没见着。明天本来就得去扛煤或者扛粮了。”
“用不着，伙计。俺手头有两百法郎。”
“连衣服都从当铺赎出来了！你怎么弄的钱？”
“各人有各人的路数，伙计。就算是你顶好的朋友，也不能让他知道你干的每一件事。哪回你精光了，别去钻白人的煤堆把自己弄得黑上加黑。只消告诉你伙计你手头什么光景。那种煤，俺看俺搬得比你在行。”
“可你用不着那么干，小子。你有你的班卓琴替你挣饭。”
“你不是也有你那杆笔。俺要你把先前讲着的那个故事写完，念给俺听。俺看你能把它弄出个好东西来。俺是个有长远脑瓜的黑鬼。俺可不像胆小鬼那号卖艺乞儿那么闷头闷脑。俺这辈子见识过不少名堂。小人物一路发迹，自大狂哪儿也到不了。俺见过这世道什么姿势都摆过：头朝下、侧着身、骑着马、仰着背，各式各样。俺要有笔真钱，就全押在你身上，好让你有工夫把那写作的事做出个名堂。”
他们在约列特广场没找见其他弟兄。他们在广场一带的咖啡馆里找，最后在一条小街上把他们逮着了——在一家旅馆饭店门前，美国领事馆总是把落难水手安置在那儿。马尔蒂、巴格西、金杰、胆小鬼、丹格尔，还有那个爱尔兰小伙。他们围着一个个头平平的女人，站成半圈。
班卓和雷走到近前，发现是个黑人妇女。她棕得像橡木，穿一条“黑鬼棕”的裙子、一件白领白袖口的黑上衣，领口垂着一条宽宽的白色舌状飘带，手里捧着一本《圣经》。她那个星期刚从纽约直抵马赛，正给小子们讲这事儿。
“俺得了主的一道旨意。他在异象里给俺托梦，说：‘拿起你的《圣经》和赞美诗集，走。走到老远的外邦去，到一个上帝忘掉的地方，那里黑人白人多得很，全排着队往地狱的老家赶。’俺把这旨意讲给俺教会的姐妹兄弟们，大伙儿一齐又祷告了一回，那祷告就应验了，叫俺收拾停当、一路奔这马赛来，俺这就到啦。”
她把她的美国护照拿给雷看，手续齐全，法国驻纽约领事签了证。“可您从前知道马赛是什么地方吗？”他问，在她那双圣滚教式的怪眼底下觉着不自在。
“不知道哩，先生，主亲自托梦之前，俺对马赛屁也不知道。哦——哦——哦，多对啊！哦——哦——哦，多对啊！”她双手合十，如痴如醉地望着天。小子们不自在地你看看我、我看看你，又往四下里张望。她莫不是要当场把那套圣灵把戏抖出来？
“是主托梦叫俺来警戒你们大伙儿的。俺知道你们这帮年轻黑鬼为啥这么恋着这块儿，全不想想自己灵魂的得救。俺落地头一天就全看在眼里了，俺的孩子们。哼——嗯——嗯——嗯！好一个花花世界！俺眼不瞎，要是没闭起眼躲开这场俺从没见过的大罪孽游行，那是因为主悄悄嘱咐过俺：‘把眼睛睁得大大的，吉特姐妹（Sister Geter），好把这一切都看全，一样能叫俺的旨意更有力的东西也别漏掉。’”
吉特姐妹那声“哼——嗯——嗯——嗯”里头的肉感劲儿，任何白纸黑字也记录不下来。班卓问她怎么找着约列特和海员旅馆的，她说都是美国领事安排的。
“领事他打发您上这儿布道来了！”班卓喊。
“他安排俺在这儿吃住。可不是叫俺来布道，因为上头那些人跟你们这头的一样不信神、一样需要拯救。俺一落地就直奔上头去，把俺的《圣经》往那位高高在上的白人老爷桌上一放，告诉他，是至高者打发俺走这一趟，来传福音的道。”
“他就开始跟俺说，俺得搭头一班开船的直接回老家去，马赛不是俺待的地方。别问俺有没有照耶稣的意思把他数落个透。俺告诉他，他跟随便哪个别人一样需要拯救，他不过是个罪人，什么职位也救不了他的命，就算他是俺们自家的总统本尊也一样——他在这儿不过是替总统站班，就像总统替上帝和咱们国家站班一样。”
“是哩孩子们，俺把该他那份旨意给了他，跟你们等会儿要领的一样，因为上帝不看职位高低。俺还没数落完，就见圣灵已经把他牢牢拿住了——他在翻俺的证件、数俺的钱，还叫人来把俺在这儿的住处安顿妥当。”
“可是，大娘，”雷说，“您要是真为拯救的营生来的，这底下对您就太清白了。您该上游民广场去，大千世界才在那儿出没。”
“可不。就是那地方。那才是地狱，一帮小白佬魔鬼全在那儿撒欢，大娘。”班卓用闹腾无赖的腔调宣布。
“俺怕他们要把俺抓住哩。”吉特姐妹答道，脑袋朝肩上一歪，那姿势微微透着点尘世的风情，一朵笑意在饱满的棕色鼻头上漾开，照亮了她的眉眼，有那么一瞬，她竟像是卖起俏来。只一瞬，却没逃过班卓那双猿猴般灵的手眼，他赶紧在后头捅捅雷，挤了挤眼。可吉特姐妹也一样快地又变回了她的传教士本色。
“您的钱全存在上头领事馆了，大娘？”金杰问。
“嗐，没有。俺换了几个美元成这儿的法国法郎，好应付一小阵子。”
“您知道，大娘，”金杰又开口，“我们全是好孩子。我们都爱大老爷上帝，没干什么伤天害理的事，只是搁浅在这儿了，弄不着娘——弄不着船；我们又老是缺钱挨饿，所以您要是能用一点儿小钱，先帮衬我们一小把——”
“哦——主——啊——啊！救救你可怜的孩子们吧，主啊，主啊！救他们别再伺候着魔鬼挨饿，一路漂到离家老远的地狱里去，主啊！”
吉特姐妹果然抖出了那套圣灵把戏，金杰话没说完就被噎了回去；她在人行道上表演起来，活像回了老家，站进了新教奋兴布道会的地界。不消片刻，她就招得一群法国人跑了过来。店主、饭馆老板、酒吧伙计、司机、水手、码头工、姑娘、拉客的——正是这一锅花花绿绿的大杂烩，叫约列特广场永远热热闹闹有戏看。人群后头，广场上的四个警察直扑现场。滩上小子们撒腿跑了。
码头上箱子堆了一摞又一摞，几十个码头工踩着跳板把它们推轮运进船舱。出租车载着客冲进来，出租车冲出去，出租车排着队等。私家侦探站着跟港口警察说话，黑皮肤的、棕皮肤的、白皮肤的向导们嗡嗡地转。白人滩上哥们穿着发臭的破衣裳来回逡巡，像饿狗一样仰望着甲板。黑哥们没那么莽，站得稍远些。两个穿运动衣的美国白人水手正跟一位船上军官说话。一个住过医院，一个误了船，两个都由领事馆出钱安置着，等遣返回国。
好一条样式堂皇的船，论块头给人的印象，比那位用它名字命名的总统留在人们记忆里的样子还要深。环球旅行的乘客一层压一层挤满甲板。有看上去并不怎么享受旅程的老人，但更多的是旁的人——满身欢腾直往外冒的青年男女。
在这一切之上，高高稳踞在这艘大邮轮烟囱顶端的，是美元那面黄铜般雪白的字号＄。雷和同伴们站在一旁，正是几个码头工停下脚、指着那字号啐唾沫，才引去了他的注意。他心里立刻也升起一阵厌恶。他懂得那些人的手势，也明白为什么那个威风凛凛的＄，像一面红旗立在桀骜不驯的法国公牛脸前挑战。
雷想，就算掌这条航线的那人当真名叫多拉尔（Dollar，美元），在这个世界金融的新时代里，把这个字号派到世界各地去巡游，至少也是种坏趣味。一个点子在雷脑中一闪，有一瞬他想，能不能注册个专利，教美元学外交，从中捞上一笔——可这念头立刻被甲板上传来的一位年轻女士的娇声赶跑了：“小子！小子！”
她朝黑小子们打手势，他们全迎上去，巴格西抢在了头里。她是个高挑的白净姑娘，棕发偏金之间，穿一件红灰色的旅行裙，穿在身上，像第五大道名店裁出的法国新款一样惹眼。
“小子，”她低头看着巴格西说，“你是迪克西来的吗？”
“是，小姐。”
“别的人也是？”
“是，小姐，我们都是美国人。”
“听听这黑鬼，”班卓对雷说，“一句句都直奔赏钱去。”
“我原想着你们是美国孩子，”姑娘说，“可你们跑得离家这么远做什么呀？”
“我们在船上做事，小姐，”巴格西说，“这会儿正等船。”
“等船的这阵子玩得好吗？”
“还不赖，小姐，虽然我们手头老是个精光。”
“这不奇了嘛！”她说出了声，更是说给自己听。“这些有色孩子在这儿，就跟在家乡一模一样！……喂，小子，你能给我买份报吗——一份美国报？”
巴格西还没来得及答话，滩上一个南非白人小子已经插到他跟前，献起殷勤来。
“您要报纸吗，女士？我去买，您要什么都行。这城我比他熟——”
“她没跟你讨什么。她问的是俺！”巴格西已经顶到那小白人的脸上——个子跟他一般大——双手抽动着，指节敲着对手的胸脯。
“滚开，你他妈的卡菲尔——黑鬼！”那白人说。
巴格西一巴掌正糊在他脸上。“你想打？那就打。”
南非人踉跄退了一步，站稳，又朝巴格西扑回来。他是好斗的。巴格西闪过奔下巴来的一拳，贴身近战。他右脚一个快勾，把南非人掀了个仰面朝天，随即拳膝齐下压了上去。
“这不是公平打法，这不公平。”南非人喊。
“什么不公平？俺在打架，俺干的就是这个。”巴格西回敬。
几个码头工围了上来，其中一个把巴格西拽起来。南非人气疯了，又冲上来，巴格西侧身一让，要不是那条拴船靠码头的大缆绳挡着，那白小子就栽进水里了。他打着刺拳又逼回来，巴格西顺势搂住他缠斗。南非人一拳低低捣进巴格西的肚子。巴格西回敬了双头槌。缠斗散开，他猛地一头扎进南非人两腿之间，抓脚把他倒掀到半空，甩出去老远，摔了个大仰趴。那小子的脑壳没磕上码头铁柱，简直是奇迹。架就这么打完了。
巴格西朝甲板望去，看到的不是那位白净乘客，而是一个中国厨子，穿一身蓝裤黄褂的本国装束，手里托着一大只苹果派。叫巴格西意外的是，那中国人咧出一口东方牙齿——相当脏——把派递给他，一边直拍他的肩膀：
“派的拿去。我给的。你打架，好。我爱看你打架，那样地打架。派的拿去。”
那中国人又拍了拍巴格西，迈着急促的小碎步赶回跳板上去，留下他捧着美国派满心欢喜，却仍对这番心意惊诧不已，一点儿也不明白到底是怎么回事。
爱尔兰小伙就在巴格西胳膊肘边。巴格西转向他。
“他说俺打得不公平。放屁！打架就是打架。在英国，他们一旦把你撂倒，就往死里踹你。俺连脚都没朝他抬一下。”
爱尔兰小伙笑了。“别理他。兴许他以为你在给那帮游客表演拳击赛呢。”
这工夫，班卓已经接替巴格西，得了那位雍容小姐的欢心。
“那份报俺去给您买。”班卓说。
“千万买美国的，别买英国的。”她顺着跳板下来了几步，装作没看见那场架。她给了班卓一美元。
班卓把那一美元掂在手里，一副试探讨巧的滑稽样，说：“可俺得上那老远的城里头才买得着，小姐，俺还得坐出租车，光车钱就得一美元。”
“是吗？”她的眼睛飞快扫过班卓那身招摇的体面。她笑着说：“那好，再给你一美元算你自己的，外加五法郎。报纸就用这个买。我身上的法国钱就这么多。……你是南方哪块儿的？”班卓伸手接钱时她问。
他顿了一顿——轻得她没察觉——说：“诺福克，小姐。”
“诺福克！哎呀，我就是里士满的，诺福克我可熟了。你具体是哪儿的？我亲戚就住那儿。你认识史密斯家吗？”
“熟，小姐。俺从前给其中一家当过司机。那家的那位是……他是……俺想他是……”
“是查理先生家吗？”
“正是，小姐。俺就是给查理老爷开车的，还有——”
“他没跟你提过他的表亲、里士满的琼斯家吗？”
“老提，小姐。他跟他太太、一家老小，张口闭口都是琼斯家。里士满俺也熟，小姐。俺从前就住那儿，惠灵顿街——”
“这不巧得叫人没法想嘛——家乡的孩子全在这儿见着了！你们觉得这地方怎么样？”
“够呛的，小姐，等船上活的这阵子。可有船靠港的时候，干我们这行的同路哥们有时会帮衬一把；赶上这样的大邮轮，我们就在附近转悠，等乘客有什么小事要人跑腿。”
“把那两美元还我。”姑娘打开一只花色斑斓的珠子包，从班卓手里收回那两美元，递给他一张五美元票子，说：“这个你们大伙儿分了，再用那五法郎给我买报。巴黎印的也行，可千万得是美国的。”
班卓摘下帽子，行了一个漂亮的黑鬼谢礼。架打完了。姑娘指了指那个南非人，问：“他也是美国人吗？”
“不是，小姐，”班卓答，“他是英国人。”
“哦！”她轻轻呼了一声，回到上面甲板的乘客中间去——那些人也一直带着局外人的兴味看完了这场架。
---
在莱斯塔克路上，班卓赶上一辆往约列特去的有轨电车。他坐上去，到了广场，买了《纽约先驱报》和《芝加哥论坛报》的巴黎版。又搭上一辆往码头回去的电车，就此省下了出租车钱。五美元，一美元换四十法郎，咱们大伙儿分，他盘算着。弟兄们一人二十五法郎，剩下五十，归俺这个交了好运、把这戏法变到手的黑鬼。
送完报纸，他又坐上电车，在阿朗克码头一家咖啡馆下了车，他的哥们正等着他。爱尔兰小伙不在。他也撞上了好买卖，拉着一个想逛保留区的乘客打车走了。
小子们已经干掉了几瓶酒，班卓到之前是雷付的钱。
“你干嘛要自己破费？”班卓质问，“你知道这礼拜全帮数俺这摇钱树最灵，回回都正中红心十环。”
他给小子们一人二十五法郎。分赃是滩上小子们的一套礼法。走运的叫花子自己留多大头都不要紧，只要拿出一份跟大伙儿分，就把那股抱团的义气焊牢了。
小子们饿坏了，除了人头一份的份子，班卓又掏钱买了吃的。小子们吃得又多又急，老板娘只好打发人出去买面包。两张绿长桌拼在一起，摆满香肠火腿三明治、一瓶瓶红酒、满杯和半满的杯子。
“论把吃食做得可口，”马尔蒂说，“还没见过哪个地方赶得上这儿——一块肉一点菜能翻出这么多花样——除了俺老家那儿；可是——”
“少拿你那些猴子岛上的家常菜放屁，”班卓打断他，“能跟法国菜一样叫嗓子眼舒坦的，只有老家迪克西黑人的家常菜。”
“你们根本不懂西印度群岛，兄弟，”马尔蒂说，“俺娘能给你焖一锅拿咸猪肉瘦肉提味的豆子饭，把你迪克西那些玩意儿打得僵凉。”
“切！米饭是苦力的吃食，”班卓说，“俺不稀罕，除非想换换口味吃回炒杂碎。拿全世界的大米豆子来换俺一大盘玉米饼炖鸡，俺都干——”
“玉米饼！”马尔蒂嗤笑，“那是喂黑鬼的——”
“这可是你说的，”胆小鬼喊，“俺盼着你们说玉米面包，别老说玉米饼。玉米饼这个词太黑鬼腔了。”
“俺娘从前就叫它玉米饼，”班卓说，“对俺来说这就够好了。”
“俺本来要说，”马尔蒂接着讲，“当年俺在查尔斯顿、萨凡纳那南边地界，那种玉米饼吃得俺肚子都过了量，可它给我们自家的香蕉饼提鞋都不配。”
“香蕉什么玩意儿？”班卓喊，“你是说炸香蕉吧。”
“俺没记错的话可不是，伙计，”马尔蒂笑，“炸香蕉是熟香蕉做的。俺说的是香蕉饼，青香蕉擦成丝，和上椰子、香料和糖，拿香蕉叶包着烤。全世界哪儿俺也没找着比那更好吃的。那也是黑人的吃食。白老爷的桌上你永远见不着。”
“我吃过，”雷说，“确实是好东西。”
“库亚！”马尔蒂喊，得了支持喜滋滋的，“海地你也吃过。”
“当然。牙买加我也吃过。小时候我在那儿待过两年。我们那儿闹了场小革命，被赶下台的总统带着他的人马流亡牙买加。我父亲就在里头，我就是这样去的。”
“你们把俺从开头要说的话岔出去老远，”马尔蒂说，“俺要说的是：法国佬在厨房里是头一号，可在面包上差点儿意思。”
“我就喜欢法国面包，”胆小鬼说，“我牙口好。”
“俺牙口也不赖，黄皮，”马尔蒂说，“可法国面包做三明治不灵。”
“反正我不喜欢法国面包，”巴格西说，“它就像上头沟里的一个烂皮条客——皮壳挺硬，里头没瓤。”
巴格西这句妙语惹来哄堂大笑，也撩得班卓对沟里的皮条客们发了一通宣言。
“沟里那些白佬穷酸，懂什么老家那套大手笔的做法？”班卓宣称，“嗐，上头那些废物，娘们给他们的钱连身换洗的行头都置不起！嗐，他们连顿像样的饭都吃不上！可俺说了，一个对三法郎的花销都斤斤计较的男人，根本不配穿裤子。俺这辈子没见过这么多癞皮赖脸的下三烂皮条客。在老家干这行的家伙，时时刻刻都得亮出条汉子样。得阔步招摇，吹大话。得摆出他谁也不怵、谁也惹不起的谱。俺在哈莱姆认得一个叫杰科（Jerco）的家伙。嗬——咿！那可真是个招摇的活宝。记得有天夜里，俺跟几个白哥们在哈莱姆一家私酒公寓里。可他们抠门得很，只给全场买啤酒。女主人打发人去把杰科找来，那黑鬼一进门，那帮抠门白佬立马就精神了。弹钢琴的都半睡着了。杰科把他往边上一搡，叫那钢琴哭出一支泪汪汪的“布鲁斯”。他要威士忌，要葡萄酒。没多大工夫，他叫整座房子都沸腾了，白佬们也乖乖照规矩掏钱。桌子底下睡着一只小狗。杰科把它弄醒，叫女主人喂它。狗吃完了，跳起舞来。“狗步舞”就是这么起头的。”
这一回小子们的笑声把整间店都震得晃了，震倒了一个酒瓶，带翻一只杯子摔在地上，双双碎了。老板娘喊了声“当心！”，从柜台后头出来捡碎碴。班卓要赔，她不肯收。她自己也笑，尽管什么也没听懂。
三明治之后，巴格西的中国馅饼成了上等好东西。小子们把它吃了个精光，离开咖啡馆，穿过码头往约列特去。吃饱了这顿，再上防波堤睡觉已经太晚。他们便三三两两地逛着，指点船坞里的船。有一条崭新的意大利船，好一条漂亮的船，就泊在他们正修建美式混凝土仓库的地方。
小子们停下来看她，也看那座建筑。再往前走，他们撞见一个瘦脸小白孩，攥着一大块面包，硬得他只好凑在消防栓底下泡软了才咽得下去。
“瞧瞧那可怜孩子！”班卓说，“饿着，俺们却刚吃了个撑破肚皮！哦，老天！这叫什么日子！有的撑得满地狼藉，有的喝凉水压肚子里的疼。……喂！过来，小孩！”
“你要干啥？别给那白人杂种一个子儿！”巴格西喊。
“闭上你的臭嘴，丑八怪。”班卓说。
那孩子看见班卓招手，走了过去。他不懂英语。班卓给了他五法郎，孩子说了声“Merci（谢谢）”，朝附近一间小酒棚走去。
“你真是个天生的冤大头，你，”巴格西说，“白人张口就有施舍，你就不行。”
“俺才他妈不在乎那一套，”班卓回，“孩子饿了，俺就给了他一把。黑不是白，这俺兴许比你懂得多，巴格西，可是——俺说得对不对，伙计？”
班卓找不着恰当的话把心里的意思说全，说到半截断了，转向雷求援。
“你当然对。我自己也正想给孩子点什么，叫你抢了先。”
“上礼拜，”班卓说，“马尔蒂想在那条P.&O.公司的船上跟一个英国人讨钱，那头该死的白猪说他才不跟黑人讲话。今天俺又从一个南方穷白人姑娘手里给大伙儿诓来五美元。这日子逗人，你就得逗着过。”
“你可真是个挨惯了鞭子的黑鬼，”巴格西说，“这话俺说过不知几回了，还得再说一遍：一个美国黑人什么时候要露出白人家里黑奴的原形，你永远摸不准。”
“俺把你的嘴贱抽出来，你这促狭的小椰子猴，”班卓喊，“你敢说俺是哪个白人的黑鬼——”说着朝巴格西下巴就是一拳，把他掀了个四脚朝天。
巴格西爬起来，嘴角挂着白沫——这是他身上一桩天生的怪处，这回在怒火里更明显了。他撑开一把大折刀，喊：“俺把你浑身乱剁，头一刀就照顾你的喉咙。”
“来试试，黑鬼，”班卓说得够平静，“打赢了那个南非犹太白小子，你以为跟俺也有一拼？”
可别的小子们插到两人中间，下了巴格西的刀。乱推乱搡里，雷的手腕被划了一道，血涌了不少。
在雷这儿，这桩小事勾起了另一桩几乎一模一样的事，发生在伦敦。那是在阿姆利则惨案的报道传开之后不久——那报道证明，被大战的种种暴行磨出老茧的世界人心，依然还能为惨剧所动。一天傍晚，雷跟两个印度人在一个广场上散步，一个独臂人从暗影里跨出来乞讨。他显然是羞愧的，帽檐拉得低低的遮住脸。两个印度人里有一个厉声回绝了，往前走着又补一句：“英国人就是用他这号人，叫我们在印度的人民在他们面前爬。”
雷觉得羞愧。羞愧那人竟被迫乞讨。羞愧那声回绝。羞愧他自己。羞愧整个人类。
他本能地觉得，那乞讨的人并不是替大英帝国站岗的那号可恨角色。可他又没法觉得他那印度朋友错了。他自己从不当众施舍，就算施舍得起的年头也不。当众施舍叫他觉得自己廉价、叫他为难。可他真想给那独臂人一点什么。而他没敢。
他恨这个把他逼进这等暧昧处境的社会。他恨文明。有一回，在哈莱姆，他一时怨愤说过：“文明烂透了。”而他走得越远、见得越多，越觉得那句狠话的真。
他恨文明，因为它对有色人的总体态度，是要剥夺他温热的人性本能，把他变得不像人。在它底下，会思考的有色人没法像他的白人兄弟那样正常运转——对快乐或痛苦、欢欣或悲愁、温厚或冷酷、宽仁、愤怒与宽恕，做不到自发的感应和回应。
只有在他自己的有色世界的疆界之内，他才能做真正的自己。可他一踏进那个白色大世界——为了活下去，他非得在那里做工、游荡、呼吸更大的空气——那一整个世界，上层、下层、中层、没层的，全都串通好了，叫他时时刻刻痛感自己的肤色和种族。
做还是不做？刻薄还是厚道？接受、给予，还是缩手？每定一个举动，他都得掂量自己的肤色。他总被肤色的尖利后怕攫住，仿佛地狱里有只魔鬼的手，猛拽那根拴着他的绳。用双重的标准去调节自己的情感。哦，做一个有色人，一个在白色世界里既有头脑又天生温热的人，真是活地狱。除非是超人，几乎不可能。
对班卓来说这容易，他凡事都凭本能行事。可对一个本能在头脑看守之下行事的黑人来说，要在白人的文明里走自己的路，不容易。但有一件事他拿定了主意：文明休想把他生命里对色彩、欢乐、美、活力和高贵的爱夺走，休想把他变成它那一大群苍白的可怜生灵中的一个。早在还不明白这西方生活的大流向是什么之前，他就本能地跟它斗；如今他长大了、开阔了、更认得它了，他可以叫头脑来给本能帮忙。
他难道看不见盎格鲁-撒克逊的标准正在对世界上一些最有趣的民族做什么吗？一些犹太人为自己是犹太人而羞愧。改掉姓名、改掉宗教……皈依基督徒的耶稣。爱尔兰人抗议在艺术里使用自己丰美的土语。非北欧少数族裔的自卑胆汁。受过教育的黑人为本种族天生爱色彩而羞愧，把自己裹进体面的灰色里，羞愧那刚果腔的大笑，羞愧自己的肤色（拼命漂白），羞愧自己旺盛的胃口。
雷不羞愧。宁可让头脑见鬼去、凭本能活，也决不丢掉自己的灵魂！

## XIII Bugsy Comes Back at Banjo / 巴格西回敬班卓
===Original===

The Cairo Café in Joliette was packed full. An aged girl, her pale, tired features grotesque under the paint, was pounding out on the piano a tragic imitation of Raquel Meller’s song:

> “Mimosa! Mimosa!

Elle n’a pas regarde chère petite,

Mais elle a vu bien plus vite

Que son coeur palpite,

Et qu’il lui tend les bras,

Mimosa! Mimosa!”

===Chinese===

若利耶特的开罗咖啡馆挤得满满当当。一个上了年纪的姑娘，苍白疲倦的脸相在脂粉底下显得怪诞，正在钢琴上重重敲出拉克尔·梅勒那支歌的一出惨兮兮的模仿：

> "含羞草！含羞草！

她没有看，亲爱的小姑娘，

可她比谁都快看见

他的心在跳，

正向她张开臂膀，

含羞草！含羞草！"

===Original===

A slightly built Algerian rattled the drum and banged the cymbals. Young men, rigged out in fashionable regalia, burning colors from shoes to cap, danced with the girls of the quarter and with one another. Some wore proletarian blue. Egyptians, Maltese, Algerians, Tunisians, Syrians, Arabians, and Chinese bobbing up and down in ungainly jerks.
Chinese and Arab men are awkward in modern dances. They have nothing of the natural animal grace and rhythm of Negroes jazzing.
Although the Cairo was a colored bar, the Negroes hardly ever went there. Negroes and Arabs are not fond of one another﻿—even when they speak the same language and have the same religion. There is a great gulf, of biological profundity, between the ochre-skinned North-Africans and the black dwellers below the desert. The Negro’s sensual dream of life is poles apart from the Arab’s hard realism.
Bugsy, passing the Cairo, saw Latnah inside and entered. Since his misunderstanding with Banjo, the wiry little fighter was walking very much by himself. And he enjoyed it. Bugsy was happiest when he was breathing some militant resentment. He did not speak to Latnah, not knowing with whom she had come nor what she was doing, but went to the bar and called for a glass of lemonade-menthé.
Latnah spoke to him. Although she was sitting at a table with white girls and brown men, she was really alone. She knew the proprietor, who was a brown man, and had stopped for a word with him. And then she had sat down and stayed, to listen, perhaps, to the language familiar to her, which Banjo mockingly called the Arabese.
Latnah called Bugsy and shifted, without getting up, to a small unoccupied table in the corner parallel to the one from which she moved. Bugsy went over to her, taking his drink.
“What you doing heah, taking that thing away from Banjo?” he asked her.
“Banjo!” Latnah sneered. “Me no never see him. Long time him no come sleep. Banjo dirty man and no good friend.”
Bugsy was very glad that Latnah was piqued and ready to hear him unburden himself about Banjo.
“You jest now finding out he’s a dirty spade and ain’t no good?” he said. “I knowed it long time. If Banjo had had plenty a money he’d never speak to a cullud person. I know that.”
“But why?” Latnah demanded. “He black man.”
“That ain’t nothing. Him is crazy ’bout white folks. He’s a Alabama nigger or cousin to one, and jest bohn foolish about that white skin. I tell you he’ll sooner give a white beachcomber a raise than one of his own color. And you know it’s easier for a white man to bum a good raise than us to. A white man can bum off his own color, and he think him is doing a colored man a favor when he pay him the compliment a bumming him, but often when we start bumming a white man, all we get outa him is ‘damned dirty nigger’ and his red moon in our face.”
“I know Banjo little mad, but I no think he love white more than colored. No, he just like everything without thinking. He *Nègre*; he can’t love the white.”
“You don’t know that nigger like I does. He ain’t lak me and you. He is a sore-back nigger and sure got white fevah. I done listen at him talking and I knows he ain’t got no use foh your kind.﻿ ﻿… Why, did you evah see him when he made that big raise off a them boys with the music on that City Line boat? You bet you didn’t. And now you ain’t seeing him, either, since Taloufa done paid and got his suit out and give him a big raise befoh going away﻿—”
“Oh, Taloufa gone away?”
“Sure. He done take his tail away from this bum hussy.” (Bum hussy was one of Bugsy’s names for Marseilles.) “And all the money he done leave Banjo that nigger is spending in Boody Lane on that kelt that he done wasted all his duds on when he come here first. Same one that wouldn’t nevah so much as look at him when he done run through all his money and got him messed up in a fight.”
“He with her again?” Latnah asked.
“Sure. Ef you go ’long up to that there rendezvous café near Boody Lane Ise sure you find them theah together.”
The thought of Banjo having money and spending it on that girl, together with Bugsy’s intimation that this was Banjo’s real preference, made Latnah crazy with anger.
“I no understand good,” she said. “I go with white man, but only for money. White race no love my race. My race no love white.”
“Banjo ain’t like us. Him is a sore-back nigger,” said Bugsy, vindictively. “Them that likes white folks riding them all the time.”
So, thought Latnah, he no like my kind. He no man. He no good. He no got no pride of race. Me give him sleep. Me give him eat. Me give him love. Me give him money for go buy that thing. Even my money he took and went off laughing and sailor-rocking like that, away from me to spread strange joy. She had never been jealous of his change of pillow. That she understood, Orientwise. But for him to lose good money to those things in the Ditch, and for what? For the benefit of their two-legged white rats. Banjo an ofay-lover. She was seething with that deep-rooted sexual resentment that the women of the colored and white races nourish against one another﻿—a resentment perhaps even more profound among the women than among the men of the species, because it is passive, having no outlet for brutal expression.
---
While Banjo had temporarily got up strutting and looked good to the Ditch again, his first flame had fallen far down the scale to a box in the Ditch. After quitting her *maison d’amour* for picnic days with Banjo, she had found another when the strutter’s funds were exhausted. But she did not remain very long in the new place. Banjo’s grandiose way of doing things must have stirred to life dead romances in her and spoiled her for the discipline of the shuttered places. However, the change was not advantageous even if she lived now in more natural light, seeing more of the street, for she was merely a “leetah” girl and down at the very bottom.
And now, in her changed estate, she did not withhold a smile from Banjo passing by more dandified than ever and looking his handsomest. Banjo, who never bore rancor for any length of time against anybody or anything, fell again.
“Chère Blanche!” That was her name, and someone had chalked it up on the rough, weather-beaten gray door of her dark little hole-in-the-wall.
Bugsy, of course, had Banjo wrong. Banjo was no ofay-lover. He simply would not see life in divisions of sharp primary colors. In that sense he was color-blind. The colors were always getting him mixed up, shading off, fading out, running into one another so that it was difficult to perceive which was which. Any pleasing color of the moment’s fancy might turn Banjo crazy for a while.
Bugsy was wrong indeed. Banjo would put no ofay before Malty, much less Ray. If he had Latnah tangled up and lost in the general color scheme, it was because she was a woman, and he took all women as one﻿—as they came﻿—roughly, carelessly, easily.
Banjo with Ray was at the little bar not far from Boody Lane. They were playing American poker with a red-skinned tout from Martinique, and a group of Corsican and Provençal touts were playing a French game at another table. Chère Blanche had deserted the sill of her box, where she was a fixture on the lookout night and day now, and was talking to the *patronne* at the bar.
Two girls came in, one of them whistling Carmen’s song. The sharp features of the whistling girl were brown as an Arab’s, but she was Provençal. She wore a flaring pink frock and her face was smeared with rouge. She was an old and hard habitant of the Ditch, but her companion, who was new to it, was very pretty, pink-rosy and young, between fifteen and sixteen. She had just a little rouge on her lips and she had on a black frock, as if she were mourning somebody; but that was camouflage. She had not a yellow card to live the life of the Ditch, for she was too young to get one. And so she was being chaperoned and cautiously initiated into the ways by the older girl. She had been only two weeks down there, having run away, so they said, from some country place. She was very much admired, naturally, for her youth and fresh prettiness among the old girls gave her the air of a little princess among scrub-women. But there was not a latent spark of interest in her eyes. She was thin, and already a fever color was supplanting the rose of her cheek, and from the bones the flesh was sagging unpleasantly.
The boys of the Ditch who were not touts gossiped about her all the time. They said that if the police caught her they would send her away to some place of confinement and keep her there a good many years, giving her time enough to reflect. But such gossiping was merely slum sentimentality, for the ways of the Ditch were open to all eyes and police eyes, like touts’ eyes, were keen to see what they wanted to see and blind to what they wanted not to see.
It was just a month since a very interesting couple had been pounced upon and borne away. A boy of seventeen and a girl of sixteen from a little tourist town. They had come into the Ditch with something of the verve of the black beach boys. She, boy-bobbed, wearing a cerise frock, and he like a romantic *apache* in black, a red cloth around his neck, a bright cap pulled down sideways on his face and often a flower, fixed always in the corner of his mouth. The girl was usually reading *Le Film Complet*, *Mon Ciné*, and moving-picture novelettes. In the bistro where they lolled out each day they were amorous of each other in a curious way﻿—like stage folk apparently forgetful of the audience﻿—an amorousness that was as different from the monkey exhibitions of the runted touts and their ladies as a good glass of red wine was from the camouflage absinthe-and-water of the Ditch. It may have been that that young couple brought something into the scene which made them impossible to the poor old actors there. Anyway, the police were soon on to *them*.
The girl who was whistling ruffled Ray’s kinky black mat. “Pay me a drink?” she asked.
“Sure; but what will you do for it?” he demanded.
She shrugged. “What is it you want?”
“You might sing what you were whistling. Do you know the words?”
“*Putain! C’est ça?* Sure I know *Carmen*. I see it every season. I never miss it. *Carmen*, *Bohème*, *Mignon*﻿ ﻿… I love them all. But *Carmen* the most. I saw it three times one season, the artiste who played Carmen was so wonderful.”
“Let’s hear you sing it. I love it, too,” said Ray.
The girl went to the counter, drank an apéritif sec, and began singing:

===Chinese===

一个瘦小的阿尔及利亚人敲着鼓、砸着钹。小伙子们一身时鬄行头，从鞋到帽子一色的燃烧色，跟窑子区的姑娘们跳，也彼此跳。有几个穿工装蓝。埃及人、马耳他人、阿尔及利亚人、突尼斯人、叙利亚人、阿拉伯人，还有中国人，笨拙地一颠一颠跳着。
中国人和阿拉伯男人跳现代舞都发僵。他们身上一点没有黑人奏爵士时那种天生的动物般的优雅和节奏。
开罗虽是个有色人酒吧，黑人却几乎从不来。黑人和阿拉伯人彼此不亲——哪怕说同一种语言、信同一个宗教。在赭色皮肤的北非人和大漠以南的黑色居民之间，横着一道生物学意义上的深渊。黑人对生活的肉感之梦，跟阿拉伯人的硬邦邦的现实主义，隔着十万八千里。
巴格西路过开罗，看见拉特娜在里面，就进去了。自从跟班卓闹了那场误会，这个精瘦的小斗士就老是一个人转悠。他还挺受用。巴格西最高兴的就是胸中憋着一股战斗的怨气。他没跟拉特娜说话——不知道她跟谁来的、来干什么——径直走到柜台，要了一杯薄荷柠檬水。
拉特娜招呼他。她虽跟几个白人姑娘和棕色男人坐一桌，其实是独自一人。她认得老板，一个棕色人，进来跟他搭了几句话，就顺势坐下不走了，也许是为了听那对她熟悉的语言——班卓管它叫"阿拉伯话"。
拉特娜把巴格西叫过来，身子没起，挪到旁边一张空着的小桌。巴格西端着饮料过去了。
"你在这块儿干啥，把那玩意儿从班卓手里拐走？"他问她。
"班卓！"拉特娜冷笑，"俺好久没见他了。他老不来睡。班卓脏男人，不是好朋友。"
巴格西很高兴拉特娜正憋着气，乐意听他倒班卓的苦水。
"你这才看出来他是个脏黑鬼、不是好东西？"他说，"俺早知道了。班卓要有的是钱，他连正眼都不会瞧有色人一眼。这个俺知道。"
"可为什么？"拉特娜质问，"他是黑人啊。"
"那算啥。他是白人迷。他不是亚拉巴马黑奴就是那一带的亲戚，天生对那身白皮犯傻。俺跟你说，他宁可施舍一个白人混海滩的，也不给自家人一个子儿。你知道白人讨钱比咱们容易多了。白人能跟自己人讨，他跟有色人讨钱的时候还觉得是抬举他；可咱们张嘴向白人讨，多半就换来一句'脏黑鬼'，外加他冲你脸亮红屁股。"
"俺知道班卓有点儿疯，可俺不觉得他爱白人胜过有色人。不，他就是什么都不多想。他是黑鬼（Nègre），他爱不了白人。"
"你不了解那黑鬼，俺了解。他不像俺和你。他是个贱骨头黑鬼，准得了白人热病。俺听他说话听多了，俺知道他对你这号人没一点用。……嘿，他从城里线那班小子们那儿靠演奏讨了一大票，那阵子你见过他吗？你肯定没见过。这阵子你也见不着他了，塔卢法付钱把衣裳赎了出来，临走还给了他一大票——"
"哦，塔卢法走了？"
"走了。他把尾巴从这破窑子城夹走了。"（"破窑子城"是巴格西给马赛起的外号之一。）"塔卢法留给班卓的钱，那黑鬼全花在布迪巷那个白妞身上了——他刚来那阵把全部行头都糟蹋在她身上的就是那个。等他把钱花光、还陷进一场架里，她连正眼都不曾瞧他一下。"
"他又跟她好上了？"拉特娜问。
"可不。你要是到布迪巷边上那家碰头咖啡馆去，保险能撞见他俩在一块儿。"
一想到班卓有钱还花在那姑娘身上，加上巴格西点破说这才是班卓真正的偏好，拉特娜气得要疯了。
"俺不大明白，"她说，"俺也跟白人走，可俺只为钱。白人种族不爱俺的种族。俺的种族不爱白人。"
"班卓不像咱们。他是个贱骨头黑鬼。"巴格西狠狠地说，"就爱让白人骑。"
于是拉特娜想着：他不喜欢俺这号人。他不是男人。他不是好东西。他没有种族的骄傲。俺给他睡。俺给他吃。俺给他爱。俺给他钱去买那玩意儿。连俺的钱他都拿了，就那么笑着、水手似的晃着走了，离开俺去撒野。她从没为他在枕头上换人吃过醋。那个她懂，东方人都懂。可他竟把好钱丢给沟区那些东西，图什么？图便宜那两脚白老鼠。班卓是个白佬迷（ofay-lover）。她心里翻腾着那种根深蒂固的性的怨毒——有色女人和白种女人彼此之间养育的那种怨毒——这怨毒在女人中间兴许比在男人中间还深，因为它是被动的，没有一条野蛮发泄的路。
---
班卓这阵子又抖起来了，在沟区重新有了看头，他的旧相好却一路跌到了沟区的一间格子屋里。跟班卓过了些野餐日子、离开了她的窑子之后，等这位抖抖派的钱袋空了，她又找了一家。可在新地方没待多久。班卓那种大手大脚的做派一定搅活了她的旧情，坏了她守着关门的地方的规矩。可这一换并没换出好处，虽说她如今活在更自然的光线里，见的街面多了，因为她如今只是个"小娘儿"，跌到了最底层。
如今她境况变了，却照样不吝给路过的班卓一个笑脸——他比从前更花哨，出落得最帅。班卓这个人，对任何人任何事都不记长仇，又栽了。
"亲爱的布朗什（Chère Blanche）！"那是她的名字，有人用粉笔写在她那黑洞洞的小格子间那扇风吹雨打发了灰的粗板门上。
当然，巴格西冤枉了班卓。班卓不是白佬迷。他只是不肯把生活看成一块块界线分明的原色。在这个意义上他是色盲。颜色老是把他的眼光搅浑，互相渗着、褪着、跑着界，叫人分不清哪个是哪个。眼前哪一种颜色讨他喜欢，都能叫他发一阵疯。
巴格西确实说错了。班卓不会把任何白佬摆在莫尔蒂前头，更不会摆在雷前头。他要是把拉特娜搅进大色盘里认不出来，那是因为她是个女人，而他把所有女人当成一体——来一个算一个——粗粗拉拉、马马虎虎、随随便便。
班卓和雷在离布迪巷不远的那家小酒吧。他们正跟一个马提尼克的红皮肤掎客打美式扑克，另一桌一伙科西嘉和普罗旺斯掎客在打一种法国牌。"亲爱的布朗什"丢下她格子间的窗台——如今她昼夜是那里的固定角色，专管瞭望——正跟柜台上的老板娘（patronne）说话。
进来两个姑娘，一个吹着口哨，吹的是卡门的歌。吹口哨那姑娘的尖脸相跟阿拉伯人一样棕，可她是普罗旺斯人。她穿一条张开的粉红裙，脸上抹了胭脂。她是沟区的老住户、硬茬子；她那个伴儿却是新来的，很漂亮，粉红玫瑰色，年轻，十五六岁。她嘴唇上只涂了薄薄一点胭脂，穿一条黑裙子，像在给谁戴孝；可那是幌子。她没有黄卡，不能过沟区的日子，因为年纪不到，领不到。所以由大姑娘带着、看护着，一点一点小心地领进门。她下来才两个星期，据说是从什么乡下地方跑出来的。她自然叫人稀罕，在那群老姑娘中间，她的青春和水灵显得像洗衣妇堆里的小公主。可她眼睛里没有一点潜在的火花。她瘦，腮上的玫瑰色已经让烧病的颜色占了上风，肉从骨头上塌下来，塌得难看。
沟区那些不当掎客的小伙子成天嚼她的舌头。说警察要是逮住她，会把她送到什么关押的地方去，一关好多年，叫她有工夫慢慢反省。可这种闲话不过是贫民窟的多愁善感，因为沟区的行当摆在所有人眼皮底下，而警察的眼睛跟掎客的眼睛一样，想看见的看得尖，不想看见的就瞎。
就在一个月前，一对很有意思的小两口刚被扑了去。一个十七岁的男孩和一个十六岁的姑娘，从一个小旅游镇来的。他们进沟区那阵，还真有几分黑海滩小子的神气。她剪着男孩头，穿一条樱桃红裙子；他一身黑，像个浪漫的阿飞，脖子上围块红布，一顶亮帽子歪扣在脸上，嘴角常年叼一朵花。姑娘成天看《完整电影》《我的电影》和电影小说。他们每天泡在那家小酒馆里，彼此恩爱得古怪——像台上的人忘了台下还有观众——那种恩爱，跟那些没长开的掎客和他们的娘们儿的猴子把戏相比，就像一杯好红酒比上沟区冒牌的掺水苦艾酒。也许正是这对小年轻给这场景带进了什么，叫那里的穷老戏子们容不下。总之，警察很快盯上了他们。
吹口哨的姑娘揉乱了雷那头卷曲的黑毛。"请俺喝一杯？"
"行啊；可你拿什么报答？"他反问。
她耸耸肩。"你要什么？"
"你方才吹的，可以唱唱嘛。词儿你会吗？"
"婊子！就这个？（Putain! C'est ça?）当然会，俺会《卡门》。俺每季都看，一场不落。《卡门》《波希米亚人》《迷娘》……俺都爱。《卡门》最厉害。有一季俺看了三回，演卡门的那位角儿太棒了。"
"那就唱来听听。俺也爱它。"雷说。
姑娘走到柜台，灌下一杯干开胃酒，唱了起来：

===Original===

> “L’amour est un oiseau rebelle﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

> "爱情是一只自由的鸟儿……"

===Original===

Her voice was rather hoarse and soiled, incapable of holding a note or ascending very far, but her acting was superb as she sidestepped about the bistro, posing and gesturing with a cigarette between her fingers. It was her manner when she whistled that had piqued Ray to ask her to sing. She was Carmen incarnate in her acting. What a hip-shaker she was!
Comic opera was ever a thing of great joy to Ray. It gave him such a perfect illusion of a crazy, disjointed relationship of all the arts of life. Singing and acting and orchestra and all the garish hues. Fascinating mélange of disorder. No one part ever equal to the other. Like life﻿ ﻿… like love. All the world on a stage just wrong enough to be right.
Ray recalled the first time he had ascended to the gods to see Geraldine Farrar in *Carmen* at the Metropolitan in New York. Geraldine certainly did not act that Carmen stuff as brazenly well as this girl. Going down from colored Harlem to the opera then was a stealing away from his high home of heavenly “blues” and ragtime to taste some exotic morsel brought from a faraway other land of music. The pals of his milieu tapped their heads knowingly at his going among the ofay gods to throw away a dollar or two. There were so many charming things at a dollar or so a throw in Harlem. He felt a little lonely going, but was compensated afterward by the blood-tingling realization of how much the composite life of Harlem was like a comic opera. He had traveled far since those days, yet no scene had ever conveyed to him such a sensuous impression of a comic opera as Harlem.
A little lusting for opera in the Ditch was a different thing. It was quite easy to find a companion of a sort in a bistro ready for a trip to the gods of the opera. And Ray never had that feeling, as he had had it in Harlem, of going out of his own warm environment into a marble-cold world of dilettantism.
For a change, a slight operatic tune in the Ditch was not an exotic thing. Such airs flowing through the alleys were as natural as rain water washing down the gutters. It was often a delicious experience for Ray suddenly to hear a girl whistling or singing such a fascinating old favorite as “Connais tu le pays ou fleurit l’oranger﻿ ﻿…” or “Oui! On m’appelle Mimi﻿ ﻿…” or a fleeting fragmentary lilt from *La Flute Enchantée*. It was nonetheless lovely if the melody was broken by a volley of bullets tearing down some dark alley and scaring the Ditch to cover. That enhanced the color of the place as a theater. That endeared the Ditch to him. There was nothing artificial about that. It was as strikingly natural as the high-heeled fancy shoes and the pretty frocks of *popeline de soie* and *crêpe Marocain* and all the voluptuous soft feminine stuff parading there in the mud and slime and refuse. The poor overplucked chickens who loved to jazz all night to American ragtime and the music-hall hits of Mistinguett also had an ear for other kinds of music﻿—even as Ray.

===Chinese===

她的嗓子又哑又破，一个音都吊不住，也高不上去，可她的做派绝了：侧着身子在酒馆里绕来绕去，手指间夹支烟，摆姿势、打手势。正是她吹口哨的那副派头让雷起了哄她唱的念头。她演起卡门，卡门就附了体。好一个扭胯的娘们儿！
轻歌剧对雷向来是桩大乐事。它给他一种完美的幻象：生活的一切艺术疯狂地、脱了节地搅在一起。唱、做、乐队，还有一应刺眼的颜色。迷人的一锅乱炖。哪一部分也不跟哪一部分般配。像生活……像爱情。整个世界一台戏，错得恰到好处，错成了对。
雷想起他头一回爬上顶层楼座，看杰拉尔丁·法勒在纽约大都会演《卡门》。杰拉尔丁那一套卡门，可没有这姑娘演得这么泼辣痛快。那时候，从黑人的哈莱姆（Harlem）上歌剧院去，是从他那"布鲁斯"和拉格泰姆的天上老家溜出去，尝一口从老远的另一个音乐国度运来的异味。他那一圈的朋友见他上白佬的天座去扔一两块钱，都懂事地敲敲脑袋。哈莱姆一块来钱上下的迷人玩意儿可多着呢。他去的时候有点孤单，可回来就补上了：一阵血里发麻的领悟——哈莱姆的复合生活多像一出轻歌剧啊。打那些年起他走了老远，可再没有哪个场景给过他哈莱姆那样一出一出肉感的轻歌剧印象。
在沟区里馋一点歌剧，是另一回事。在小酒馆里找个伴儿一块爬歌剧院的顶层天座，容易得很。雷也再没有过在哈莱姆那种感觉——走出自己暖和的窝，进一个大理石般冷的高雅世界。
换换口味，沟区里飘一小段歌剧调子，可不是什么稀罕物。这些调子在巷子里飘着，就像雨水冲着阴沟一样自然。雷常常正听见哪个姑娘吹口哨或哼唱这么一支迷人的老玩意儿——什么"你可认识那橙花盛开的地方……"啦，"对！人家叫我咪咪……"啦，或是《魔笛》里一星半点的旋律——那滋味美极了。哪怕旋律让一阵枪声打断——子弹扫下哪条黑巷子、吓得沟区钻洞藏身——也不减它的可爱。那倒给这地方添了戏台的颜色。这就叫他把沟区放在心上。这里头没有一点做作。它自然得扎眼，就像那些高跟鞋、花哨鞋、"丝绸泡泡纱"和"摩洛哥绉"的漂亮裙子，还有那一切在泥浆、黏汗和垃圾里招摇过的柔软肉感的女人行头。那些爱通宵伴着美国拉格泰姆和米斯唐盖特的音乐厅金曲跳爵士的、拔秃了毛的可怜鸡们，对别样的音乐也照样有耳朵——跟雷一个样。

===Original===

> “L’amour est enfant de Bohême,

Il n’a jamais connu de loi;

Si tu ne m’aimes pas, je t’aime!

Si je t’aime, prends garde a toi!”

The girl flicked her skirt in Ray’s face, and, laughing, ended her song. At that moment Latnah entered the bistro. She had abruptly left Bugsy at the Cairo bar to come to the Ditch. Chère Blanche was familiarly posing against Banjo. Latnah rushed up to her and said in French: “You haven’t done him enough harm when you robbed him and got him in trouble. Now you run after him again. You no good, you damned mean slut. That for you.”
She slapped the girl in her face. The girl screamed and started toward her, but Latnah caught her flimsy frock at the neck and with one fierce jerk, ripped it apart so that it fell at Chère Blanche’s feet. And that was all her clothing. She gathered the pieces about her and fled from the café.
Banjo grabbed Latnah’s wrist. “What in hellfire you come here messing with the gal foh?”
“You fool!” cried Latnah. “Gal no love you. Because you got good clothes now and little money and she thrown out of the *maison fermé* and got no friend, not even a dirty *maquereau* wanting her, she running after you again.”
“You lemme manage mah own self and don’t come poking you’ nose in mah business, for I don’t want no black woman come messing me up.”
“I no black woman.”
“You ain’t white.”
“But I no nigger like you. So what Bugsy say is true, eh? You prefer help ofay than colored boys. You no proud of race, no like your own color. You no good then. You no come no more my house, no speak no more to me. Me finish.”
“I don’t care. You know why I went with you? I did that to change mah luck.”
“I no understand.”
“You don’t?” Banjo explained. “When I was up against it, as if the ofays hadda done hoodoohed me, I thought that by changing color I might change mah luck.”
Now Latnah understood. It humiliated her. She crumpled under Banjo’s jibe. He had spoken in a bantering way, but his words were cruel; they ate into her.
“Bye-bye, mamma.” Banjo touched her shoulder playfully﻿—“and don’t nevah you pull off no moh of that hen-scratching stuff on me.”
“Touch me again and I stick you!” She whipped her little dagger out of her bosom.
Banjo saw the silver-headed thing and recoiled quickly as from the sudden menace of a rattlesnake. His eyes and mouth popped open, his face wearing horror like an African mask.

===Chinese===

> "爱情是波希米亚的孩子，

它从来不懂什么法律；

你不爱我，我偏爱你！

我若爱你，当心你自己！"

姑娘把裙子往雷脸上一甩，笑着收了尾。正在这时，拉特娜进了酒馆。她突然把巴格西撇在开罗酒吧，赶到沟区来了。"亲爱的布朗什"正熟腻腻地偎着班卓摆姿势。拉特娜冲上去，用法语对她说："你抢了他的钱、害他吃了官司，还没害够。现在又来缠他。你不是好东西，你这下贱的骚货。这个给你。"
她一巴掌扇在姑娘脸上。姑娘尖叫着朝她扑过来，拉特娜一把攥住她脖子那里薄薄的裙衫，狠狠一扯，撕了个开膛，裙子瘫在"亲爱的布朗什"脚边。那就是她全身的行头。她把碎布片拢在身上，逃出了咖啡馆。
班卓攥住拉特娜的手腕。"你他妈跑到这儿来招惹人家姑娘干啥？"
"你这个傻子！"拉特娜喊道，"姑娘不爱你。不过是你如今有了好衣裳、有了几个钱，她被窑子撵了出来，没个相好，连个烂皮条客都不要她了，才又来缠你。"
"你让俺自己管俺自己，别拿你的鼻子来拱俺的事，俺可不要哪个黑女人来搅俺。"
"俺不是黑女人。"
"你也不是白人。"
"可俺不像你这样的黑鬼。这么说巴格西说的是真的，嗯？你宁帮白佬不帮有色弟兄。你不为种族骄傲，不喜欢自己的颜色。那你就不是好东西。你再也别来俺家，再也别跟俺说话。俺跟你完了。"
"俺不在乎。你知道俺当初为啥跟你？俺是为了改俺的运气。"
"俺不明白。"
"不明白？"班卓解释道，"俺那阵子走背字，就像叫白佬下了蛊，俺想换个颜色，兴许运气也就换了。"
这下拉特娜明白了。这是羞辱。她在班卓这句挖苦底下垮了。他说得嬉皮笑脸，可字字狠毒，咬进她肉里。
"回见，妈妈。"班卓顽皮地碰碰她的肩——"往后可别再跟俺来这套母鸡挠人的把戏。"
"再碰俺一下，俺捅了你！"她从怀里哧地抽出小匕首。
班卓看见那个银头的东西，猛地缩回去，像撞见响尾蛇突然亮出的威吓。他眼睛嘴巴一齐张开，脸上戴着恐怖，像一张非洲面具。

## XIV Telling Jokes / 讲笑话
===Original===

There was one Southern black on the beach whom Banjo and his boys hated to see and always avoided. He had come to Marseilles from a North-African port, where he had been paid off on a foreign ship. When he arrived at Marseilles, like the boy of the “Don’t-light-it-afire” story, he was highly disdainful of the beach boys and would have none of them; but he allowed himself to be picked up by some insectile Corsican *voyous* who made their headquarters in a sewer hole of a hotel-bistro of the Ditch. When his money was gone he went to the American consulate, and it was arranged for him to be returned home by an American freighter. But when the day came for him to ship he refused to go. He had got a little money somewhere. He lived on it for a few days. After that was gone he again returned to the consulate for help, but the clerk in charge of the seamen’s department would have nothing to do with him.
Then he tried to get in with the beach boys, but they would not have him. Not merely because he had scorned their company at first, but because he was a dead thing with no spark in him of the vagabond flair for life which was the soul of the beach existence. The boys called him Lonesome Blue.
Lonesome Blue had been excited by the boys’ talk of raiding the good wine of the docks. And one fine afternoon he hiked down and bunged out a barrel on the breakwater, right under the eyes of the police. He was arrested and got prison for three months and a writ of expulsion from the country effective ten days after he was released.
If you have the hard luck to get expelled from France, the department of the Sûreté Générale does not worry itself about the manner of your going. The order is, Get out! and you yourself must find the way. Because of this, many criminals merely change their names and the scene of their activities in order to remain in the most fascinating of European countries. Some of them stay in the same place, if it is, like Marseilles, big enough to hide in, having faith in their cleverness to escape the toils of the police. Ginger, for example, having got into difficulties, had been sentenced to do a little time and then to be deported. That was long, long ago. But on coming out of jail he had destroyed the expulsion paper and was still enjoying Marseilles. That is not such a simple thing as it sounds, for the police are ever on the lookout for evaders, for whose arrest they get a premium of some ten francs per head. Ginger had been caught in many a *rafle*, but his little store of colloquial French and his good-natured wit had got him through the examination every time.
Poor Lonesome Blue was tongue-tied and witless. Since his first imprisonment, he had twice been in jail for disobeying the expulsion orders, and he had made souvenirs of the papers for the benefit of the police. He had just been let out again and entered the African café on seeing Banjo and the boys, who had assembled there after lunch.
“Here is ole Lonesome Blue again,” cried Banjo. “Always exposing himself when you least expect, scarifying like a haunts.”
“Why don’t you get outa it, mah boy?” said Ginger. “Seeing as youse messed up you’self in this Frenchman’s town, why don’t you ketch you a broad and get outa it? Look how you stand.”
Lonesome Blue was in a crumpled tangle of rags, his toes poking through a poor proletarian pair of Provençal *pantoufles*, his face scabby and wearing a perpetually soured expression, as if some implacable, invisible demon had a clutch on the back of his neck.
“Youse a sick nigger,” said Banjo. “You look in a bad way to me, lak somebody done got a passport for the boneyard.”
“You don’t have to tell me that. I know it,” replied Lonesome Blue. “I know it without you saying it. Only Gawd knows how I feel,” he finished with a belly-deep groan.
“Gawd won’t hulp you a damn sight mohn the debbil will, nigger,” retorted Banjo. “You better cut out the preaching Jesus stuff and get you a broad foh going back home. And when you git back you take you’se’f to a hospital and get some shots, for if you ain’t got the sip I ain’t nevah seen none.”
“Wha’ you wanta drink?” Ginger asked.
“Not a damn thing to drink with our gang,” said Banjo. “Ain’t none of us gwine encourage Lonesome Blue to lay around heah and die. If we got any money, let’s give it tohm. But let him keep to himself until he’s so lonely that he’ll sure get right outa this Frenchman’s town. I don’t know what youse hanging around here foh. The consul ain’t evah gwine to do anything for you again, and the police will git a hold a you every-time them ten days am finished.”
“That’s the truth,” said Goosey. “I quit my ship and have never gone to any consul since. And I don’t intend to. When the consul put you on that ship, Lonesome, it would have been better you had gone. You made a big mistake.”
“A nigger is a bohn mistake,” declared Banjo, laughing. “When Gawd made the white man, he put a little stuff in his haid for him to correct his mistakes. And so when the white man invented writing and pencil, he put an eraser on that pencil to rub out mistakes. But Gawd nevah gived the nigger no brain-stuff foh’m to correct his mistakes, and so the nigger kaint invent anything to correct his mistake.
“For when Gawd was making the first nigger, a bluebird jest fly down into the Tree of Life and started singing that the wul’ was ready and waiting foh the love a Gawd. And the tune was so temptation that Gawd lost his haid and set down the golden bowl with the nigger’s brain in it. And the serpent was right there. And he ups and et the nigger’s brain and put a mess a froth in the golden bowl. And that stuff for the nigger’s brain gived the serpent the run of the earth.﻿ ﻿…
“And when Gawd done took up his work again, he took the froth in the bowl and dumped it into the nigger’s brain and finished his job. And that’s why you find the world as it is today. The debbil ruling hell and earth, the white man always getting by and there, and the nigger always full a froth or just dumb like this heah Lonesome Blue.”
All the boys had a rollicking laugh in which they were joined by Kid Irish, who had come in while Banjo was holding forth, accompanied by a fleshy young man, a Jew, who made a living as a guide and a seller of sex post cards to tourists. The Jew had arrived from Toulon, whence he had followed the American squadron, that had just put in at Marseilles.
“But, Lawd Gawd,” said Ginger, “imagine what we niggers woulda been today if Big Massa hadn’t a made a mistake. Why, if we am as we is from a mistake, what wouldn’t we be if we had been made right from the start? We woulda had Gawd and them angels in glory and all nuts.”
The boys roared out again and Goosey said: “That story you told was raw niggerism, Banjo, and you ought to be ashamed to tell that on the race before a white person.”
“Eh-eh-ehieeee!” Malty laughed. “Can’t the race stand a joke?”
Whereupon the Jew said he knew a better one than Banjo’s and volunteered to tell it if the boys didn’t mind.
“Sure. Speak up, kid,” said Banjo. “There ain’t no ladies here objecting, except Goosey. And ef he don’t like it he can take his box outa here.”
The Jew said: “I guess you all heared about *Shuffle Along*, the colored show that had such a long run on Broadway. I seen it about six times. Gee! there was some swell-looking colored goils in it. I used to see real money guys waiting just to get a glimpse of them coming out of the theater. Well, what I’m going to say is about two of the goils and I’m telling it straight as I heard it.
“One night two of the show girls was walking home together, and they saw a white man lying in the gutter. They didn’t know whether he was hurt or drunk, and they woulda liked to help him, but they were goils and colored (you all understand), so they couldn’t do anything for him.
“ ‘It’s really too bad to leave him be like that,’ says one of the goils, ‘but we’ve gotta, because some white person might see us helping him and think something bad of us. Let’s go on our way, dearie.’
“And as they walked off the other goil she says: ‘Too, too bad poor white man fallen so low. Seems to belong to some dickty family, too. Did you notice his clothes? And such a handsome profile.’
“ ‘Profile!’ says the other goil. ‘It wasn’t no profile you see, honey, but his flask a liquor in his pants.’ ”
The joke went over the heads of all the boys excepting Ray and Goosey. Ray smiled and remarked that most of the stock “Negro” jokes were of Jewish origin, but Goosey scowled and cursed under his breath.
“That makes me remember,” said Ray, “I read in a colored newspaper that one of those *Shuffle Along* girls was fired from the company for keeping a date with a white man.”
“Served the damn wench right,” said Goosey.
“Oh, I know a good one meself,” said Kid Irish.
“I think we’ve had enough of colored jokes,” said Goosey.
“Enough you’ grandmammy!” cried Banjo. “Quit you’ bellyaching blah and get along from here. A joke is a joke﻿—”
“Yes, but white people don’t make jokes like that about themselves,” maintained Goosey. “Especially the one-hundred-percent Yankees. You fellows don’t know anything about the race movement. Ray knows better, yet he holds in with you. You don’t know why the white man put all his dirty jokes on to the race. It’s because the white man is dirty in his heart and got to have dirt. But he covers it up in his race to show himself superior and put it on to us. The Yankees used to make jokes out of the Germans. Then when the Germans got strong enough to stop that, they got it out of the Irish and Jews. When the Irish and Jews got too rich and powerful in politics, they turn to Italian and Negroes.”
“That ain’t right on the Irish, me man,” said Kid Irish. “There’s barrels o’ Irish jokes going around.”
“If the Yankee can’t afford a joke and the Negro can, then the Negro is the bigger and richer man,” said Ray.
“That’s poetical,” replied Goosey. “The weak and comic side of race life can’t further race advancement.”
“You talk just like a nigger newspaper,” said Ray.
“Niggah from you, Ray!” exclaimed Goosey, “and with white folks among us! I expected that from Banjo or Malty, but not from you.”
“Yes, nigger,” repeated Ray. “I didn’t say ‘niggah’ the way you and the crackers say it, but ‘nigger’ with the gritty ‘r’ in it to express exactly what I feel about you and all coons like you. I know you think that a coon is a Negro like Banjo and Ginger, but you’re fooling yourself. They are real and you are the coon﻿—a stage thing, a made-up thing. I said nigger newspaper because a nigger newspaper is nothing more than a nigger newspaper. Something like you, half baked, half educated, full of false ideas about Negroes, because it can’t hold its head up out of its miserable purgatory. That’s why we﻿—you﻿—the race﻿—can’t get beyond the nigger newspaper in the printed word. That’s why an intelligent man reads it only for the comic﻿—the joke that it is. You talk about niggerism. Good Lord! You’re a perfect example of niggerism. Sometimes you get me so worked up with your niggery bull, I feel like giving you a poke in your stupid yaller jaw.”
Swept by a brainstorm, Ray was gesticulating in Goosey’s face.
“Get your monkey-chasing hand out of my face, black nig﻿—man,” cried Goosey, getting hot. “Because you’re a man without a country, you have no race feeling, no race pride. You can’t go back to Haiti. You feel there’s no place for you in Africa, after you’ve hung around here, trying to get down into the guts of the life of these Senegalese. You hate America and you despise Europe. You’re just a lost sorehead. You pretend you’d like to be a vagabond like Malty and Banjo here, but you know you’re a liar and the truth is not in you.”
“Aren’t you happy you’ve got a country and a flag to go back to?” asked Ray, now quieting down.
“When it comes to myself, I’m not studying those United Snakes,” retorted Goosey.
“Holy Gees!” cried Kid Irish. “Don’t start a riot among ye, or if youse going to, wait and let me deliver meself first.”
“Sure. Go on with it, bud,” said Ginger. “Ray, how come you make Goosey get you’ goat like thataway?”
Ray laughed, puzzled himself at his little flare-up.
Kid Irish said: “There’s four people in me story, so that makes it a square joke.
“There was two Irish friends from Galway. They were very poor and they went to America to make their fortune. The oldest friend was engaged to be married. When the two partners reached New York they soon got jobs and they lived together. The oldest one became a policeman and his friend got a job as a bartender. They had an apartment in San Juan Hill in a district where there were many niggers﻿—”
“Negroes,” corrected Goosey.
“Yes, Niggerows. Excuse me,” said Kid Irish. “The policeman started in to save to send for his girl. But after a year he didn’t have enough money. So his friend offered to help him and proposed they should all three housekeep together to save expenses when the girl came. She arrived and was married to the policeman, and the three of them took a flat in the same quarter. And of course in time the bride got in the family way.
“The husband was very happy and he and his friend began saving more than ever so that after the birth they would all go back to Ireland. But the strangest thing happened, some funny freak o’ nature, for when the baby was born it was black. The husband said he didn’t want the baby and he wasn’t going to stay in New York; he was going back home and he couldn’t take the bride with him. The friend said he would go back home, too. So they bought steamship tickets to go back to Ireland and left the bride and the strange infant. But the friend was awfully sad about the whole business, and when they were on the pier, waiting to board the ship, he broke down and cried as if his heart was going to break.
“And the husband said to him: ‘Cheer up! The way you carry on they would think it was you and not me that was married to her.’
“And his friend replied: ‘I just can’t help it, seeing how it took two Irishmen to make one nigger.’ ”
The boys roared as Kid Irish stopped. Goosey liked that joke and joined in the laughter.
Banjo got up, jigging round the café, chanting the popular melody: “It takes a long, tall brownskin.”
“What about going down to the docks, fellahs?” asked Ginger. “There’s a good broad in. I know the crew.”
“I’m game for anything,” said Malty.
“Let’s give Lonesome Blue some money, between us,” said Banjo, “for I just ain’t gwine to have this rear-end facer trailing us.”
They found five francs for Lonesome Blue, and as he was limping off from the café Ray called after him:
“Wait for me. I’m going with you.”
“Whereat?” demanded Banjo. “You’re going with us.”
“No, I’m going to police headquarters or to the American consulate with Lonesome. If they expel him, why don’t they send him home to America? This jailing of a man again every ten days after he is out seems the most abominable thing to me. And a man like Lonesome. Sick and nutty and not able to help himself. How can a civilized government do that? Is there no international law for deportees?”
“I tell you, pardner, as you best friend,” said Banjo, “if I was you I’d keep away from all them gov’ment people, whether theyse French or Americans. I ain’t nevah fooled round them. What you want to go and get mixed up with them for, all on account of this dumb Alabama darky﻿—”
“I ain’t from Alabam’﻿—”
“You look like you is, all the same,” Banjo said to Lonesome Blue.
“All the same, I am going to see what I can do,” insisted Ray. “We don’t want to see him die off like a dog around here, like that old man in Joliette.”
Ray went off with Lonesome Blue.
The old man in Joliette was a half-crippled, white-head fixture who came on crutches every morning to squat in the Place de la Joliette near the fountain where the coal workers stripped to the waist to wash themselves after work.
When the black beach boys bummed food they brought him some, pieces of bread and scraps of meat. And sometimes the coal workers gave him coppers. Banjo would get food for him and give it to one of the boys to take to the old man. But Banjo always steered wide of the spot where he sat. Banjo lived entirely on his strength and was scared of contacts with any Negro that had lost the one thing a vagabond black had to live by.
The old man was a used-up British seaman. Looking down on the square from a hilly street is the British Mediterranean Mission to Seamen, which operates under the patronage of His Britannic Majesty, the Archbishop of Canterbury, and other distinguished personages. Floating above it is a blue flag bearing a white angel flying to the aid of seamen. And nearly every day a cockeyed white servant of His Majesty’s mission passed by the disabled old black seaman in the square to visit the incoming ships and distribute tracts and mission cards to able-bodied sailors. One morning the old man could not come to the square, for he had died in his sleeping-hole on the breakwater.

===Chinese===

海滩上有一个南方黑人，班卓和他那伙小子见了他就腻烦，老是躲着。他从北非一个港口来到马赛，在那儿从一条外国船上结了工钱下来。刚到马赛那阵，跟"别拿它点火"故事里那个小子一样，他把海滩小子们看得一文不值，一个也不理；倒是让几个虫子似的科西嘉流氓（voyous）捡了去，那伙人把老巢安在沟区一家下水道坑一样的小旅馆兼酒馆里。钱一光，他就去了美国领事馆，人家安排他搭一条美国货船回国。可上船的日子到了，他死活不肯走。他从哪儿又弄了点钱，靠它过了几天。花完了，又回领事馆求助，管海员事务的那个职员再也不理他了。
后来他想混进海滩小子们中间，可他们不收。不光因为他起先瞧不起他们，还因为他是个死东西，身上没有一点流浪汉对生活的天赋火花——那正是海滩生活的灵魂。小子们管他叫"孤独布鲁斯"（Lonesome Blue）。
"孤独布鲁斯"听小子们念叨码头的好葡萄酒，动了心。一个晴好的下午，他跑下去，就在防波堤（the breakwater）上、警察眼皮子底下，砸开了一桶。他被抓了，蹲了三个月监狱，还领了一张驱逐令：出狱十天内滚出法国。
你要是有那个背运，被法国驱逐，总保安局才不操心你怎么个走法。命令只有一条：滚！路你自己找。因为这个，许多罪犯不过换个名字、换个活动的场子，好留在这个最迷人的欧洲国家。有些人干脆原地不动——要是那地方像马赛一样大得藏得下人——指望自己的机灵能逃脱警察的罗网。比如金杰，惹了麻烦，被判蹲了些日子，然后驱逐。那早是老黄历了。可他一出狱就把驱逐令毁了，到今天还在马赛享福。这事可不像听起来那么简单，因为警察一直在缉拿逃避驱逐的人，抓一个，他们有大约十法郎一头的赏钱。金杰叫突击大搜捕网进去过多少回，可他那点地道的口语法语和一副好脾气的机灵，回回都让他过了关。
可怜的"孤独布鲁斯"嘴笨，脑子也笨。打头一回坐牢起，他又因为不遵驱逐令蹲了两回，那些驱逐令他全当纪念品留给了警察。他刚又被放出来，看见班卓和小子们午饭后聚在那家非洲咖啡馆，就进去了。
"孤独布鲁斯"老兄又来啦，"班卓喊道，"老是趁你最想不到的时候冒出来，跟个鬼魂似的吓人。"
"你怎么不脱身呢，俺的孩子？"金杰说，"你既然在这法国人的城里把自己搞成这样，怎么不找个婆娘（broad）一起脱身？瞧瞧你这德行。"
"孤独布鲁斯"一身皱皱巴巴缠成一团的破烂，脚趾从一双蹩脚的普罗旺斯无产者拖鞋里戳出来，脸上一块块疮，永远挂着一副酸苦的表情，好像有个不依不饶的看不见的恶魔掐着他后脖颈。
"你是个病秧子黑鬼，"班卓说，"俺看你情况不妙，像有人给你办好了去墓园的护照。"
"不用你告诉俺。俺知道。"孤独布鲁斯答道，"你不说俺也知道。只有老天爷知道俺啥滋味。"他末了从肚子里哼出一声长叹收尾。
"老天爷才不会帮你个屁，黑鬼，魔鬼倒兴许会，"班卓顶回去，"你趁早收了那套传耶稣的调调，找个婆娘一起回老家去。回去以后赶紧上医院打几针，你要不是得了梅毒，俺就从来没见过梅毒。"
"你要喝点啥？"金杰问。
"跟俺们这帮人不喝他妈的任何东西，"班卓说，"俺们谁也不会攒掇孤独布鲁斯赖在这儿等死。俺们要是有钱，凑点给他。可让他自己待着，待到他孤单得受不了，准保滚出这法国人的城。俺不明白你在这儿耗着干啥。领事再也不会管你了，十天一到期，警察回回都要抓你。"
"这话在理，"古西说，"俺跳了船，从没上过什么领事馆，往后也不打算去。当初领事把你安顿到那条船上，孤独布鲁斯，你要是走了就好了。你犯了个大错。"
"黑鬼天生就是个错。"班卓宣布，笑起来，"老天爷造白人的时候，往他脑袋里搁了点材料，好让他改自己的错。所以白人发明了写字和铅笔，又在铅笔后头装了块橡皮，好擦掉错处。可老天爷没给黑鬼改错的脑子材料，所以黑鬼发明不出改错的东西。
"老天爷造头一个黑鬼那会儿，一只蓝鸟刚好飞进生命之树，唱起来说世界已经备好、正等着老天爷的爱。那调子太勾人，老天爷昏了头，把盛黑鬼脑子的金碗搁下了。蛇就在旁边。它爬过去，把黑鬼的脑子吃了，往金碗里吐了一堆泡沫。那点当脑子的材料，换来了蛇在地上横行。……
"等老天爷重新拿起活计，他捞起碗里的泡沫，倒进黑鬼的脑袋，把活干完了。所以你今天才见到这么个世界。魔鬼统治着地狱和人间，白人处处吃得开，黑鬼呢，一肚子泡沫，或者干脆哑巴，就像这位孤独布鲁斯。"
小子们哄堂大笑，跟着一起笑的还有"爱尔兰小子"，班卓高谈阔论那会儿他进来了，还带着一个肥头肥脑的年轻人，一个犹太人，靠给游客当向导、卖色情明信片过活。那犹太人从土伦（Toulon）过来，一路跟着美国舰队——舰队刚开进马赛。
"可是，老天爷啊，"金杰说，"想想要是大老爷当初没弄错，俺们这些黑鬼今天会是什么样。嘿，俺们要是从一个错处来的都成了这样，要是当初就造对了，俺们还了得？俺们准把老天爷和那一帮天使从天堂里挤兑下来，全归俺们。"
小子们又吼起一轮笑，古西说："班卓，你讲的这个是生黑人主义，当着白人讲种族这个，你该害臊。"
"呿-嘿-嘿-咦！"莫尔蒂笑，"种族连个笑话都受不起？"
于是那犹太人说，他知道一个比班卓的还好的，要是小子们不介意，他来讲。
"当然。讲吧，老弟。"班卓说，"这儿没有哪位太太反对，除了古西。他要不爱听，可以抱着他的琴滚出去。"
犹太人讲道："你们大概都听说过《一路欢跳》（Shuffle Along），百老汇连演了老久的那出黑人戏。我看了差不多六回。嚯！里面有几个黑姑娘真水灵。我常见真正有钱的大爷等在戏院门口，就为瞧她们出来一眼。好，我要讲的是其中两个姑娘的事，我怎么听来的就怎么讲。
"有天夜里，戏班两个姑娘一起回家，看见一个白人躺在阴沟里。她们不知道他是伤了还是喝醉了，本想帮他，可她们是姑娘，又是有色人（你们都懂的），所以什么也不能为他做。
"'就这么扔下他，实在太不像话，'其中一个姑娘说，'可咱们只能这样，因为要是叫白人瞧见咱们扶他，会往坏处想咱们。走吧，亲爱的。'
"她们走开的时候，另一个姑娘说：'太可惜了，太可惜了，可怜的白人落得这么惨。瞧着还是体面人家的呢。你注意到他的衣裳没有？多俊的侧影。'
"'侧影！'另一个姑娘说，'宝贝，你看见的可不是什么侧影，是他裤兜里的酒壶。'"
这个笑话除了雷和古西，小子们全都没听懂。雷笑了笑，说那些现成的"黑人"笑话大半出身犹太，可古西黑着脸，压着嗓子骂。
"这倒叫俺想起来，"雷说，"俺在一份黑人报纸上读到，《一路欢跳》一个姑娘因为跟一个白人约会，被戏班开除了。"
"那贱货活该。"古西说。
"哦，俺自己也知道一个好笑话。"爱尔兰小子说。
"黑人的笑话俺们听够了。"古西说。
"够你个头！"班卓喊，"少他妈哼哼唧唧，该干吗干吗去。笑话就是笑话——"
"是，可白人不拿自己开这种玩笑，"古西坚持，"尤其那些百分之百的北方佬。你们这伙人对种族运动一窍不通。雷懂得多些，可他偏跟你们混。你们不知道白人为什么把他那些脏笑话全堆到种族头上。因为白人心里脏，离不了脏。可他为了显得自己高人一等，把脏往自己种族脸上遮，全栽到俺们头上。北方佬从前拿德国人编笑话。等德国人硬得能叫他闭嘴了，他们就改挖爱尔兰人和犹太人。等爱尔兰人和犹太人在政治里有了钱有了势，他们又转向意大利人和黑人。"
"这话说爱尔兰人不对，老哥，"爱尔兰小子说，"爱尔兰笑话多得满桶满桶地淌。"
"要是北方佬开不起玩笑而黑人开得起，那黑人就是更伟大、更富有的人。"雷说。
"这是诗。"古西答，"种族生活软弱可笑的一面，推进不了种族的进步。"
"你说话跟一张黑人报纸一模一样。"雷说。
"从你嘴里说出'黑鬼'（Niggah），雷！"古西嚷起来，"还当着白人！班卓和莫尔蒂说这个俺不奇怪，你可不该。"
"对，黑鬼。"雷重复，"我说的不是你和那些穷白佬嘴里那种'黑鬼'，是带一个硬邦邦'儿'音的"黑鬼"，一字不差表达我对对你和你这路黑人的看法。我知道你把"黑人"想成班卓和金杰那样的黑人，可你骗的是你自己。他们是真黑人，你才是那个"舞台黑人"——一个戏台上的东西、一个捏造出来的东西。我说黑人报纸，因为黑人报纸就不过是张黑人报纸。跟你一个样，半生不熟，一知半解，满脑子关于黑人的错误观念，因为它那颗脑袋从它那悲惨的炼狱里抬不起来。这就是为什么咱们——你们——这个种族——在印刷出来的字里头，怎么也超不过黑人报纸。这就是为什么明白人只拿它当笑柄读——它本来就是笑话。你张口闭口黑人主义。老天爷！你本人就是黑人主义的完美标本。有时你那套黑鬼调调把我拱得火起，我真想朝你那张愚蠢的黄下巴来一拳。"
雷一阵头脑风暴刮过来，正对着古西的脸比划。
"把你那只撵猴子的手从俺脸上拿开，黑鬼——先生！"古西喊，火起来了，"就因为你是个没有祖国的人，你没有种族感情，没有种族骄傲。你回不了海地。你又觉得非洲没有你的位置，你在这儿混了一圈，一头扎进这些塞内加尔人的日子肚肠里。你恨美国，又瞧不起欧洲。你就是个失意的酸茄子。你装着想当莫尔蒂和班卓这样的流浪汉，可你自己知道你在撒谎，你心里没有一句实话。"
"有你这么个可以回去的祖国和一面国旗，不是很幸福吗？"雷问，这会儿静下来了。
"说到俺自己，俺才不惦记那众蛇合众国。"古西回敬。
"我的老天！"爱尔兰小子喊，"你们可别先内讧起来，真要闹，也等等，先让俺把俺的笑话交出来。"
"行。讲吧，伙计。"金杰说。"雷，你怎么让古西这么容易就把你的火点着了？"
雷笑了，自己也不明白这股无名火打哪儿来。
爱尔兰小子讲道："俺的故事里有四个人，四四方方一个笑话。
"有两个戈尔韦来的爱尔兰好朋友。他们很穷，就上美国去发财。年长的那位订了亲。两个伙伴到了纽约，很快找着了活儿，住在一块儿。年长的当了警察，他那朋友当了酒吧伙计。他们在圣胡安山租了套房子，那一片黑人多——"
"是'黑人'。"古西纠正。
"对，黑-人。失敬失敬。"爱尔兰小子说。"警察开始攒钱，好把未婚妻接来。可一年下来钱不够。他朋友就提出帮衬，等姑娘来了，三个人搭伙过日子，省钱。姑娘到了，跟警察结了婚，三人在同一片街区租了套公寓。不用说，新娘过了些日子就有了身子。
"丈夫高兴得很，跟他朋友比先前更省，指望孩子生下来后一块回爱尔兰。可最怪的事发生了，大自然开了个天大的玩笑——生下来的婴儿是黑的。丈夫说他不要这个孩子，也不在纽约待了，他要回老家，新娘他也带不回去。朋友说他也一起回去。于是两人买了回爱尔兰的船票，把新娘和那个怪婴儿撇下了。可朋友对这整桩事伤心得不得了，站在码头上等着上船的时候，他哭得心都要碎了。
"丈夫对他说：'高兴点儿！你这么哭天抹泪的，人家还当跟她结婚的是你不是我呢。'
"他朋友答：'我就是忍不住，你想想，得两个爱尔兰人才生得出一个黑人。'"
爱尔兰小子一收尾，小子们哄堂大笑。古西也喜欢这个笑话，跟着一起笑。
班卓站起来，在咖啡馆里跳着快步，哼着那支流行的调子："得一个高高个儿的棕皮肤。"
"下码头去怎么样，哥儿们？"金杰问，"进了一条好船，上面有货真价实的婆娘。我认得那船员。"
"我啥都奉陪。"莫尔蒂说。
"咱们大伙凑点钱给孤独布鲁斯，"班卓说，"我实在不想让这个扫尾的累赞跟着俺们。"
他们凑了五法郎给孤独布鲁斯。他一瘸一拐刚要出咖啡馆，雷追着喊：
"等等我。我跟你一起走。"
"去哪儿？"班卓质问，"你跟俺们一块儿去。"
"不，我要陪孤独布鲁斯去警察总局或者美国领事馆。他们要驱逐他，为什么不把他送回美国？这个人一出狱每十天就抓他一回，我看这是顶顶卑鄙的事。何况是孤独布鲁斯这样的人，病着，糊涂着，自己顾不了自己。一个文明的政府怎么能这样？对被驱逐的人就没有个国际法吗？"
"我跟你说，搭档，作为你顶好的朋友，"班卓说，"我要是你，就离那些官家的人远远的，法国的美国的一样。我从没跟他们搅和过。你干吗为这么个哑巴亚拉巴马黑奴去沾一身腥——"
"俺不是亚拉巴马来的——"
"可你长得像。"班卓对孤独布鲁斯说。
"不管怎么说，我要去看看能做点什么。"雷坚持，"咱们不能眼看着他像条狗一样死在这一带，像若利耶特那个老头儿一样。"
雷跟孤独布鲁斯走了。
若利耶特那个老头儿是个半瘫的、白头发的老家伙，每天早上拄着拐来，蹲坐在若利耶特广场喷泉边上——装煤工人下了工就在那儿脱光上身洗澡。
黑海滩小子们讨到吃的，就给他捎点，几块面包、几片碎肉。有时装煤工人也给他几个铜子。班卓常给他弄吃的，交给哪个小子给他送去。可班卓自己总绕开老头儿坐的那块地方远远的。班卓全靠自己的力气活着，见了任何一个丢掉流浪黑人立身之本的，他都怕沾。
老头儿是个耗干了的英国水手。从一条坡街往下俯瞰广场的，是不列颠地中海海员布道会，在大英皇帝陛下、坎特伯雷大主教和其他显贵名流的庇护下运营。它上头飘着一面蓝旗，旗上一个白人天使，飞着去救海员。几乎每一天，皇帝陛下布道会的一个斜眼白人仆役都会从广场上那个残废的黑人老水手身边走过，登上进港的船，把布道册子和教会卡片分发给身强力壮的水手们。一天早上，老头儿没能到广场来——他死在防波堤上他睡觉的洞里了。

## XV White Terror / 白色恐怖
===Original===

Simultaneously with the American squadron, an American freighter and two large English ships, one from South Africa and another from India, had arrived in port. There were also a number of British tramps at anchor for some days. There was much changing of dollars and pounds and ten-shilling notes in agencies and cafés by sailors, officers, and tourists. The guides were as busy as could be showing the new arrivals about. For the chauffeurs of the docks it was a picnic day. All the night places were excited with anticipation of new guests. The *boîtes de nuit* had sent delegations of cocottes down to the docks to greet the newcomers with cards of invitation.
Some of these cards were decorated souvenirs, and, like many of the cabarets, bore the flags of the great shipping nations and advertised British ale and whisky and American cocktails.﻿ ﻿…
It was twilight and Ray was hurrying along the Rue de la République toward Joliette where he was to meet Banjo, as he had promised. Besides its usual peripatetic exhibition of youths in proletarian blue, cocottes, Arabs, Senegalese, soldiers, and sailors with red pommels on their caps, the street parade included groups of British seamen and white-capped sailors from the destroyers.
In the Place Sadi Carnot, Ray was accosted by a staggering seaman with a card in his hand.
“Is this the﻿—the﻿—Bru﻿—Bru﻿—Bru﻿—Brutish-Amurican Bar?” he asked in a drunken stutter, punching with his finger a card that he held.
Ray looked at it. An advertisement of the British and American Bar with its delightful symbolic trademark﻿—a Union Jack and Stars and Stripes united upon a Tricolor. It also bore a plan of Marseilles, with a long red line like a serpent indicating the route from the quays to the establishment in the Place de la Bourse.
“No,” said Ray, “this is not the British-American Bar, but you keep straight on until you reach the end of this street. Then you are at the Vieux Port and anybody will show you the Bar.”
“Thank yer, mite,” and the seaman staggered onward, repeating, “Bru﻿—Bru﻿—Bru﻿—Bru﻿—Brutish-Amurican Bar.﻿ ﻿…”
On the same street, where the Boulevard des Dames crosses it, Ray had another rencontre, this time a surprising one﻿—three American Negro seamen from an American freighter, one of whom was a waiter he had known on the railroad.
Ray’s old friend insisted that he should turn back with them. They went to the Senegalese Bar. Banjo was there, having returned from Joliette by the short way. Ray introduced the seamen to him, the *patron*, and a handsome Senegalese boxer.
The acquaintance between Ray and the railroad waiter, now turned ship’s steward, was slight. They had never worked on the same dining-cars, but had met each other casually at the railroad men’s quarters in Philadelphia. Yet they met now and acted like old and dear friends. Meeting like that was so unique, it stirred them strangely.
The seamen stood drinks. They said they would like to go to some place where they could amuse themselves. Banjo suggested a place in the Ditch, but they wanted something of the better sort. All three of them were well dressed. The boxer thought the British-American Bar would be all right. So the whole party decided to go. Banjo was in such an exciting, merrymaking mood that he won the admiration of the boys and was the target of most of the questioning. The atmosphere of the Senegalese Bar had won them immediately. It was run by a Negro and catered to colored men and they agreed that it was the best they had seen in any foreign port. When the proprietor talked English to them they felt proud that he had emigrated to America and made enough money there to return to France and start a business.﻿ ﻿… And Banjo! So gay and dressy on his hand-to-mouth existence without ever worrying about anything. That was marvelous!
“You find it all right over here, eh?” one of the newcomers asked him. “The froggies treat you better than the hoojahs, eh?”
“Well now, that’s a question I wouldn’t know how to answer noneatall,” said Banjo, “for it all depends on which way you take it. There ain’t no Canaan stuff sweeter than this heah wine and honey flowing in this place, but otherwise speaking, the Frenchies them have the same nose like a Jew, and ef you don’t smell a money they can’t use you.”
“Hi! now you’re saying that thing,” Ray laughed.
“All the same, you’ve got more freedom here,” said the seaman; “when you have money you can go any place you got a mind to.”
“Sure can,” said Banjo. “Theah’s moh freedom, all right, if you know how to handle it. But some a them niggers come here, boh, am as funny and dumb jes’ like that thing. They get in every way except the right way. They ketch the wrong end of the stuff. They ketch the pohliceman’s billy, they ketch the jailhouse, and what not ketch? Oh, Lawdy! ask not me!”
“Maybe the good liquor makes them crazy after boozing so long on moonshine corn,” said a seaman.
“And mos’n a them don’t even know how to use it right,” said Banjo. “They come here wanting whisky and gin, and when I tell them to drink French wine, that’s the best stuff to feel good on, they say it’s sour dago red. Can you beat that?”
“Don’t be too hard on them, Banjo,” said Ray. “They got to learn.”
“Learn!” sneered Banjo. “Them kind a babies nevah learn anything. A real traveling guy has got a preambulating nose for the bestest thing in any country whenever it is accommodated to him, but there’s many people running round the world that nevah shoulda been outa them own home town.”
For certain reasons, arrived at from a wide knowledge of the eccentricities of civilization and experience personal and impersonal, Ray felt no eagerness to transfer the party to the British-American Bar on such an evening. He was really rather reluctant, but because he preferred not to deaden in any way the keen anticipation of the evening’s pleasure for his comrades, he said nothing.
The atmosphere of the cabaret, when the boys got there, was heavily charged with contrary foreign influences and they were greeted by an extraordinary salvo of shrill female laughter as they entered. The Senegalese was irritated and said he did not like the atmosphere and the reception. Ray told him he did not think it was mocking laughter. Ray was never on the lookout for hostile hints; his mind was too rich of sane, full living for that. But there was no obtuseness there to prevent him from making immediate note of any such tendency. He had often remarked that white people were never more contemptibly vulgar than when a Negro entered a white place of amusement. If it were not a hostile exhibition of bad manners, as in America, it would be an imbecile theatrical demonstration, as often happened in Europe. It was as if the black visitor could not be seen in any other light but that of a funny actor on the stage.
He had never known black people to act like that when white persons entered a Negro place of pleasure. On such occasions Negroes could assume a simple dignity as remote from white behavior as primitive African sculpture is from the conventionalism of a civilized drawing-room. He had never remarked a vulgar gesture. Primitive peoples could be crude and coarse, but never vulgar. Vulgarity was altogether a scab of civilization.
The boys squeezed together round a table and had some drinks. The Senegalese was right. None of the girls wanted to dance with them. It was purely a matter of good business. Ray understood and he was glad to get away from the place. Cockney was not a musical accent to his ears, nor was there any aesthetic pleasure in the sight of those white caps on hard-boiled, over-shaved heads. But it hurt him that these black boys, coming off the ship after a long hard trip should tumble into this.
Not far from the British-American Bar was another. The head waiter was a boxing enthusiast and was friendly with Ray and the Senegalese. Ray told the boys to wait for him in the square while he went to the bar to talk to the head waiter. It was a more expensive bar than the British-American.
The head waiter was at the bar when Ray entered. He was glad to see Ray and offered him a drink, but he wasn’t pleased to hear what Ray wanted for his comrades. He wished it was any other time, for the *boîte de nuit* was full of American and British officers spending plenty of money, drinking champagne. He was sure there would be trouble. There had been before when there were colored men in the bar and English and American customers﻿—especially Americans. Once that bar had been ordered closed for six months because of a colored-white incident. He was for the boys, all right, for he was one of them himself, but if they did come in there might be a fight and it would spoil the boss’s business.
Business! Prejudice and business. In Europe, Asia, Australia, Africa, America, those were the two united terrors confronting the colored man. He was the butt of the white man’s indecent public prejudices. Prejudices insensate and petty, bloody, vicious, vile, brutal, raffiné, hypocritical, Christian. Prejudices. Prejudices like the stock market﻿—curtailed, diminishing, increasing, changing chameleon-like, according to place and time, like the color of the white man’s soul, controlled by the exigencies of the white man’s business.
Back in the square with his comrades, Ray said he knew of one other place where he had been a few times with the two gentlemen bums, but he felt sure it would be no different from the rest on a night like that.
“Damn the white man’s bars!” he said. “Let’s go back to the Ditch.”
“When I enlisted in the army during the war,” said Banjo, “mah best buddy said I was a fool nigger. He said the white man would nevah ketch him toting his gun unless it was to rid the wul’ of all the crackers, and I done told him back that the hullabaloo was to make the wul’ safe foh democracy and there wouldn’t be no crackers when the war was ‘ovah and ended,’ as was done said by President Wilson, as crackers didn’t belong in democracy. But mah buddy said to me I had a screw loose, for President Wilson wasn’t moh’n a cracker. He was bohn one and he was gwineta live and die one, and that a cracker and a Democrat was one and the same thing. And mah buddy was sure right. For according to my eyesight, and Ise one sure-seeing nigger, the wul’ safe foh democracy is a wul’ safe foh crackerism.”
“I was just waiting for one of those Americans to make a move against us,” said the Senegalese boxer. “I would like to murder one of them. I have my gun.”
“No good that,” said Ray, who, although he was always ready to defend himself in the jungles of civilization, was dead set against stupid violence.
“It’s just about two years,” said the Senegalese, “that some Americans caused a black prince to be thrown out of a cabaret in Montmartre, and Poincaré made a declaration against it. He said Americans cannot treat Negroes in France the way they do in America.”
“That won’t prevent discrimination, though,” said Ray, “so long as the pound is lord and the dollar is king and the white man exalts business above humanity. ‘Business first by all and any means!’ That is the slogan of the white man’s world. In New York we have laws against discrimination. Yet there are barriers of discrimination everywhere against colored people. Sometimes a Negro wins a few dollars in a test case in the courts. But no decent Negroes want to go to court for that. We don’t want to eat in a restaurant nor go to a teashop, a cabaret, or a theater where they do not want us, because we eat and amuse ourselves for the pleasure of the thing. And when white people show that they do not want to entertain us in places that they own, why, we just stay away﻿—all of us who are decent-minded﻿—for we are a fun-loving race and there is no pleasure in forcing ourselves where we are not wanted.
“That’s why the amusement side of the life of the Negro in America is such a highly-developed thing. And in spite of the deep differences between colored and white, it is the most intensely happy group life of Negroes in any part of the civilized world.”
“You’re right,” said one of the visitors. “I have been in many a poht, all right, and I’ve spread joy some, but when it comes to having a right-down good time, there ain’t any a them that’s got anything on Harlem. Well, whar we going?”
“It’s rotten luck,” said Banjo, “for you-all to hit this town when it is lousy with crackers. It ain’t always like this. But the Ditch is all right, though. Everything is down theah. And I nevah crave to leave it for any other show.”
The boys had shuffled off along up the Canebière, talking.
“Sure the Ditch is all right,” said Ray. “I was just thinking how we fellows traveling around like this learn a whole lot a things. A sailor ought to be the most tolerant person in the world, he has seen so much. And I think he is in his rough way, from all I’ve seen of sailors knocking around port towns. Except the white American sailor. He sees everything, but he learns nothing. And I don’t think he’s capable of learning. He carries abroad with him everything that should be left back home. Everything that is mean, hard-boiled, and intolerant in American life.
“Well, if we can’t learn anything from the traveling representatives of American culture, we might learn from other people. I’ll tell you something about these sailors. A few months ago I was visiting Toulon, when this same squadron arrived. Now on the Boulevard de Strasbourg at Toulon there is a tavern where the young officers always dance. Many of the better class of cocottes go there. The common French sailor is not allowed in there. But when the American sailors came they were given the run of the tavern. Why? Because they had plenty of dollars to spend﻿—the pay of an American sailor turned into francs is probably as much as the pay of a French lieutenant. I’m not sure.
“Some hundreds of low cocottes came to Toulon for the American sailors. And they all flocked with them to the tavern. I was interested to know what the young French officers would do. Of course, they couldn’t stand the changed atmosphere of the place. And they just stopped going there! There was a little exclusive dancing-place in an out-of-the-way street, and I saw a few of them dancing there with their girls.
“After all, they were officers with a right to kick. But they didn’t. They just separated themselves from the canaille. They knew what a little extra good business meant to a French *commerçant*. You know in America, with our high wages and the dollarized standard of living, we have no idea of money value and economy in the ordinary European sense. But that is something else. All I want to say is that I learned something helpful from that incident at Toulon. Something that made me sure of myself and stronger in my own worth.”
“I get you,” one of the seamen said.
“You do?” asked Ray. “There are different ways of growing big and strong, for individuals as well as for races.”
When the boys reached the Place de la Bourse again they were suddenly surrounded by a troop of painted youths who, holding hands, danced around them with queer gestures and queerer screams, like fairy folk in fables.
“Here they are!” laughed Ray. “If there’s a British-American bar over there, there’s none here.”
“A regular turnout foh the deep-sea stiffs,” commented Banjo. “Ain’t nothing missing in this burg.”
“They ought to give them yellow cards, too,” said the Senegalese.
“*Pourquoi?*” asked Ray.
“*Pour la santé publique, comme les filles. Et voyez, ils sont toujours en concurrence avec eux. Ce n’est pas juste.*”
Ray laughed. “Justice, like equality, *mon vieux*, does not exist in the mathematics of life. It’s a man’s world, you might say a white man’s world and﻿ ﻿… ‘a man’s a man for a that.’ ”
Two sailors, arm in arm, their white caps set far back on their heads, came out of the British-American Bar and moved in a slow drunken roll across the square, chanting, as if they were rooting for a team: “*We are Americans.﻿ ﻿… We are Americans.﻿ ﻿…*”
The African Bar was jammed full when the boys got back there. Smoke hung in gray chunks in the hot, strong-smelling air. Under it the player-piano was spitting out a “Charleston” recently arrived in Marseilles, while Martinique, Madagascans, and Senegalese soldiers, dockers, *maquereaux*﻿—and, breaking the thick dark mass in spots, a white soldier or docker﻿—were jazzing with one another and with the girls of the Ditch.
A Senegalese, squeezed up against the bar, with his wrist in a sling, called to Banjo as the boys pushed themselves in:
“Hey! you see American sailor?”
“I seen plenty a them, but I don’t pay them no mind,” said Banjo. “Wha’s matter with you’ hand?”
The Senegalese related to the boys how a gang of American sailors had rushed a bistro-dancing in Joliette, where colored and white of the quarter were amusing themselves, and tried to break it up. He had got a sprained wrist, but for revenge he had landed a sailor a butt that skinned his forehead and clean knocked him out.
“How did it finish up?” Ray asked.
“The police came just when the *patron* got out his revolver.”
“Oh, Lawd!” Banjo began in a Negro prayer-meeting tone. “It’s a hellova life and all Gawd’s chilluns am creatures of the debbil, but, oh, Lawd, lawdy, don’t let a cracker cross mah crossings in this Frenchman’s town.”
The boys pushed into the dance.

===Chinese===

跟美国舰队一起进港的，还有一条美国货船和两条英国大船，一条从南非来，一条从印度来。还有几条英国不定期货船下锚待了好几天。水手、军官和游客们在职业介绍所和咖啡馆里大把地换美元、英镑和十先令的票子。向导们忙得脚不沾地，领着新来的人四处转。对码头的汽车夫们，这是个野餐日。所有夜场子都因为盼着新客而亢奋。各夜总会（boîtes de nuit）派出了野鸡代表团下码头，拿着请柬卡迎新客。
有些卡是精装的纪念品，跟许多卡巴莱一样，印着各大航运国的国旗，广告上写着英国麦芽酒、威士忌和美国鸡尾酒。……
正是暮色，雷顺着共和国街往若利耶特赶，他答应了在那儿跟班卓碰头。这条街照例有走马灯似的展览：穿工装蓝的小伙子、野鸡、阿拉伯人、塞内加尔人、士兵，还有帽子上缀红绒球的水手；此外还添了一伙伙的英国水手和驱逐舰上戴白帽的水手。
在萨迪·卡尔诺广场，一个打晃的水手手里捏着张卡凑上来招呼雷。
"这是不是那——那——英——英——英——英美酒吧？"他醉得结结巴巴地问，手指头戳着他手里那张卡。
雷看了看。一张英美酒吧的广告，商标讨喜而有寓意——一面米字旗和一面星条旗并在一起，架在三色旗上。上面还印着一张马赛地图，一条蛇似的长红线标出从码头到交易所广场那家店的路线。
"不是，"雷说，"这儿不是英美酒吧，不过你一直往前走，走到这条街的头上，就到旧港了，随便谁都能指给你那家酒吧。"
"谢了，小伙儿。"水手打着晃往前挪，一路念叨，"英——英——英——英美酒吧。……"
同一条街上，在贵妇大道交叉口那儿，雷又有一次偶遇，这回是意外之喜——三个美国货船上的美国黑人水手，其中一个是他在铁路上认识的餐车侍者。
雷的老朋友非要他折回去跟他们一起走。他们进了塞内加尔酒吧。班卓在那儿，他抄近路从若利耶特回来了。雷把水手们介绍给他、介绍给老板，还有一位英俊的塞内加尔拳击手。
雷跟这位如今改行当了乘务员的铁路餐车侍者，交情其实很浅。他们从没在同一列餐车上干过活，只是偶然在费城的铁路职工宿舍碰过面。可这会儿见了面，两人却像多年至交。这样的相逢太难得，把两人都搅得心里怪怪的。
水手们做东请酒。他们说想去个能痛快玩的地方。班卓提议沟区一家，可他们想要体面些的。三人穿得都不赖。拳击手觉得英美酒吧不错。于是全伙决定去。班卓那股子兴高采烈、寻欢作乐的劲头，赢得了小伙子们的喜欢，成了被问东问西的中心。塞内加尔酒吧的气氛一下子就抓住了他们。那是一个黑人开的店，做有色人的生意，他们公认这是他们在任何外国港口见过的最好的店。老板跟他们说英语，他们很为他骄傲——他移民去了美国，在那边挣够了钱，回法国开了买卖。……还有班卓！吃了上顿没下顿，却这么快活、这么讲究，什么都不操心。真是了不起！
"你觉得这儿都不错，嗯？"一个新来的问他，"青蛙佬待你比老家那帮人强，嗯？"
"哎，这个问题俺可不知怎么答才好，"班卓说，"全看你从哪头说。没有哪块迦南的蜜奶比这店里的酒还甜，可话又说回来，法国人长着跟犹太人一样的鼻子，你要是让他们闻不出钱味，你就没用。"
"嘿，你这话说到点子上了。"雷笑。
"可你在这儿到底自由多些，"水手说，"有钱的话，想去哪儿去哪儿。"
"那可不，"班卓说，"自由是多，只要你使得来。可有些黑鬼跑这儿来，天哪，又可笑又哑巴，就是这么回事。哪条道都走，就是不走正道。什么都抓错的这一头。抓警察的警棍，抓班房，还有什么抓不着？哦，老天爷！可别问俺！"
"兴许是好酒把他们灌疯了，私酿玉米酒喝了那么久。"一个水手说。
"他们大多数人还不知道怎么正经喝呢，"班卓说，"跑来就要威士忌要杜松子，俺跟他们说喝法国酒，那才是喝着最舒坦的好东西，他们说那是酸哒哒的达戈红汤。你说绝不绝？"
"别把话说太狠，班卓，"雷说，"他们总得学嘛。"
"学！"班卓嗤了一声，"那号宝贝什么都学不会。真跑码头的人长着一只四处溜达的鼻子，哪国的好东西给他方便，他一闻就着。可世上多少人满世界跑，其实压根儿就不该出他们老家镇子。"
出于某些原因——那是他对文明的种种乖僻的广博见识、亲历的和听来的经验教给他的——雷并不急着在这么一个晚上把这一伙人领进英美酒吧。他实在有点不情愿，可他不愿扫了伙伴们对今夜乐子的热盼，也就什么都没说。
小伙子们到的时候，卡巴莱里的空气沉甸甸地充着各种互相顶牛的外国气，他们一进门，就挨了一阵刺耳的女人大笑的齐射。塞内加尔人火了，说他不喜欢这空气、这迎接。雷跟他说，他觉得那不是嘲笑。雷从不竖着耳朵捕捉敌意；他的头脑里装满了健全充实的生活，容不下那个。可他也不迟钝，这种苗头一冒头他就记下了。他常说，一个黑人走进白人的娱乐场所时，白人从没有比那时更卑鄙粗俗的。要不是像美国那样当场来一场恶意的坏礼数，就是像欧洲常有的那样，来一场白痴式的戏台表演。仿佛黑皮肤的客人除了被当成台上的滑稽戏子，就没法被别的眼光看见。
他从没见过黑人当白人走进黑人的乐子场时来这一套。那种场合，黑人自有一副朴素的尊严，离白人的做派之远，就像原始非洲雕刻之离文明客厅的俗套。他从没见过一个粗俗的动作。原始人可以粗野、可以莽，可从不俗气。俗气整个儿是文明的一块疮疤。
小伙子们挤在一张桌子边要了酒。塞内加尔人说得对。没有一个姑娘肯跟他们跳。这纯粹是笔好买卖的事。雷心里明白，巴不得离开这地方。伦敦土腔在他耳朵里不是什么音乐，那些煮硬了的、刮得铁青的脑袋顶上的白帽，也毫无美感可言。可这几个黑小子，跑了一趟又长又苦的船下来，一跤跌进这么个地方，让他心疼。
离英美酒吧不远还有一家。领班是个拳击迷，跟雷和塞内加尔人都熟。雷叫小伙子们在广场上等他，自己去那家酒吧找领班。那是一家比英美酒吧更贵的店。
雷进去时领班正在吧台。他见了雷很高兴，请他喝一杯，可听到雷替伙伴们提的要求，面有难色。他但愿是别的什么日子，因为这夜总会里满是挥金如土、喝着香槟的英美军官。他断定要出事。从前有色人进店碰上英美客人——尤其是美国人——就出过事。有一回就因为一场有色对白人的冲突，这家店被勒令停业六个月。他是向着小伙子们的，这没错，他自己就是他们一路人，可他们要真进来，兴许就要动拳头，那老板的买卖就砸了。
买卖！偏见和买卖。在欧洲、亚洲、澳大利亚、非洲、美洲，这就是有色人面前的那两尊合体的恐怖。他是白人不体面的公开偏见的笑柄。那些偏见无理性、琐碎，血腥、狠毒、下流、残暴、精雅、虚伪、基督教。偏见。偏见像股市——削减、缩水、加码、变脸，跟变色龙似的，随时随地，像白人灵魂的颜色，全看白人买卖的行情支使。
回到广场伙伴们中间，雷说他还知道一家，跟那两位绅士游民去过几回，可他拿得准，这么一个晚上，那家跟别家不会有两样。
"去他妈的白人酒吧！"他说，"回沟区去。"
"打仗那会儿俺参军，"班卓说，"俺顶好的哥们儿说俺是傻瓜黑鬼。他说白人想叫他扛枪，除非是把全天下的穷白佬都除了名。俺回他说，这场大呼隆是为了给民主造个安全的世界，等仗"打完收场"——威尔逊总统说的——就再没有穷白佬了，因为民主里头搁不下穷白佬。可俺哥们儿对俺说，俺脑瓜里有颗螺丝松了，威尔逊总统自己就是个穷白佬。生下来是，活着是，到死还是，穷白佬跟民主党佬就是一码事。俺哥们儿说得真对。因为照俺的眼力——俺可是个眼尖的黑鬼——民主的安全世界，就是穷白佬主义的安全世界。"
"我正等着那帮美国人里哪一个冲咱们动手呢，"塞内加尔拳击手说，"我真想宰他们一个。我的枪就带着。"
"那可不行。"雷说。他虽说在文明的丛林里随时准备自卫，却死命反对愚蠢的暴力。
"也就两年前，"塞内加尔人说，"几个美国人闹得一位黑人亲王被蒙马特一家卡巴莱轰了出去，庞加莱还为此发了个声明。他说美国人不能在法国像在美国那样对待黑人。"
"那也挡不住歧视，"雷说，"只要英镑是老爷、美元是王，只要白人把买卖抬到人上头。'买卖第一，不择手段！'这就是白人世界的口号。纽约有反歧视的法律。可歧视的关卡到处都拦着有色人。有时一个黑人打官司能赢几个钱。可正派的黑人谁也不愿意为这个上法庭。我们不想在人家不要我们的饭馆吃饭，不想去人家不要我们的茶室、卡巴莱、戏院，因为我们吃饭、取乐图的是那个乐子。白人摆明了在他们开的场子里不想招待我们，那好，我们就干脆不去——咱们所有心里有数的人——因为我们是个爱乐子的种族，硬挤进人家不要咱的地方，还有什么乐子。
"所以美国黑人的生活里，玩乐这一面才发达成这么一门高深的玩意儿。而且别看有色和白人之间隔阂这么深，那是文明世界任何角落的黑人里最火热快活的群体生活。"
"你说得对，"一个来客说，"俺跑过的港口可多了，乐子也撒过一些，可要说到痛痛快快玩一场，哪儿也比不上哈莱姆（Harlem）。好了，咱们去哪儿？"
"真倒霉，"班卓说，"你们偏偏这镇上塞满穷白佬的时候来。平时不这样。不过沟区不赖。什么都在那儿下头。俺从来不为别的戏码想离开它。"
小伙子们顺着卡内比耶尔（Canebière）往坡上站着走，一路聊。
"沟区当然不赖，"雷说，"俺是在想，咱们这样满世界跑的人，学到的东西可真不少。水手该是世上最宽容的人，他见过的太多了。照俺看，他也是的——凭俺见的那些跑码头的水手——用他那粗拉拉的方式。美国白人水手除外。他什么都看见，什么都没学会。俺看他也没本事学。他把本该留在国内的东西全带出国来。美国生活里一切刻薄、煮硬了、容不得人的东西。
"唉，美国文化的这些流动代表既然教不了咱们什么，咱们兴许能跟别人学。俺给你们讲讲这帮水手的事。几个月前俺在土伦（Toulon），赶上的正是这支舰队。土伦的斯特拉斯堡大道上有一家酒馆，年轻军官们总在那儿跳舞。上等的野鸡也多上那儿。普通法国水手不许进。可美国水手一来，那家酒馆随他们跑。为什么？因为他们有的是美元好花——美国水手的饷钱换成法郎，兴许抵得上一个法国中尉的。俺也说不准。
"几百个下等野鸡为美国水手涌到土伦，全跟着他们挤进了那家酒馆。俺挺有兴趣，想看看年轻的法国军官们怎么办。当然，他们受不了店里的空气变得那么个样子。他们干脆就不去了！另有一条偏街上有家小小的会员舞场，俺看见他们几个带着自己的姑娘在那儿跳。
"说到底，人家是军官，有资格闹一场。可人家不闹。人家只是把自己跟那帮贱民隔开。他们懂得一笔小小的好买卖对一个法国生意人（commerçant）意味着什么。你们知道，在美国，凭着高工资和美元化的生活水平，我们对欧洲通常意义上的货币价值和节俭毫无概念。不过那是另一码事。俺想说的只是，土伦那件事教了俺一样东西。一样让俺拿稳了自己、让自己的分量更硬的东西。"
"俺懂你。"一个水手说。
"是吗？"雷问，"长大变壮的路数不止一条，个人如此，种族也如此。"
小伙子们回到交易所广场，突然被一伙涂脂抹粉的小伙子围住了。他们手拉着手，围着他们转圈跳，手势怪里怪气，叫声更怪，像寓言里的仙童。
"他们来啦！"雷笑道，"那头有英美酒吧，这头就没有。"
"专为远洋死鱼们摆的仪仗。"班卓评论道，"这城里什么都不缺。"
"也该给他们发黄卡。"塞内加尔人说。
"为什么？（Pourquoi?）"雷问。
"为了公共卫生（pour la santé publique），跟姑娘们一样。您瞧，他们还老跟姑娘们抢生意呢。这不公平。"
雷笑了。"公道，跟平等一样，老兄（mon vieux），在生活的数学里是不存在的。这是个男人的世界，你还可以说是个白人的世界，而且……"不管这一切，人总是人。"
两个水手胳膊挽着胳膊，白帽子远远扣在后脑勺上，从英美酒吧里出来，带着醉意慢慢地打着滚横过广场，嘴里喊着号子，像给哪个球队加油："俺们是美国人。……俺们是美国人。……"
小伙子们回到非洲酒吧时，里面挤得满满当当。烟一团团灰蒙蒙地悬在又热又冲的空气里。烟底下，自动钢琴正噼里啪啦吐着一支刚传到马赛的"查尔斯顿"，马提尼克人、马达加斯加人、塞内加尔士兵、码头工人、皮条客（maquereaux）——还有几个白人士兵或码头工，把那厚厚的一团黑压破了几处——正彼此跟沟区的姑娘们跳爵士。
一个塞内加尔人挤在吧台边上，手腕吊着三角巾，见小伙子们挤进来，冲班卓喊：
"嘿！见着美国水手没有？"
"见了一嘟噜，俺才不把他们放眼里，"班卓说，"你手咋啦？"
那塞内加尔人对小伙子们讲了事情经过：一伙美国水手冲进若利耶特一家有色白人同乐的舞馆，想砸场子。他手腕给拧伤了，可为了报仇，他一头撞过去，磕破了一个水手的额头，把他撞得不省人事。
"后来怎么了？"雷问。
"老板掣出手枪那阵儿，警察正好到了。"
