对雷来说，黑人是整个世界人类生活的一个意味深长、咄咄逼人的方面。有一派黑人知识分子，给这个"问题"添上了他们最好的贡献：让种族蒙着面纱出场，外加一声声假仁假义的"塞拉"。再没有比这更可笑的呈现了。照他的经验，戴面纱的大家伙正是白人，用面纱罩住自己的生活，也罩住别族的生活。黑人太爱阳光敞亮的活法，不肯躲在什么面纱后头。要说黑人非得有个定义，那就有十足的理由把他定义为西方文明的一个挑战，而不是一个"问题"。
两人正说着，一个学生熟人加入了他们这一桌。这新来的对雷一向怀着友好的敬意。他去过巴黎，听过黑人爵士乐手，他喜欢那些爵士乐师和约瑟芬·贝克——他在疯狂牧女游乐场看过她——于是也想喜欢雷。一见雷手里的《人道报》，他登时炸了毛，把一份《法兰西行动报》砰地拍在雷面前的桌上。
雷头一回注意到这学生翻领上那枚鸢尾花徽章。
"你读这个干什么？"他质问，"这不是法国的！你干嘛不读份法国报纸？"
"比如？《人道报》就是法文印的。"
"可它照样不是法国的。"
"我猜你是想替我挑法国读物。你要我读《法兰西行动报》？"
"我没那么说，可你至少可以读一份真正法国的报纸，比如《小巴黎人报》或《新闻报》。"
"我恨《新闻报》，"雷说，"它顶好的是每日专栏小说，可那一套——女人拿小白脸骗丈夫或资产阶级情夫，人人都抿嘴窃笑——我早腻了。莫泊桑之后，这就没什么意思了。"
"那你读《法兰西行动报》也比读《人道报》强。你是文人，主编多代是我们活着的最伟大的文人。他写起法国作家来，活着的死了的，笔头最漂亮最俏皮。你读《法兰西行动报》，就跟法国文学的头等货色接上了气。"
"也许吧，"雷说，"《法兰西行动报》我是真读——偶尔。可一想到你们那位多代拿他十五岁儿子的自杀或他杀做政治宣传，我就受不了这份报。这一条就够把你们的《法兰西行动报》变成一件脏东西。你知道，盎格鲁-撒克逊国家虽然那么虚伪，在英国或美国，哪个主笔、哪个政客敢这么干、还干得成？兴许因为盎格鲁-撒克逊的公众比法国公众笨一点、滥情一点。反正拿这么个病态题目跟他们玩党派政治，玩不动。"
"你这么想是因为你不懂法国政治，"学生说，"那男孩的被杀是一桩政治行为。警察杀了他。你不了解法国警察。"
"我了解，"雷说，"我看他们是全世界最烂的。"
"别这么说我们的警察，"学生说，"不中听。你为什么这么说？"
克罗斯比笑了。雷说："因为我就这么觉得的。"
"我看你不领法国文明的好处，"学生火了，"我们对有色人格外宽容。我们待他们比盎格鲁-撒克逊各国好，因为我们是世界上最高度文明的国家。"
"你用的这套话，跟一个百分之百的美国人会跟我用的一个样，不过是字眼和重音有点差别，"雷答道，"我跟你说吧，在我这儿，没有什么'文明国家'这种畜生。我相信每个国家多少都有几个正派的头脑，可我不会把他们全推到所多玛和蛾摩拉那道测验跟前试出来。还是信了好！你说你们国家对有色人比盎格鲁-撒克逊人宽容，这话对。可照我看到的这座城对黑人和阿拉伯人的态度，我不知道你们欧洲人要是在欧洲也管着一大片有色人口，会是个什么光景。我求老天保佑别让你们摊上。你们欧洲人在非洲的纪录可是漂亮得很，我想你们个个都骄傲。就差美国也插一手了。我还巴不得她插。别看她对黑人的传统态度，她在非洲也许能像在中国那样，叫欧洲难堪。"
学生猛地离了桌。雷把他惹火了，雷倒觉得痛快。这人肚子里塞满了那套大吹大擂的法国文明的好处——尤其对有色人的好处。他那些熟人，从工人到学生，个个学舌这一套，虽然他们自己的态度里恰恰没有那套的真精神。野鸡对这一点敏感得出奇，报纸上满纸都是，还有几个聪明作家咬定：巴黎是非洲裔美国人（Aframerican）的天堂。
雷往喧闹底下更深的地方找真相，他真找到的，是对黑人一种根本的轻蔑，跟盎格鲁-撒克逊地界一样露骨。对黑人的通行看法，同文明世界的通行看法没有两样。要说有区别，倒是一种不加掩饰的居高临下，对一个想活得不受"被人可怜、被人恩养"的败坏影响的黑人，那就是苦胆。在这儿，同在任何地方一样（那位警察督察已经用他的高论点得明明白白），一个黑罪犯等于全体黑罪犯，一个黑掎客等于全体黑掎客，一个黑鬼等于全体黑鬼，一个失败的黑人等于全体失败的黑人。
例外是不算数的。要不是雷有那条"例外高于平均"的原则，光凭白人世界对黑人的态度，他早把它整个儿看成一摊可鄙的东西了。白人的头脑总体上恰恰从相反的角度接近黑人世界。他常琢磨：没有那条原则撑着，他那种智识生活还可能不可能。
假设他单凭报纸的社会新闻栏去对法国或随便哪个文明做反应。去年夏天有那么发疯的一个月，他读到的净是发疯的罪案：小两口为一点可怜的遗产拿斧子劈了祖父母；母亲把孩子们按在水里淹死；父亲在女儿初领圣体那天占了她；凶手朝大街扫射；还有全部那些肮脏的"情杀激情罪"——其实全是"钱罪"。
对一个冷眼旁观这出戏的黑人来说，抓住这些东西、把它们当作这个文明本性的证据而幸灾乐祸，本可以很容易——只要由着它们扭曲他、抢走他那套比较价值的原始感觉，抢走他那双透过表象看人生千奇百怪的深变奏的直觉。
可生活是用来品尝的，不是用来控诉的，品尝比控诉奇妙得多。控诉留给文明正义的那群小道德虫子吧。他们为白色的受害者磨快了小匕首，运气好逮着一个黑的，就把他抡成大棒，砸向整个黑人种族。
有一场宰杀让雷格外开了眼：一个发疯的塞内加尔士兵犯下一桩可怕的罪，整个黑种族就被押到了舆论的被告席上。授权刊登的是一家激进派报纸，撑的是激进派政府——正是在那个政府的治下，西非黑人正被从故土里连根拔出，从家人身边硬扯走，装船运到欧洲，来领教战争的艺术和梅毒的病。这一份文明逻辑的启示太逗了，雷把它留了下来，尤其因为他其实默认那论点，只是恶心它的讲法：

===Original===

> “Un tirailleur sénégalais, pris d’on ne sait quel vertigo, a fait, à Toulon, un affreux carnage.
> “On s’évertue maintenant à savoir par quelle suite de circonstances ce noir a pu fracturer un coffre et s’emparer des cartouches avec lesquelles il a accompli le massacre.
> “Qu’on le sache, soit. Mais la question me semble ailleurs. Il faudrait peut-être se mettre la main sur le cœur et se demander s’il est bien prudent d’apprendre à des primitifs à se servir d’un fusil.
> “Je n’ignore pas qu’il y a de belles exceptions; qu’il y a des ‘nègres’ députés, avocats, professeurs et que l’un d’eux a même obtenu le prix Goncourt. Mais la majorité de ces ‘indigènes’ à peau noire sont de grands enfants auxquels les subtilités de notre morale échappent autant que les subtilités de notre langue. La plus dangereuse de ces subtilités est celle-ci:
> “Tu tueras des êtres humains en certaines circonstances que nous appelons guerre.
> “Mais tu seras châtié si tu tues en dehors de ces circonstances.
> “Le Sénégalais Yssima appartient à une catégorie humaine où il est d’usage, paraît-il, quand on doit mourir de ne pas mourir seul. Le point d’honneur consiste à en ‘expédier’ le plus possible avant d’être soi-même expédié.
> “Si cela est vrai, on voit où peuvent conduire certaines blagues de chambrées. Pour tout dire franchement, il n’est pas prudent de faire des soldats avec des hommes dont l’âme contient encore des replis inexplorés et pour qui notre civilisation est un vin trop fort. Sous les bananiers originels, Yssima était sans doute un brave noir, en parfaite harmonie avec la morale de sa race et les lois de la nature. Transplanté, déraciné, il est devenue un fou sanguinaire.
> “Je ne veux tirer de cet horrible fait divers aucune conclusion. Je dis que de semblables aventures (qui ne sont d’ailleurs pas isolées) devraient nous faire réfléchir sérieusement﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

> "一名塞内加尔狙击兵，不知中了什么邪，在土伦（Toulon）制造了一场可怕的大屠杀。
> "如今人们正使劲查究，这个黑人究竟是经过怎样一连串的机缘，才撬开了钱柜、夺下了他用来进行屠杀的子弹。
> "查清楚也好。可在我看来，问题在别处。也许该把手按在心口上问问自己：教原始人使枪，到底稳当不稳当。
> "我并非不知道有漂亮的例外；有当议员、律师、教授的"黑鬼"，其中一位甚至还得了龚古尔奖。可这些黑皮肤的"土著"，大多数都是大孩子，我们道德的精微，同我们语言的精微一样，他们一概摸不着。这些精微里最危险的一条是：
> "你将在某些我们称为战争的场合杀人。
> "可你要是在这些场合之外杀了人，就要受罚。
> "塞内加尔人伊西马属于这样一类人：据说在他们那里，风俗是要死也不独自死。荣誉的关键在于，在自己被送走之前，尽量多"送走"几个。
> "倘若真是这样，人们就看得出营房里某些玩笑能引到什么地步去了。直说了吧：拿一些灵魂里还有未曾勘探的皱褶、我们的文明对他们来说是烈酒的人去造士兵，是不稳当的。在老家的香蕉树下，伊西马没准是个好黑人，跟他的种族的道德和自然的法则和谐得很。一经移植、拔了根，他就成了一头嗜血的疯子。
> "我不愿从这个可怕的社会新闻里引出任何结论。我只是说，这类事件（况且并非孤立）应当让我们认真想一想……"

===Original===

Suddenly and strangely Ray felt a hard hatred for Crosby that seemed inexplicable, and yet was not. He had fallen into a mood in which the whole white world of civilization appeared like an obscene phenomenon. And Crosby sitting there by him was a freak because he was not indecent. He was too fine a type, something too real for Ray’s frame of mind. His presence became unbearable.
“I am going back to the Ditch,” Ray said. He frowned at Crosby and left him without any word of explanation.
He went to his hotel and got his bag and returned to the Ditch. It was moving out of the Ditch that caused the policeman to take me for a criminal nosing round the quarter of respectability, thought Ray. Better had I stayed down here with Banjo and the boys where the white bastards thought I ought to be. They always searched me like a criminal down there, but they never beat me up. I moved away from there and got myself messed up. It was all through Crosby persuading me to go respectable. Whenever I get mixed up with nice people I always catch it. Better to know nice people, if I must know them, in books, and me for living my own vagabond life. Maybe I would have felt better if the knuckle-dusting frogs had beaten me up by mistake down here. He felt that somebody ought to be blamed, ought to be hated, for what had happened to him, and he worked himself up against Crosby.

===Chinese===

突然，奇怪地，雷对克罗斯比涌起一股硬邦邦的恨，看着没来由，其实不然。他陷进了这样一种心绪：整个白人的文明世界活像一件淫秽的物事。坐在他身边的克罗斯比成了一个怪物——就因为他不淫猬。他太正点了，对雷此刻的心境来说太"真"了。他这个人待在那儿，叫人受不了。
"我回沟区去。"雷说。他冲克罗斯比皱起眉，一句解释也没有，把他撇下了。
他回旅馆取了提包，回到沟区。是搬出沟区这件事，才叫警察把我当成了在体面街区探路的罪犯，雷想。当初跟班卓和小子们待在下头还好些，那些白畜生本来就觉得我该待在那儿。他们在下头也回回像搜罪犯那样搜我，可从没揍过我。我一挪窝，就把自己揽进了浑水。全怪克罗斯比劝我学体面。我一回跟体面人揽在一起就倒楣。体面人，非要认识不可的话，还是在书里认识的好，我呢，过我自己的流浪日子。兴许那些拿拳节掸尘的青蛙佬当初在下头误把我揍一顿，我心里反倒痛快些。他觉得，他遭的这一场总得有个谁挨骂、有个谁可恨才对，于是他把这股火全做在了克罗斯比身上。

## XXIII Shake That Thing Again / 二十三 再来一遍《Shake That Thing》
===Original===

Ray returned to the Ditch, and at the African Bar Banjo was treating Malty, Ginger, Dengel, and some West African boys. Banjo had received notice from the consulate to prepare to leave in a day or two. Ray was boisterously welcomed. Girls and their touts were dancing to the continuous racket from the pianola. Banjo suggested that the gang should go to his old hangout, where he would play and they could kick up their own racket.
The long back room in the rear of the bistro was the boys’ for spreading joy. Banjo revived “Shake That Thing” for the party. Malty joined him blowing a little horn or whistling, while the boys kept up a humming monotone of accompaniment as they danced.
Front and rear the bistro was jammed﻿—girls and touts and beach boys. The girls helped themselves liberally to the boys’ wine on the tables. Dengel, who rarely danced, was dogging it with a boy from Grand Bassam. A vivacious girl pointed at them and cried: “Look at that Dengel dancing. I thought he didn’t do anything but booze.”
She cut in between them and, her feminine curiosity rising over her passion for gain, she ignored the boy from Grand Bassam, who was new to the Ditch and supposed to have money, and taking hold of Dengel, said “Dance with me.” Tall and very slim, Dengel looked like a fine tree fern. He bent over to the girl in that manner of swaying inebriation peculiar to him, and executed an African jig so wildly that space had to be cleared for them. Surprised at Dengel’s rough wildness, the girl laughed and shrieked and wiggled excitedly.
When Banjo stopped playing, she rushed up to him and asked for the same thing again. Just at that moment a tout entered and whispered something to the Jellyroll *patronne* of the bistro, who held up her hand and called: “Listen! If any of you have guns or any other weapons, give them to me, for there’s going to be a *rafle* tonight.”
The touts handed over their guns and knives to her. Of the colored men, only a mulatto, a Martiniquan, had a revolver, which he gave to the woman. She put the weapons in a drawer of the counter and locked it. A boy who was a stranger to the quarter asked her; “You always know when the police are going to operate down here?”
“Sure. That’s understood,” she said. She was near the entrance, and stepping out into the narrow alley she said, with a raucous laugh, “That for the police.”
She reentered the bistro heaving with laughter and, patting one of the Senegalese who was standing white-eyed by the door, said: “*Tu as vu le clair de lune?*”
Hearing that the police were coming, Ray felt that he could not stand being handled by them again just then. He might do something crazy and get into serious trouble. So he quietly slipped off. Just as he reached the corner the police entered the bistro. He had to cut across Boody Lane to reach the Bum Square, and as he was passing he saw a policeman coming out of one of the holes-in-the-wall and finger-wiping his long mustache as if he had just finished the most appetizing hors d’oeuvre in the world. Maybe.
In the Bum Square he met Latnah. Her manner was strangely preoccupied. Ray asked her if she knew the boys were celebrating in the Ditch. She knew, but did not care to go.
“I think you’re blue like me,” said Ray. “Maybe what we need to fix us up is a pipe dream.”
“You do that, too?” Latnah asked.
“I do anything that is good for a change. All depends on the place and the time and the second person singular?”
“Then I have stuff,” Latnah said. “We go.”
They went up to her little place. She spread the colored coverlet on the floor and threw down two little cushions for pillows. She brought out a basket of oranges and dates. And they sat down together on the rug. A little brass plate, lamp, tube and, the iodine-like paste strangely fascinating in its somnolent thickness. Latnah prepared for the ritual.
“Take fruit. It good with fruit,” she said.
“I know that,” Ray replied.
“You know all about it,” she smiled subtly. “I think is leetle Oriental in you.”
“Maybe. There’s a saying in my family about some of our people coming from East Africa. They were reddish, with glossy curly hair. But you have the same types in West Africa, too. You remember the two fellows that used to be at the African Bar during the summer? They looked like twins and they were heavy-featured like some Armenians.”
“I think they were *mulattres*,” said Latnah.
“No, they weren’t mixed﻿—not as we know it between black and white today. Perhaps way back. I heard they were Fulahs.”
“We all mixed up. I’m so mixed I don’t know what I am myself.”
“You don’t? I always wonder, Latnah, what you really are. Except for the Chinese, I don’t feel any physical sympathy for Orientals, you know. I always feel cold and strange and far away from them. But you are different. I feel so close to you.”
“My mother was Negresse,” said Latnah. “Sudanese or Abyssinian﻿—I no certain. I was born at Aden. My father I no know what he was nor who he was.”
Latnah picked an orange clean of its white covering and handed a half of it to Ray. He put his tube down and slipped a lobe into his mouth. The incense of the rite rose and filled the little chamber, drifting on its atmosphere like a magic canopy. Drowsily Ray remembered Limehouse and those days of repose in the quiet dens there.
Latnah must have captured his thoughts psychically, for she suddenly said, “It no never haunt you?”
“No. I remember it as one of the strange and pleasant things in my life, just as another person might recall any interesting event. But when I quit I just put it out of my mind﻿—forgot it and started in living differently.”
“You beaucoup Oriental,” said Latnah. “Banjo never touch anything strange like us. *Il est un pur sauvage du sang.*” She sighed.
Ray locked her to him in his elbow. Peace and forgetfulness in the bosom of a brown woman. Warm brown body and restless dark body like a black root growing down in the soft brown earth. Deep dark passion of bodies close to the earth understanding each other. Dark brown bodies of the earth, earthy. Dark﻿ ﻿… brown﻿ ﻿… rich colors of the nourishing earth. The pinks bring trouble and tumult and riot into dark lives. Leave them alone in their vanity and tigerish ambitions to fret and fume in their own hell, for terrible is their world that creates disasters and catastrophes from simple natural incidents.
A little resting from the body’s aching and the mind’s trouble in sweet dreaming. Ray’s hankering was for scenes of tropical shores sifted through hectic years. Salty-warm blue bays where black boys dive down deep into the deep waters, where the ships shear in on foamy waves and black youths row out to them in canoes and black pilots bring them in to anchor. Coconut palms like sentinels on the sandy shore. Black draymen coming from the hilltops, singing loudly﻿—rakish chants, whipping up the mules bearing loads of brown sugar and of green bunches of bananas, trailing along the winding chalky ways down to the port.
Oh, the tropical heat of earth and body glowing in the same rhythm of nature﻿ ﻿… sun-hot warmth wilting the blood-bright hibiscus, drawing the rich creaminess out of the lush bellflowers, burning green fields and pasture lands to crispy autumn color, and driving the brown doves and pea doves to cover cooing under the fan-broad cooling woodland leaves.
But he dreamed instead of Harlem﻿ ﻿… the fascinating forms of Harlem. The thick, sweaty, syrup-sweet jazzing of Sheba Palace﻿ ﻿… Black eyes darting out of curious mauve frames to arrest the alert prowler﻿ ﻿… little brown legs hurrying along﻿ ﻿… with undulating hips and voluptuous caressing motion of feminine folds.

===Chinese===

雷回到沟里，非洲酒吧里班卓正做东请马尔蒂、金杰、丹格尔和几个西非小子。班卓接到领事馆的通知，要准备一两天内动身。雷受到一片闹腾腾的欢迎。姑娘们和她们的皮条客合着自动钢琴不停歇的聒噪跳舞。班卓提议大伙儿挪去他的老窝，那儿他可以拉琴，弟兄们也能闹出自家的动静来。
酒馆后头那间长条后屋归小子们撒欢。班卓为这一场复活了《Shake That Thing》。马尔蒂吹小号或吹口哨加入进去，小子们一边跳一边嗡嗡哼着单调的伴奏。
酒馆前前后后挤得满满当当——姑娘、皮条客、滩上小子。姑娘们放开了手，把小子们桌上的酒随意往自己杯里倒。难得跳舞的丹格尔，正跟一个大巴萨姆（Grand Bassam）来的小子跳狗步。一个泼辣姑娘指着他们喊：“瞧丹格尔跳的。俺还当他除了灌酒什么都不会呢。”
她插进两人中间，女人的好奇心压过了逐利的贪心——那个大巴萨姆小子初来沟里、想必有钱，她也不管了——抓住丹格尔说：“跟俺跳。”丹格尔又高又细，像一株秀气的树蕨。他朝姑娘俯下身去，带着他独有的那种摇摇晃晃的醉态，跳起一支非洲吉格，野得四下里只好给他们腾地方。丹格尔这一手粗野的狂放叫姑娘又惊又喜，笑着尖叫，兴奋地扭摆。
班卓一停琴，她就冲上去点同一支再来一遍。正在这时，一个皮条客进门，凑到那位“杰利罗尔”的老板娘耳边低语了几句。老板娘举起手喊道：“听着！你们谁身上带枪带家伙的，都交给我，今晚上有大搜捕（rafle）。”
皮条客们把枪和刀交到她手里。有色人里头，只有一个马提尼克的混血儿携着左轮，也交给了她。她把家伙什收进柜台的抽屉，上了锁。一个外地来的小子问她：“警察要在这一带动手，你们回回都事先知道？”
“当然。这是规矩。”她说。她正在门口，跨出去站到窄巷里，哑着嗓门笑了一声：“这一记，给警察。”
她笑得浑身直颤，回到酒馆里，拍了拍一个瞪白眼守在门边的塞内加尔人，说：“Tu as vu le clair de lune？（看见月光了？）”
听说警察要来，雷觉得自己这会儿实在经不起再让他们上手搜身了。他兴许会干出什么疯事，惹出大乱子。于是他悄悄溜了。他刚走到街角，警察就进了酒馆。他要抄近路穿布迪巷去游民广场，走过时看见一个警察正从墙洞的窝里出来，手指抹着两撇长髭，那样子像刚吃罢全世界最开胃的头道菜。兴许是。
在游民广场他碰见拉特娜。她的神情出奇地恍惚。雷问她知不知道小子们正在沟里闹腾。她知道，可不想去。
“我看你跟我一样蔫蔫的，”雷说，“兴许咱们缺的是同一剂药——一管子烟梦。”
“你也来这个？”拉特娜问。
“凡能换换心境的事我都来。就看地点、时候，还有第二人称单数是谁了？”
“那俺有货。”拉特娜说，“走。”
他们上她那小屋去。她把花被子铺在地板上，扔下两个小垫子当枕头。她端出一篮橘子和椰枣。两人一同在地毯上坐下。一只小铜盘、灯、烟枪，还有那碘酒似的膏子，昏稠昏稠的，怪迷人。拉特娜张罗起这套仪式。
“吃果子。配着果子好。”她说。
“这我知道。”雷答。
“你什么都懂。”她笑得微妙，“我看你身上有点儿小小的东方。”
“兴许。我们家有个说法，说我们有些人打东非来。他们皮肤发红，卷发油亮。西非也有同样的人种。你记得夏天常在非洲酒吧的那两个家伙吗？长得像双胞胎，眉眼粗重，像些亚美尼亚人。”
“我看他们是混血儿（mulâtres）。”拉特娜说。
“不，他们不是混的——不是今天咱们说的黑白那种混。兴许是老早老早以前。我听说他们是富拉人。”
“咱们全混在一起了。俺混得连自己是什么自己都不知道。”
“不知道？拉特娜，我还老纳闷你到底是什么人。跟你说，东方人里头，除了中国人，我的身子骨对他们没有一点亲近感。我总觉得冷、生分、隔得远。可你不一样。我觉得离你这么近。”
“俺娘是个黑女人（Négresse），”拉特娜说，“苏丹人还是阿比西尼亚人——俺拿不准。俺生在亚丁。俺爹是什么人、是谁，俺一概不知。”
拉特娜把一只橘子剥净了白络，掰一半递给雷。他放下烟枪，把一瓣送进嘴里。仪式的香烟升起来，注满小屋，像一顶魔帐浮在这屋子的空气上。雷昏昏沉沉想起莱姆豪斯（Limehouse），想起在那儿安静的烟窟里歇息的日子。
拉特娜像是魂灵里逮着了他的念头，忽然说：“那事从来不缠你？”
“不缠。我记得它，只当我生命里一件又怪又美的事，就像别人回想什么有意思的经历一样。可我一戒，就把它整个撒出脑子——忘掉，换一种活法从头来。”
“你真东方（beaucoup Oriental），”拉特娜说，“班卓从不沾咱们这种怪东西。Il est un pur sauvage du sang（他是个血里纯粹的野人）。”她叹了口气。
雷用臂弯把她锁进怀里。棕色女人的胸怀里的安宁与忘却。温热的棕色身体和不安分的黑色身体，像一条黑色的根扎进松软的棕色泥土里往下长。贴着大地的两具身体，深黑的激情彼此懂得。大地般的深棕色身体，土一样的身体。黑……棕……滋养大地的浓艳颜色。粉皮们给黑暗的生命带来纷乱、喧嚣和暴乱。随他们揣着虚荣和虎狼野心，在他们自己的地狱里煎熬咆哮去吧——他们那个世界实在可怕，简简单单的自然事，也要炮制成灾祸和浩劫。
从身体的酸痛和心头的烦恼里小歇片刻，泡进甜梦里。雷馋的是热带海岸的光景，隔着滚烫的岁月筛下来的光景。咸暖的蓝色海湾，黑小子们一头扎进深水，船剪开白沫的浪涌进来，黑皮肤的青年划着独木舟迎上去，黑皮肤的引水员领它们抛锚停泊。椰子树像哨兵立在沙滩上。黑人赶车夫从山顶下来，放声唱着——浪荡的调子，鞭着驮红糖和青香蕉串的骡子，沿着蜿蜒的白垩土路逶迤下到港口。
哦，大地和身体的热，在同一样的自然节律里发光……太阳的热力晒蔫血一样红的木槿，把丰腴的乳脂从肥硕的吊钟花里蒸出来，把绿田和牧地烤成焦脆的秋色，赶得棕斑鸠和灰斑鸠咕咕地躲进扇面一样宽大的荫凉的林叶底下。
可他梦见的偏偏是哈莱姆……哈莱姆那些勾魂的形体。示巴宫里又浓又汗、糖浆般甜的爵士……黑眼睛从奇特的紫红框子里射出来，逮住那警觉的夜游神……棕色的小腿急急赶路……荡着胯，一身女人的褶皱揉出肉感的、爱抚的浪。

## XXIV The Chauffeur’s Lot / 二十四 司机朋友的命途
===Original===

At noon the next day Ray went out, treading on air. His nature was buoyant. He went to a little Italian restaurant and fed. In the early afternoon he joined Goosey and Banjo on a café terrace.
His chauffeur friend, passing by, hailed him. He said he was going out to look at his suburban place and asked if Ray would like to go. Ray said he wouldn’t mind going, but that he was with Banjo and Goosey. The chauffeur said that he would take them all if they wanted to go. They did, but Goosey objected that the ship might arrive and the consul call for them.
“That won’t make no difference,” said Banjo. “No ship ain’t nevah gwine out the same day it put in at this heah hallelujah poht.”
“Yes, the Dollar Line boats do, too,” said Goosey.
“Well if it does the skipper has got to wait on us, his bum passengers, believe me,” said Banjo. “Come on, pardner, let’s go.”
Ray rode beside the chauffeur. The suburban route was melancholy. Before he went to the vintage he had gone out to another country place, and it had been refreshing then all along the way to see trailing bramble roses in the ripened green grass and marigolds and irises blooming in truck gardens of cauliflowers, and bunches of tempting grapes hanging from the fences. Now there was nothing but dead and rotting leaves everywhere and some withered blackberries.
The chauffeur’s place was like any of the common suburban lots owned by the great army of the lower middle class of modern cities. A cottage of three rooms and a kitchen, a young chestnut tree near the gate and a large fig tree in the rear.
Of the lots on either side of him, the chauffeur told the boys that one belonged to a bistro-keeper and the other to a policeman. Ray thought his neighbors were just right and told him so. The chauffeur smiled. He was proud of his neighbors, too. The other lots were worth a little more than his, he said, for they had their water supply and he hadn’t yet.
Banjo asked him how much he had paid for his place.
“Eleven thousand francs,” the chauffeur replied, and that he was still making periodical payments on it.
“Sometimes we have evening parties up here,” he told Ray.
“What kind?” Ray asked.
“If I pick up a tourist and a girl who want to take a joyride I bring them out here. Anything to help pay for the place. One night I had a gang, and it would have been fun to have you. But you’re so changed now from when I knew you at Toulon﻿—always with the Senegalese﻿—I didn’t trouble.”
“That’s all right,” said Ray. “Change is my passion. Can’t stay in one rut forever. Got to pull out and find something new.”
Goosey had detached himself from them and was in the act of digging under the fig tree.
“What you doing there, you?” Banjo called.
They went up and found Goosey filling a little squat glass jar with earth.
“What you doing with that theah dirt?” asked Banjo.
“To keep as a souvenir,” said Goosey.
“Oh, my Gawd, what a nigger, though!” Banjo horse-laughed. “It’s you should be making pohms and not Ray, for youse the sweetest cherry-diver I evah did see.”
Goosey held his jar of earth against his heart.
“It’s the soil of France,” he said. “I couldn’t make it, all right. I was outa luck, but I can always remember that I was here.”
“Kain’t you remember without that theah flask a dirt?” asked Banjo. “Better you tote back a flask a real Martell cognac on you’ hip, for that you won’t find back home in Gawd’s own, but you’ll find plenty a dirt. Dirt is the same dirt all ovah the wul’ cep’n’ for a li’l’ difference in color, maybe.”
“Leave him alone,” said Ray.
“You don’t understand this thing, Banjo,” said Goosey, angrily, “so it’s better you shut up.”
The chauffeur had been listening with sentimental interest and now he said to Goosey: “*Vous avez raison. La France, c’est le plus joli coin du monde.*”
“How do you know that?” said Ray. “You have never been out of it.”
“Yes I have,” responded the chauffeur. “I’ve been to North Africa and Spain and Italy and Constantinople. You forget I was a sailor.”
“Le’s quit arguing about dirt and find the first café where we can have wine,” said Banjo.
Later in the afternoon they all returned to the Vieux Port. After a parting drink with the boys the chauffeur drove off. Ray looked after him contemplatively and thought how differently he felt toward him now in comparison with the days when he knew him at Toulon. The chauffeur’s life at Toulon had been about the same kind of animal-cunning existence that it was at present.
He had once recounted to Ray how he had been arrested in a raid, when the police took from him a miniature ledger in which he kept a check-and-balance account of all the extra change he made, the places and persons (when he knew their names) that contributed to it.
The affair had been very amusing for Ray, just as it had been amusing for him to give the chauffeur all the tips and hints and cues he knew that he could follow up to gain something. In his picturesque uniform, old and overworked symbol of a free and reckless way of living, the chauffeur’s ways of eking out his means of enjoying life did not seem at that time unbeautiful.
Living the same life now with more freedom, he appeared loathsome to Ray. Perhaps it was what he was living for that made the difference. For as to how he was living﻿ ﻿… there were many luxury-clean people who had become high and mighty by traffic in human flesh. As a Negro Ray was particularly sensible to that fact﻿—that many of the titled and ennobled and fashionable and snobbish gentry of this age have the roots of their fortunes in the buying and selling of black bodies. And had he any reason to doubt that the landlords of Boody Lane and the Ditch as a whole were collecting that prostitute rent to live respectably and educate their children in decency?
What made the chauffeur so unbearably ugly to him now was that he was trafficking obscenely to scramble out of the proletarian world into that solid respectable life, whence he could look down on the Ditch and all such places with the mean, evil, and cynical eyes of a respectable person.
“Just imagine that chauffeur paying eleven thousand francs foh that place!” said Banjo to Ray. “Only eleven thousand! I coulda bought it with what money I landed here with and have something left ovah.”
“You could, all right, and yet you couldn’t,” said Ray. “You and I were not made for that careful touting life. Did you ever meet, back home, a black p.i. that was saving up off his women to marry respectable? Or a brown sob-sister chippy whining that she was doing that to support an old mother? You bet you and I never did meet any of the black breed like that. They were all true-blue sports in the blood.
“That chauffeur will marry with a clear conscience from his scavenger money. He may chuck up the chauffeur job and buy a café﻿—become a respectable *père de famille*﻿—a good taxpayer and supporter of a strong national government, with a firm colonial policy, while you and I will always be the same lost black vagabonds, because we don’t know what this civilization is all about. But my friend the chauffeur knows. It took over a thousand years of lily-white culture to make him what he is. And although he has no intelligence, he has the instinct of civilization, Banjo, and you and I just haven’t got it.”
“I can’t make out nothing, pardner, about that instinking thing that youse talking about. But I know one thing and that is if I ain’t got the stink of life in me, I got the juice.”
Passing through the Place Sadi Carnot, the boys saw Sister Geter being conducted down a side street by a policeman.
“I wonder if they’ve arrested her?” said Ray. “Such a long time since I’ve seen her, I thought she’d gone home.” He was hesitating about going to see what was up, but Banjo said, “Let’s find out, anyway, what theyse doing with her, pardner.”
“She might start that ‘Black Bottom’ stunt on us again,” objected Goosey, “and then﻿—good night!”
“Oh, come on. She kain’t make no moh ‘Black Bottom’ than her nacheral,” said Banjo.
The boys caught up with Sister Geter and the policeman and Ray spoke to her. The policeman asked Ray if he knew her and what she was doing raising such a racket in the streets. He couldn’t understand her, for she did not speak French. Ray told him that she was an evangelist. The policeman let her go and said he had only walked her away so that the big crowd that was collecting round her in the square should disperse. The people thought she was a high priestess of fetish Africa and would work *magie noire*. He told Ray to tell her not to preach in the streets. Sister Geter walked with the boys toward Joliette.
“I thought you were gone away,” Ray said, “so long since I never saw you.”
“No, chile, Ise right here deliv’ring that holy message. The Lawd Him done sent me heah with His wohd in mah mouf, and I ain’t thinking about moving nowheres else tell Himself gimme another marching order. I been preaching that message right along. Sometime the pohlice com’n’ moves me along jest like that one done did. But they nevah hold me no time. They look at mah bible and turn me loose.”
“But the people kain’t understand what you’re preaching to them, since you don’t talk French,” said Goosey.
“What you know ’bout understanding reeligion, yal’ boy?” demanded Sister Geter. “I belongs to the Pentecostal Fire Baptized Believers and I ain’t studying no lang-idge but the lang-idge of faith. I was fire-baptized in the gift of tongues and when I deliver this heah Gawd’s message” (she tapped nervously on the bible and humped herself up, while the boys glanced apprehensively at one another, thinking “Black Bottom”) “people heahs what I say and jest gotta understand no matter what lang-idge they speaks.”
“Funny I didn’t run into you again,” said Ray. “I was away for a little over a month at the vintage, but I’ve been a long time back.”
“Yo’-all don’t see me, mah chilluns, ’causen you don’t want to. For yo’-all know prexactly what’s holding you heah in this mahvelous poht, and that Ise a-preaching against all the most deadliest sins, and theah’s none moh deadly than fohnication. Ise warning you’-all now and straight that Gawd’s sure agwina git you for all them sins youse sweetening on and looking so good on it.”
“We ain’t so bad as you thinking, ma,” said Banjo. “Wese a hard-hustling bunch a regular fellahs. It’s them sweetmens back down in the Ditch you should go preaching to.”
“Yo’-all needs it, too. Mah message is foh you to take it and use. Git converted, git salvation, change you’ ways a living in sweet sin. For if you don’t the Lawd him will git hold a you and wrastle with you and throw you down on a bed a tribulation and give you the biggest shaking you evah did get.”
Banjo began softly whistling, “Shake That Thing.”
They had arrived in the Place de la Joliette before the Seamen’s Bar.
“Let’s go in here for a drink a soda-pop,” said Banjo.
Goosey and Ray grinned.
“Take a glass with us, ma?” Banjo asked.
“No. I ain’t putting mah feets in no gin shop with Gawd’s Wohd in mah hand. I sweahs off gin and any drink that’s sold in a bottle evah since I was fire-baptized, and tha’s seven years gone now.”
“But there might be a sinner in here needing conversion, ma,” said Ray. “The Salvation Army folk don’t mind going into a saloon.”
“Maybe it’s because they loves the smell a the gin they done sweahs off,” replied Sister Geter, “but I doesn’t.”
She waddled away.
The boys went into the Seamen’s Bar and there was *Home to Harlem* Jake drinking with a seaman pal at the bar. He and Ray embraced and kissed.
“The fust time I evah French-kiss a he, chappie, but Ise so tearing mad and glad and crazy to meet you this-away again.”
“That’s all right, Jakie, he-men and all. Stay long enough in any country and you’ll get on to the ways and find them natural.”
Ray introduced Banjo and Goosey.
“I guess youse the two gwine back home with us,” said Jake. “I heard the skipper say some’n’ about it when he got back from the consulate.”
“Well, chappie,” Jake said to Ray, “we just got in last night and wese pulling out tomorrow, so wese all gwina get together and spread some moh joy in this heah sweet poht tonight. What it takes to pay I’ve got, and I’m gwina blow mahself big foh this hallelujah meeting-up. Whachyu say?”
“I say OK,” replied Ray. “But how come you here? You remember you told me you were never going to fool with the sea again?”
“I did say that, yes. But some moh things done happen to me after you quit Harlem, chappie.”
Jake told Ray of his picking up Felice again and their leaving Harlem for Chicago. After two years there they had had a baby boy. And then they decided to get married. Two years of married life passed and he could no longer stick to Chicago, so he returned to Harlem. But he soon found that it was not just a change of place that was worrying him.
“I soon finds out,” he said, “that it was no joymaking business for a fellah like you’ same old Jake, chappie, to go to work reg’lar ehvery day and come home ehvery night to the same ole pillow. Not to say that Felice hadn’t kep’ it freshen’ up and sweet-smelling all along. She’s one sweet chile that sure knows how to make a li’l’ home feel good to a fellah. But it was too much home stuff, chappie. So it done gone a year now I think that I just stahted up one day and got me a broad. And now it’s bettah. I don’t feel like running away from Felice no moh. Whenevah I get home Ise always happy to be with her and feel that Ise doing mah duty by Ray.”
“Ray?” exclaimed Ray.
“Sure, the kid. I done name him after you. Not that I want him to be like you in many ways. But Ise gwina give him what I neveh had and tha’s an edjucation. And p’raps he’ll learn to write pohms like you. He’s a smart-looking kid.”
“You’re a thousand times a better man than me, Jake. Finding a way to carry on with a family and knuckling down to it. I just ran away from the thing.”
“You! You din’t leave Agatha a li’l’ one, did you?”
“I leave more things than I want to remember,” replied Ray. “Come on, let’s have another drink and get outa here.”
Banjo and Goosey hurried off to the consulate to see what orders were there for them and Jake and Ray were left alone to gab about Harlem before and after Prohibition. Jake had found Harlem wonderfully changed when he returned from Chicago. The Block Beautiful had gone black and brown. One Hundred and Twenty-fifth Street was besieged and bravely holding out for business’ sake, but the invaders, armed with nothing but loud laughter, had swept around it and beyond. And higher up, the race line of demarcation, Eighth Avenue, had been pushed way back and Edgecombe, Jerome, Manhattan, St. Nicholas, and other pale avenues were vividly touched with color. The Negro realtors had done marvels.
In Chicago, Felice had begun reading the *Negro World*, the organ of the Back-to-Africa movement, and when they came back she was as interested in Liberty Hall as in Sheba Palace. She had even worried Jake to take a share in Black Star Line.
“Let’s get on to it too, dad,” she had said. “There can be some’n’ in it. Times is changing, and niggers am changing, too. That great big nigger man ain’t no beauty, but, oh, lawdy! he sure is illiquint.”
But Jake had resisted Felice’s new enthusiasm and it was only a few months after their return to New York that Liberty Hall lost Leader Marcus to the Federal jail.
At supper time the boys came together again in the Bum Square. Banjo, playing the square game according to the standards of the bum fraternity, had rounded up all the beach boys he knew to meet Jake and be treated to drinks or handouts. They went to the African Bar. Ray introduced Jake to the proprietor and the two chatted a little about Harlem. Jake stood a round of drinks for the picturesque black rabble.
Banjo, Goosey, Ray, and Jake were going to sup together. Ray suggested they should eat at an Italian chophouse in the quarter where he sometimes fed when he was flush with francs. The cooking there was always well done and moderately spiced as he loved cooking to be, and the wine one grade removed from the *vin rouge ordinaire* was superb for the price and mellow to the palate. And the proprietor could concoct the most delicious *zabaione*.
But when the boys came to the restaurant, Jake objected to feeding there because it was next door to a *cabinet d’aisance*﻿—so much next door that if you were a little gone in your cups you could easily mistake one entrance for the other.
“By the britches of Gawd, chappie!” cried Jake, “what done happen to you sence you show Harlem you’ black moon. After me telling you that I want to blow to the swellest feed there is, you bring me to a place with a big W.C. sign ovah it. I remember when you was a deal moh whimscriminate. When you couldn’t eat the grub on the white man’s choo-choo ’causen you was afraid the chef cook done did nastiness to it as he swoh he would. And now﻿—”
“But just look at that!” cried Goosey. “There’s the whole family setting down to eat right in there.”
Yes. At the lower end of the shop, flanked on either side by cabinets, the family dinner was spread. A long loaf of bread, two large bottles of red wine, and a great basin of soup with a ladle in it. And around the table sat husband and wife, a girl of about fourteen years, a boy a little less, and a shrunken gray grandmother. Clients were coming and going and the family were swallowing their soup amid the sounds and odors of the place, while the wife occasionally vacated her seat to attend to business.
“But don’t they have a *home*?” demanded Jake. “Sure they ain’t sleeping theah, then why does they wanta feed in it?”
“I wouldn’t let nobody see me do that work, much less eat in there,” said Goosey. “These people don’t know any shame.”
“Shame you’ trap,” said Jake. “It ain’t no being ashame’. Can we niggers cry shame about any kind a work to make a living in this big wul’ of the ofays? It ain’t doing the work, but what you make it do with you. You remember, Ray, when you and me was on the road together? When you done finished with that theah pantry hole and I got outa that steaming grave, we couldn’t even stomach them lousy quathers. We was crazy to go any place we could fohgit the whole push.”
“That was seven years gone, Jake,” said Ray. “And in seven years many things can change to nothing according to law. You haven’t changed any. You’re a good black American. Too American. We had a fellow in our gang called Bugsy. He died here﻿—died with his eyes wide open. He was the toughest black boy I ever knew. Yet when he found out he’d been eating horse meat in his cook-joint, thinking it was beef, he cried﻿—”
“He was a real nigger. I woulda puked,” said Jake.
“One day,” continued Ray, “one of my nice liberal friends ran into me here and I happened to tell him about Bugsy and the horse steak. And he was so surprised that a Negro should have prejudices﻿—especially such a delicate sort.”
“Ain’t a bumbole thing delicate about a man being particular what he’s putting away in his guts,” said Jake.
“All the same, Jake, this place, that family. You’re in the most civilized country in the world, and you aren’t civilized enough to understand.”
“I ain’t what’s that?” demanded Jake.
“You heard exactly what I said, ole-timer.”
“Well, whether I is or ain’t, you take me away from here and show me the swellest house-of-many-pans foh feeding in this heah poht of the frogs. Ise got enough a them francs to blow fifty face-feeders with the few dollars I done change. I don’t know what done happen to you in the seven years we ain’t seen one another. But foh the sake a good ole friendship, chappie, I hope you ain’t no stink-lover.”
“What kind a place you want?” asked Ray. “I don’t want to go to one a those places with a lotta stiff, hard-faced people stuffing themselves, and flunkies that don’t know what to do with you because you aren’t like them.”
“And I didn’t mean one a them sort,” said Jake. “I mean a clean place where we can get good eats and jolly one another and be nacheral without anybody being offended.”
They went to the other side of the Vieux Port on the Quai de Rive Neuve where the restaurants specialized in sea foods. A waiter brought them a basket of fine fish and told them to choose. Soles, dorades, loups, mullets﻿—some alive and twitching. Jake insisted on having champagne. And when the smiling head waiter submitted the wine list he chose an expensive brand, Duc de Montebello, because, he said, “the name sounded lak a mahvelous mouful.”
The boys had a gorgeous time feeding and sampling the sparkling liquor and swapping jokes, except for one little snag, which they swept grinning over. At the third table across from them there was a party of two women and three men, and one of the men, who looked like a middle-aged salesman, kept throwing phrases at the boys in English: “It’s good here, eh?﻿ ﻿… You like drink fine champagne.﻿ ﻿… I know many blacks. I been in America.﻿ ﻿… You get good treatment here. Eat good, sleep good.﻿ ﻿… *Les filles.*” He smirked and leered nastily at them and Ray told him in French:
“We don’t know you and we don’t want you to butt into our party.”
A look of mean hatred came into the man’s face and he replied, “You are not polite.”
“I know,” said Ray. “When we don’t let you condescend to interrupt us, we’re not polite, and if any of us had tried to do the same thing with your party we would be impertinent blacks.”
The man’s party paid their bill and left the restaurant a little after the incident.
Then Goosey remarked: “You didn’t have to trip him up so hard, Ray. You know in New York we couldn’t eat in a white restaurant like this.”
“I don’t give a white damn for that,” said Ray. “If we can’t eat downtown we can eat better in Harlem. I wouldn’t give Aunt Hattie’s smoky cook-shop for all the Childs restaurants whitewashed like a tomb. I guess that puss-faced Frenchman was thinking just like you. But all the same, if they let us into their white places, they’ve got to treat us naturally like other guests. This black boy won’t stand for any condescending crap.”
“Mah pardner is right, Goosey,” said Banjo. “For all you’ bellyaching talk about race, youse a white man’s nigger in the bottom. You got you’ haid so low down behind that white moon, believe me, that you kain’t see nothing clear.”
The Bum Square was a close, busy, bustling place when the boys returned there. There were many ships in port﻿—American, English, Norwegian, Italian, and others﻿—and all the common seamen had come to the quarter for amusement. It was like a pit with all things in it﻿—men, women, aged, infirm, boys, touts showing their girls with ghoulish gestures, children, dogs, and cats﻿—all boiling in desire. But there was no free, wild bubbling over. It was a boiling as of purchased food put in a cauldron and carefully fed with fire to a certain point. A boiling exhibition for a strong smell of change was in the Bum Square. Loveless eyes told and hounds’ voices barked without words the price of the circus﻿—boxes, balcony, gallery, parquet, pit, front and rear.
Automatically the piano-panning jumped madly out of the Anglo-American Bar to clash rioting in the square with that of the Monkey Bar.
“Gawd’s love!” exclaimed Jake. “Ain’t no wonder you fellahs stick in this sweet mud. Fust place I evah feel mahself in a jazzing circus some’n’ lak Harlem. It shoh smells strong.”
“Not like Harlem, though,” said Ray. “Harlem’s smell is like animals brought in from the fields to stable. Here it’s rotten-stinking.”
Jake grinned. “You remember you’ send-off feed in ole Aunt Hattie’s cook-shop, chappie? You ain’t fohgit how I done told you there was no other place like Harlem in the white man’s wul’. And now foh putting a li’l’ Harlem stuff in this jazzing.”
A young girl went by them, limping with a pathetic, half-resentful, half-terrified look in her eyes and a fever-hot color in her cheeks.
“See that gal?” Banjo said. “Jest a few months back she hit this Ditch the cutest thing in it. Comed from the country, they said, and, oh, Lawdy! if it wasn’t a rushing wild. And then blip-blap it was the hospital next and look at her now.”
They went to the Ditch to a bistro full of brown and black and Mediterranean seamen, automatic music, strenuously jazzing girls, loud, ready-made laughter, and vigorous swilling of liquor.
Ray’s friend, the chauffeur, was there, drinking and surrounded by an admiring group of pale touting youths. Ray went over to him for a moment. The chauffeur asked him to join the party, but Ray pointed out that he was with Banjo and Jake. He glanced a little inquiringly at the boys. The chauffeur smirked and said: “They are all my boys. They do everything you want them to do. Steal, murder, love in all ways, lie, and spy.”
The boys’ features wore a sickly smile as they listened to the chauffeur boosting them. Ray rejoined his group. A little later Latnah came in with Malty and Dengel. Latnah, peeved, unreconciled to Banjo’s going away, although she had not uttered a word about it, sat rather apart on the edge of the group. The girls of the circle glanced resentfully at her. They could never like the little brown woman. Although quite unobtrusive, the superiority of her difference from them was too eloquently obvious.
The chauffeur left his place and went over to where Latnah was sitting. Standing behind her, he put his arms around her.
Latnah said, “Take you’ dirty hands off me and leave me alone.”
The chauffeur laughed and said, “I am boss of everything here.”
“Except me,” replied Latnah. “I’m not like your Arab wench.”
This Arab girl was different from the Arab-black one. She was honey-colored, with flaky-soft shining coal hair, deeply curled. Her mouth was not cruel, but her eyes were mad. She was one of the chauffeur’s women.
The chauffeur said: “Don’t mention her any more. I’m through with her. We had a big row and I am finished.”
Latnah smiled and said, “You’ve got enough of her, eh?”
The chauffeur attempted to caress her again, but Latnah’s hand shot threateningly to her bosom and he backed away from her.
“You’d better leave that woman alone,” said Banjo.
Right then the Arab girl marched into the café. She bent down with a funny gesture, brought a revolver up from under her skirt and emptied it into the chauffeur. He crumpled to the floor, and she fell upon him and began keening: “I didn’t mean to kill him.﻿ ﻿… I didn’t mean to kill him.”

===Chinese===

第二天中午，雷出了门，脚底下像踩着云。他天性轻快。他去一家小意大利馆子吃了饭。午后刚过，他到一家咖啡馆露台上跟胆小鬼和班卓会合。
他的司机朋友打这儿过，招呼了他。他说要出城去看看他郊区那处宅子，问雷去不去。雷说不去白不去，只是他跟班卓和胆小鬼在一块儿。司机说想去就全带上。他们都想，只是胆小鬼有顾虑：船兴许就到，领事要传人呢。
“那碍不了事，”班卓说，“进了这座哈利路亚港的船，没有当天进当天又出去的。”
“有，美元轮船公司的船就当天走。”胆小鬼说。
“真要当天走，那船长也只好等咱们这帮叫花子乘客了，信俺的，”班卓说，“走，伙计，去了。”
雷坐在司机旁边的副座上。出城的这一路一片萧索。去采葡萄之前，他也去过乡下另一处地方，那时一路上多提神：熟透的绿草里拖着蔓生的荆棘蔷薇，菜花田里金盏花和鸢尾开着，栅栏上挂着一串串馋人的葡萄。如今四下里只有死烂的叶子和几蓬干瘪的黑莓。
司机这处宅子，同现代城市里那支下中产大军人人都有的一块寻常郊区地皮没两样。一座三室一厨的小屋，门口一株小栗树，屋后一株大无花果树。
两边邻家的地，司机告诉小子们，一家是开酒馆的，一家是当警察的。雷心想这邻居配得正好，也照直说了。司机笑了。他也为邻居得意。他说，那两块地比他的值一点钱，因为人家水都通上了，他还没通。
班卓问他这处宅子花了多少钱。
“一万一千法郎。”司机答，还说至今仍在按期付款。
“有时我们在这上头开晚场派对。”他告诉雷。
“什么样的？”雷问。
“要是拉上一对想兜风找乐的游客和姑娘，我就把他们载到这儿来。什么能贴补这宅子都行。有天夜里我载了一整伙，你在场才叫有意思。可你如今同我在土伦认得你那会儿全变了——成天跟塞内加尔人混——我就没好意思麻烦你。”
“没关系的，”雷说，“变是我的爱好。一条车辙不能永远待下去。总得拔出来找点新东西。”
胆小鬼早从他们中间脱了身，正猫在无花果树底下挖土。
“你在那儿干什么呢，你？”班卓喊。
他们走过去，见胆小鬼正往一个矮墩墩的玻璃罐里装土。
“你弄那土干什么？”班卓问。
“留着做纪念。”胆小鬼说。
“哦，俺的老天，瞧这黑鬼！”班卓放声大笑，“该写诗的是你，不是雷，你可是俺见过的头一份的樱桃潜水员。”
胆小鬼把那罐土捧在心口上。
“这是法兰西的泥土，”他说，“我没能混出名堂，没错。我运气不好，可我总能记住我来过这儿。”
“不拎那罐土你就记不住了？”班卓问，“不如腰上挎一瓶真马爹利白兰地带回去，那玩意儿在上帝自家的地界你找不着，土可有的是。全世界的土都是一样的土，顶多颜色差那么一丁点。”
“别搅他。”雷说。
“你不懂这事，班卓，”胆小鬼火了，“所以你最好闭嘴。”
司机一直带着伤感的兴味听着，这时对胆小鬼说：“Vous avez raison. La France, c'est le plus joli coin du monde（您说得对。法兰西是世界上最美的角落）。”
“这你怎么知道？”雷说，“你又没出去过。”
“出去过。”司机应道，“北非、西班牙、意大利、君士坦丁堡我都去过。你忘了，我当过水手。”
“别掰扯土了，找头一家能喝酒的咖啡馆去吧。”班卓说。
下午晚些时候，他们全回了老港。同小子们喝过分别酒，司机开车走了。雷望着他的背影出神，想自己如今对他的感情，同当年在土伦认得他那会儿差别多大。司机在土伦过的日子，同眼下一样，都是那种兽一般狡黠的活法。
他有一回向雷讲过自己被突击搜捕逮住的事：警察从他身上搜出一本微型账本，里头一笔一笔复式记账，记着他捞的所有外快，出钱的场子和人名（认得的才记名字）。
那桩事当时叫雷觉得趣味十足，就像把自己知道的所有门道、暗示和线索教给司机、让他顺藤摸瓜捞点东西一样有趣。那时司机穿着那身花哨的行头——自由莽浪活法的一个用旧了、用烂了的象征——他那些贴补寻欢的营生，在雷眼里并不显得丑。
如今过着一模一样的日子，还多了几分自由，他在雷眼里却变得可憎了。兴许差别就在他图的是什么。至于怎么个活法……多少人身上干干净净珠光宝气，地位权势正是拿人肉生意换来的。
身为黑人，雷对这一事实格外敏感——这个时代多少有头衔的、封了爵的、时髦的、势利的老爷太太们，家产的根子都埋在黑皮肤的买卖里。那他有什么理由怀疑，布迪巷乃至整条沟的房东们，收着姑娘的房租，不正是为了过得体面、让孩子们受像样的教育？
如今叫司机在他眼里丑得没法忍受的是：他下贱地做着人肉买卖，就为从无产阶级的世界里爬出去，爬进那种稳稳当当的体面生活里——从那儿，他就能拿一个体面人那副刻薄、恶毒、犬儒的眼睛，俯视这条沟和一切这样的地方了。
“想想那司机，一万一千法郎买那么处宅子！”班卓对雷说，“才一万一千！俺当初落地马赛身上那些钱就买下了，还能剩一截。”
“买得起，没错，可你不会买，”雷说，“你我天生不是过那种精打细算的皮条客日子的人。在老家，你见过哪个黑人皮条客攒娘们的钱、打算体面地成家？或者哪个棕色皮肤、哭腔哭调的窑姐儿，呜呜咽咽说自己干这行是为了养老娘？你我准保一个也没见过。那路黑人，个个都是血子里带来的真玩客。”
“那位司机揣着他清道夫挣来的钱，良心清清白白地成婚。他兴许把司机的差事一掊，买下一家咖啡馆——当个体面的当家人（père de famille）——当个奉公守法的纳税人，当个强硬国府、铁腕殖民政策的拥虥；你我呢，永远是那两个迷途的黑流浪汉，因为咱们闹不明白这套文明到底是怎么回事。可俺那位司机朋友明白。一千多年的百合花般雪白的文明，才把他造就成今天这个样。他虽然没有头脑，却有文明的本能，班卓，而你我偏偏没有。”
“你说的那个本能的臭玩意儿，俺一点儿参不透，伙计。可俺知道一样：俺身上就算没有生活的臭，也有生活的汁水。”
穿过萨迪·卡诺广场时，小子们看见吉特姐妹正被一个警察领着往小街上走。
“不会是把她抓了吧？”雷说，“这么久没见着，我还当她回老家了。”他正犹豫要不要过去看看怎么回事，班卓说：“不管怎么着，先弄明白他们要把她怎么着，伙计。”
“她兴许又对咱们来那套《黑臀舞》把戏，”胆小鬼反对，“那可就——完了！”
“嗐，走吧。她那套《黑臀舞》，跳不过她天生的那一套。”班卓说。
小子们赶上吉特姐妹和警察，雷同她搭了话。警察问雷认不认识她、她在街上吵嚷什么。他听不懂她的话，因为她不会法语。雷告诉他，她是位布道师。警察放了她，说自己只是把她带开，好叫广场上围拢她的人群散掉。那些人当她是拜物非洲的大祭司，要施黑魔法（magie noire）呢。他叫雷转告她别在街上布道。吉特姐妹同小子们一道往约列特走。
“我还当您走了呢，”雷说，“这么久都没见着您。”
“没有哩，孩子，俺就在这儿，传俺那圣洁的旨意。是主他亲自打发俺来，嘴里含着他的道，主自己不下另一道开拔令，俺压根儿不琢磨挪窝。俺这旨意一直传下来。有时候警察来，把俺往前赶，就像方才那位。可他们从不扣俺。瞧瞧俺的《圣经》，就把俺放了。”
“可您不会讲法国话，人家听不懂您布的道啊。”胆小鬼说。
“你懂什么懂教，黄皮孩子？”吉特姐妹质问，“俺属于五旬节火洗信徒会，俺什么语言也不钻研，只钻研信心的语言。俺领过火洗，得的是说方言的恩赐；等俺传这道上帝的旨意时”（她紧张地敲着《圣经》，弓起了身子，小子们忧心忡忡地面面相觑，心里想着《黑臀舞》）“人们听见俺的话，不管说的是哪国话，就非懂不可。”
“真怪，一直没再撞见您，”雷说，“我出去采了一个多月的葡萄，可回来也老久了。”
“你们见不着俺，俺的孩子们，因为你们压根儿不想见。你们心里明镜似的，是什么把你们拴在这座奇妙的港里；俺传的道，抵的就是那顶顶致命的罪，而罪里头没有比奸淫更致命的了。俺这会儿正告你们：你们甜滋滋泡着、还咂着滋味的那一桩桩罪，上帝笃定要跟你们算账。”
“我们没有您想的那么坏，大娘，”班卓说，“我们是一帮卖力气讨生活的正经汉子。您该去沟里那些甜心郎跟前布道。”
“你们也用得着。俺的旨意就是给你们领去用的。悔改吧，得救吧，把你们泡在甜蜜罪里的活法改了吧。不然的话，主他就要揪住你们，跟你们摔跤，把你们掼到苦难的床上，给你们一生最大的一顿抖。”
班卓轻轻吹起了《Shake That Thing》的口哨。
他们到了约列特广场，正站在海员酒吧门前。
“进去喝杯汽水吧。”班卓说。
胆小鬼和雷咧嘴笑了。
“陪俺们喝一杯，大娘？”班卓问。
“不去。俺手里捧着上帝的道，决不把脚伸进酒馆。打俺领火洗那天起，俺就戒了杜松子酒和一切瓶装卖的饮料，到如今七年了。”
“可这里头兴许有等着皈依的罪人呢，大娘，”雷说，“救世军的人就不忌讳进酒馆。”
“兴许是因为他们戒了那酒、还爱闻那个味儿哩，”吉特姐妹答，“俺可不。”
她一摇一摆地走了。
小子们进了海员酒吧，《回到哈莱姆》里的那位杰克（Jake）正跟一个水手哥们在吧台喝酒。他和雷搂住亲了一口。
“俺这辈子头一回法式亲一个爷们，老伙计，可再这么撞见你，俺真是又疯又喜，忍不住了。”
“没什么，杰克老弟，纯爷们也一样。哪个国家待得够久，你就摸着他们的门道，还觉得挺自然。”
雷引见了班卓和胆小鬼。
“俺猜跟俺们一块回老家的就是你们俩了，”杰克说，“船长从领事馆回来时，俺听见他念叨这事儿。”
“对了，老伙计，”杰克对雷说，“俺们昨儿夜里刚进港，明儿就出港，所以今儿晚上大伙儿凑一块儿，在这甜蜜港再撒一回欢。要花多少俺有，这场哈利路亚的重逢，俺要大大地破费一回。你说呢？”
“我说好，”雷答，“可你怎么到这儿来了？还记得你跟我说过，再也不跟海打交道了？”
“这话俺是说过，没错。可你离开哈莱姆之后，俺又出了些事，老伙计。”
杰克讲给雷听：他怎么又撞见了菲莉斯，两人怎么离开哈莱姆去了芝加哥。在那儿过了两年，生了个男孩。于是决定结婚。婚后两年过去，芝加哥他实在待不住了，便回了哈莱姆。可他很快发现，闹得他心神不宁的，并不只是换个地方的事。
“俺很快就明白，”他说，“像俺这老杰克这样的家伙，天天按点上班、夜夜回同一只旧枕头，那可不是什么开心买卖。倒不是说菲莉斯没把它一直拾报得新鲜喷香。她是个甜孩子，真懂得怎么把一个小家弄叫男人舒坦。可家里味儿太重了，老伙计。到如今有一年了吧，俺想俺就是哪天起了个头，给自己又弄了条‘娘们’（船）。现在好些了。俺再不想从菲莉斯身边跑开了。每回回家，俺都高高兴兴跟她待着，觉得俺这是对雷尽到了本分。”
“雷？”雷喊起来。
“可不，那孩子。俺拿你的名儿给他起的。倒不是指望他方方面面都像你。可俺要给他俺从来没捞着的东西——教育。兴许他还能学你写诗呢。那孩子看着机灵。”
“你比我强一千倍，杰克。找着了路子撑起一个家，还塌下心受住了。我呢，撒腿就跑了。”
“你！你该没把个小家伙留给阿加莎吧？”
“我留下的东西，比我想记住的多得多。”雷答，“来，再喝一杯，出去。”
班卓和胆小鬼赶去领事馆看有什么指令，杰克和雷留下，聊禁酒令前后的哈莱姆。杰克从芝加哥回来时，发现哈莱姆变得叫人惊叹。“美丽街区”（The Block Beautiful）黑了棕了。一百二十五街被围了城，看在生意的份上英勇死守，可那帮入侵者什么家伙都没带，只带着响亮的大笑，早从它两边卷了过去，卷向更远处。再往上，种族的分界线第八大道被推得老远，埃奇库姆、杰罗姆、曼哈顿、圣尼古拉斯，还有别的那些苍白的道上，都点上了鲜亮的颜色。黑人的地产经纪们干出了奇迹。
在芝加哥，菲莉斯读起了《黑人世界》——“回非洲”运动的机关报——回来以后，她对自由大厅的兴趣不亚于示巴宫。她甚至缠着杰克，要他认黑星轮船公司的股。
“咱们也入股吧，当家的，”她说，“这里头兴许有名堂。时代在变，黑哥们也在变。那个黑大个儿长得是不怎么样，可是，哦，老天爷！他那张嘴可真会讲。”
可杰克扛住了菲莉斯的新热望。他们回纽约才几个月，自由大厅就送走了它的领袖马库斯——进了联邦大牢。
到晚饭时分，小子们又在游民广场聚齐。班卓按叫花子兄弟会的规矩下广场这盘棋，把他认得的滩上小子全拢了来见杰克，喝他的请、领他的赏。他们去了非洲酒吧。雷把杰克引见给老板，两人聊了聊哈莱姆。杰克请那群活色生香的黑色闲汉喝了一轮。
班卓、胆小鬼、雷和杰克要一块吃晚饭。雷提议去街区里一家意大利肉食小馆，他法郎宽裕时偶尔在那儿吃。那儿的菜总是烧得到位、香料下得节制，正合他的口味；比普通红酒（vin rouge ordinaire）高一个档次的酒，论价钱算得上乘，入口也醇。老板还能调制最地道的意式蛋奶甜酒（zabaione）。
可小子们到了馆子门口，杰克不乐意在这儿吃了，因为它紧挨着一间厕所（cabinet d'aisance）——挨得那个紧，你多喝了两杯，兴许就把这两扇门认岔了。
“凭上帝的裤衩子起誓，老伙计！”杰克喊，“你亮出黑月亮跟哈莱姆道别之后，到底出了什么事？俺明明白白说想请你们吃最阔气的一顿，你倒把俺带到一家门头顶着个大大的W.C.招牌的馆子。俺记得你从前可比这讲究挑剔多了。那时候白佬火车上的饭你都不敢吃，生怕大厨照他赌咒的那样往里头使了坏。可现在——”
“可瞧瞧那个！”胆小鬼喊，“人家一家老小正在里头坐下吃饭呢。”
可不是嘛。店堂最里头，两侧一边一排小间，家宴正摆着：一条长棍面包，两大瓶红酒，一大盆汤，汤里插着长勺。桌边坐着丈夫和妻子、一个十四五岁的姑娘、一个略小些的男孩，还有一位干缩灰白的老祖母。客人来来去去，一家人就在这片声响和气味中间喝他们的汤，太太不时还得离座去照料生意。
“可他们没有*家*吗？”杰克质问，“他们总不在这儿睡吧，那干吗偏在这儿吃饭？”
“那种活计我都不让人看见我做，更别说在里面吃饭了。”胆小鬼说，“这些人不懂什么叫羞耻。”
“闭上你的臭嘴。”杰克说，“这跟害不害臊不相干。在白佬的这个世界里挣口饭吃，咱们黑人干的哪一样活计好意思喊丢人？要紧的不是干什么活，是你别让那活儿把你干了。你还记得不，雷，咱俩一起在路上跑的那阵？你从那个配餐的黑窟窿里脱了身，俺也从那座蒸笼坟里爬了出来，咱们连那些虱子客栈都咽不下去。咱们发疯似的只想找个地方，把这一整摊子忘个干净。”
“那是七年前了，杰克，”雷说，“七年里头，多少东西都能依法变回乌有。你倒一点儿没变。你是个好样的美国黑人。太美国了。咱们帮里有个伙计叫巴格西。他死在这儿了——眼睛睁得大大的死的。他是我认识的最硬的黑孩子。可他在他做工的馆子里发现自己一直当牛肉吃着的是马肉，他哭了——”
“他是个真黑人。换俺早吐了。”杰克说。
“有一天，”雷接着说，“我一位好心的自由派朋友在这儿撞见我，我碰巧跟他讲起巴格西和马肉排的事。他惊讶得不行——一个黑人居然也有忌讳，还是这么雅致的一种。”
“一个人讲究往自己肠子里塞什么，这跟雅致沾个屁的边。”杰克说。
“话虽这么说，杰克，这地方，这家人。你待在全世界最文明的国家，可你文明得还不够，懂不了这个。”
“俺不够什么？”杰克质问。
“我说的什么你一个字一个字都听见了，老资格。”
“嗐，俺够不够的，你先把俺从这儿带走，领俺去这蛤蟆港里锅子最多的头号大馆子。俺换的那几块美元，兑成法郎，够五十张嘴海吃一顿。咱们七年没见，这七年你出了什么事俺不知道。可看在老交情的份上，老伙计，俺盼着你不至于成了个爱臭的货。”
“你要去什么样的馆子？”雷问，“我可不想去那种地方——一群死板硬脸的家伙在那儿塞肚子，侍者们见你跟他们不一样，手脚都没处放。”
“俺也不是说那种，”杰克说，“俺是说一家干净的馆子，吃得上好菜，咱们互相打趣，痛痛快快自自然然，没人看了不痛快。”
他们过到老港对岸的新岸码头（Quai de Rive Neuve），那一带的馆子专做海鲜。侍者拎来一篮好鱼叫他们挑。鲥鱼、鲷鱼、狼鲈、鲻鱼——有几条还活着，直打挺。杰克非喝香槟不可。笑容满面的领班递上酒单，他挑了种贵的，蒙特贝洛公爵（Duc de Montebello），理由是——用他的话说——“这名字念在嘴里满口生香”。
小子们吃得痛快，品着冒泡的酒，互相丢着笑话，只绊了一根小刺，也是笑着扫过去的。隔着三张桌子那儿坐着一伙人，两个女人三个男人，其中一个看着像个中年推销员的，不停拿英语朝小子们扔话：“这儿不错吧，嗯？……你们爱喝好香槟。……我认得好多黑人。我去过美国。……你们在这儿待遇好。吃得好，睡得好。……Les filles（姑娘们）。”他嘻嘻笑着，斜眼瞟着他们那副下流相，雷用法语回敬他：
“我们不认识您，也不劳您来插我们这一伙。”
那人脸上浮起一层刻毒的恨，回道：“你们不客气。”
“我知道，”雷说，“我们不让您屈尊打断我们，就是不客气；可我们哪个要是敢对您这桌来上这么一出，我们就是不知上下的黑鬼了。”
出了这一出没多久，那伙人结账走了。
随后胆小鬼开了腔：“雷，你用不着把他绊得那么狠。你知道在纽约，这样的白人馆子我们根本进不来。”
“这个我半点不在乎。”雷说，“商业区吃不上，哈莱姆吃得更好。把奇尔兹餐厅全加起来，白得像座坟，我也不拿哈蒂大婶那冒烟的小饭馆去换。我猜那位猫儿脸的法国佬想的正跟你一样。可话说回来，他们既然放我们进他们的白人馆子，就得拿我们当寻常客人一样待。这个黑孩子受不惯谁的屈尊怜悯。”
“俺伙计说得对，胆小鬼，”班卓说，“你种族长种族短的哭丧话说了一箩筐，骨子里却是个白人跟前的黑奴。你的脑袋在那轮白月亮后头埋得那么低，信俺的，你什么都看不清。”
小子们回到游民广场时，那里已是又挤又忙又闹的一片。港里船多——美国的、英国的、挪威的、意大利的，还有别的——寻常水手全上这一带找乐子来了。它像一口大坑，什么都装在里头——男人、女人、老头、病残、男孩、拿鬼一样的手势展示姑娘的皮条客、孩子、狗、猫——全在欲里煮沸。可没有那种自由的、撒野的翻滚漫溢。那煮沸，像花钱买的吃食下了大锅，火小心翼翼地喂到一个份上就停。游民广场上是一场煮沸的表演，煮出一股浓烈的铜板味。无爱的眼睛在报价，狗一样的嗓门不吐一个字地吠出这场马戏的票价——包厢、楼厅、顶层楼座、正厅前座、后座、前前后后。
自动钢琴的叮咚疯了一样从英美酒吧里跳出来，同猴子酒吧的那一架在广场上撞个正着，闹作一团。
“上帝的慈爱！”杰克喊，“怪不得你们这帮家伙陷在这甜蜜泥塘里不肯挪窝。俺头一回在一个跟哈莱姆差不多的爵士马戏场里觉出自己。这味儿可真冲。”
“可不像哈莱姆，”雷说，“哈莱姆的气味，像牲口从地里赶回圈。这儿是烂臭。”
杰克咧嘴一笑。“你还记得在哈蒂大婶老饭馆给你饯行那顿吗，老伙计？俺跟你说过，白佬的这个世界里再没有第二个哈莱姆，这话你没忘吧。现在该往这爵士里掺一点哈莱姆的料了。”
一个年轻姑娘从他们身边走过，一瘸一拐，眼里一副又怨又怕的可怜神情，两颊烧着热病似的红。
“瞧见那丫头没？”班卓说，“就几个月前，她一头扎进这条沟，还是里头最俊的一个。说是乡下来的，哦，老天爷！那真是野得欢。跟着噼啪一下，隔天就进了医院，瞧她现在这样。”
他们进沟里去，进了一家小酒馆——满屋子棕色黑色的地中海水手，自动音乐，拼命扭摆的姑娘，现成的响亮大笑，大口大口地灌酒。
雷的朋友，那位司机，也在，正喝着，身边围了一圈满脸仰慕的白脸皮条客青年。雷过去应酬了一下。司机请他入伙，雷指了指自己同班卓和杰克一伙。司机朝那两位略带探问地瞟了一眼，皮皮地笑着说：“他们都是我的小子。你要他们干什么他们就干什么。偷、杀、样样来地爱、撒谎、当探子。”
听着司机这样抬举他们，小子们的脸上挂着病恹恹的笑。雷回了自家那一伙。过了一会儿，拉特娜同马尔蒂和丹格尔进来。拉特娜怄着气——班卓要走，她心里一直没有平——嘴上一个字没提，只坐在人群边上，离得稍开。圈里的姑娘们怨怨地瞟她。她们永远喜欢不上这个棕色的小女人。她虽然毫不张扬，可她那份与她们的不同里透出的高出一头，明明白白，藏都藏不住。
司机离了座，走到拉特娜坐的地方，站到她身后，两条胳膊搂住了她。
拉特娜说：“把你那脏手拿开，别碰俺。”
司机笑了，说：“这儿的一切，我说了算。”
“俺除外。”拉特娜答，“俺可不像你那阿拉伯娘们。”
这个阿拉伯姑娘同先前那个阿拉伯黑姑娘不是一路人。她蜜色皮肤，一头乌黑发亮、软似绒絮的头发，卷得深深的。嘴不凶，眼睛却疯。她是司机的女人之一。
司机说：“别再提她。我跟她完了。大吵了一架，我腻了。”
拉特娜笑了，说：“她把你喂饱了，嗯？”
司机还想再动手抚她，拉特娜的手唰地护向胸口，那架势带着威胁，他退开了。
“那位女人你最好别碰。”班卓说。
正在这时，那阿拉伯姑娘大步走进了咖啡馆。她做了个古怪的手势弯下腰，从裙子底下抽出一把左轮，对着司机把子弹打了个精光。他瘫倒在地板上，她扑到他身上，嚎了起来：“俺不是存心杀他的……俺不是存心杀他的。”

## XXV Banjo’s Ace of Spades / 班卓的黑桃A
===Original===

A funeral was winding its way through the Ditch. It was not the chauffeur’s, but a policeman’s. He had been shot a day before the chauffeur by a Ditch-dweller just let out of prison. In the Ditch they said it was a story of revenge. It was a large funeral. All the big city officials were there or represented, black-bearded, gray-haired men, black-clothed, decorated, beribboned and medaled. The most important ones had orated valiantly over the corpse, praising the valor and virtues of the force.
*Obseques solennelles.*
A full turnout of the force. And dutiful comrades in service actively making the way clear for the mourning officials and the immense crowd. Wreath-covered hearse and carriages following, chockful of flowers. From the church on the hill above the quarter, slowly, pompously, and solemnly the mournful army went marching through the Ditch and all the girls along the way crossed themselves and all the touts uncovered.
Directly in the line of march, Ray was sitting on the terrace of the African Bar. Not wanting to salute, nor be conspicuous by not saluting, a show stinking with insincerity and more loathsome to see than the obscene body of a crocodile, he got up and went inside, turning his back on the lugubriously-comic procession.
When the noble company had passed far and away out of the Ditch, Ray started off for Joliette to find Banjo and Goosey and give them the farewell hand. But in the Bum Square he met Goosey, who had spent all the morning hunting for Banjo. He had the consular letter from the captain of Jake’s ship on which they were to go home. But Banjo was missing. He had not returned to the hotel after last night’s feasting and merrymaking. Goosey had gone by all the familiar box-holes of the place, but Banjo was not to be found in any.
“Only thing to do is go back to Joliette and wait for him at the hotel,” suggested Ray. “Then if he doesn’t show up in time, you’ll have to go alone.”
They went to the hotel in Joliette and waited on the terrace over a couple bottles of beer. And when the impatient Goosey was becoming unbearably fidgety as the time of the boat’s departure approached, Banjo came rocking leisurely up to them.
“Good God, man, get some American pep into you and don’t act so *African*,” cried Goosey. “Don’t you know we’ve got to move by the white folks’ schedule time now? You think the skipper’s going to wait on us?”
“Don’t excite you’se’f, yaller boy. Go you’ ways without me. I ain’t gwine no place.”
“Not going!” cried Goosey. “After the consul paid for your board and lodging and gave you a free passage back home? You sure joking. You remember Lonesome Blue?”
Lonesome Blue had finally disappeared from the scene. When a ship was found for him he had vanished. The police could not have picked him up again, for he had been furnished papers that gave him immunity. Nobody knew where he had gone.
“Remember you’self, you,” said Banjo. “I ain’t studying you nor Lonesome nor no consuls when I done finish make up mah mind. There is many moh Gawd’s own consuls than theah is in Marcelles and this heah Lincoln Agrippa, call him Banjo, has got moh tricks in his haid than a monkey.”
Goosey looked bewildered and scared of going alone. He was shocked by Banjo’s sudden desertion and felt cheated of his strong support. His lower lip hung down in a mournful way.
“Well, I guess I’ve got to go back alone,” he said. “I’ve been sick near death’s door and would have been in the boneyard like Bugsy if the consul hadn’t helped me out. I’m going home.”
“Sure gwine back this time, eh?” Banjo grinned aloud. “Won’t take no chances telling another skipper to chase himse’f. Yo’ gwine back home to what you call them United Snakes after you done sweahs offer them. You was so bellyaching about race I knowed you’d bust. Ise a guttersnipe as you said, all right, and mah pardner done bury his brains in the mud and we ain’t singing no Gawd’s own blues﻿—”
“That hasn’t got a thing to do with my going back,” said Goosey. “I still hold to my opinion. I know what my race has got to buck up against in this white man’s world, if you don’t know and Ray with his talent don’t want to. I know what I was running away from and if I couldn’t make it over here﻿—”
“Couldn’t make the point of mah righteous nose!” exclaimed Banjo. “Red-nigger, you kain’t make nothing at all but the stuff you was made foh. You done got carried ovah heah by accident. And a li’l’ French luck carried you along upstate. But you done flopped so soon as you got left on you’ own, ’causen you ain’t got no self-makings in you. Get me? You go right on back to them United Snakes that you belongs to with you li’l’ pot a French dirt.”
“And you’ll hear from me, too, some day,” said Goosey. “Some day you’ll hear about me orating for my race and telling them about the soil of liberty.”
With a kind of prayerful gesture Goosey held up his sacred souvenir.
“And you think we don’t care a damn about race, eh?” Banjo turned seriously on Goosey. “Listen and hear me, Goosey. You evah seen a lynching?”
“No.”
“I guess you hadn’t. Well, I seen one down in Dixie. And it was mah own li’l’ brother. Jest when he was a-growing out of a boy into a man and the juice of life was ripening a pink temptation kept right on after him and wouldn’t let be until he was got and pulled the way of the rope. You didn’t go through the war, neither?”
“No, I didn’t.”
“I knowed. Because you was too young. I did because I was jest young enough. I was in Kenada when I joined up and I remember a buddy a mine calling me a fool for it. I remember he said that he would only wanta fight if they was calling him to go to Dixie to clean up foh them crackers. But I joined up all the same, and went through that war, for I was just crazy for a change. And the wul’ did, too. And one half of it done murdered the other half to death. But the wul’ ain’t gone a-mourning forevah because a that. Nosah. The wul’ is jazzing to fohgit.”
“Except the bloody politicians,” said Ray.
“They ain’t in our class, pardner. Yessah. The wul’ is just keeping right on with that nacheral sweet jazzing of life. And Ise jest gwine on right along jazzing with the wul’. The wul’ goes round and round and I keeps right on gwine around with it. I ain’t swore off nothing like you. United Snakes nor You-whited Snakes that a nigger jest gotta stand up to everywhere in this wul’, even in the thickest thicket in the Congo. I know that theah’s a mighty mountain a white divilment on this heah Gawd’s big ball. And niggers will find that mountain on every foot a land that the white man done step on. But we niggers am no angels, neither. And I guess that if evah I went down in the bushes in the Congo, even the cannibnals them would wanta mess with mah moon if I leave me careless, and if I runned away to the Nothanmost pole, the icebugs would squash me frozen stiff if I couldn’t prohect mahself. I ain’t one accident-made nigger like you, Goosey. Ise a true-blue traveling-bohn nigger and I know life, and I knows how to take it nacheral. I fight when I got to and I works when I must and I lays off when I feel lazy, and I loves all the time becausen the honeypot a life is mah middle name.
“You got a li’l’ book larnin’, Goosey, but it jest make you that much a bigger bonehead. You don’t know nothing when to use it right from when you should fold it up and put it away like you does a dress suit after a dickty party. You got a tall lot yet to larn, Goosey boy. You go right on back to them theah United Snakes and makem shoot a li’l’ snakebite wisdom into you’ and take somathat theah goosiness outa you’ moon.”
The noisy honk-honking of a horn dispersed an idly-gossiping group in the middle of the streets as a taxicab dashed through them and swerved to a stop before the hotel. Out of it jumped Jake.
“I done took it in mah haid to come and get you fellahs,” he said. “Because after that theah goodest of time last night, I got to thinking you-all might be feeling too sweet in you’ skin to get outa it for that unrighteous sea change. So here I is with taxi and everything to make sure you-all don’t get left.”
“Youse one most faithfully buddy,” Banjo grinned. “But Ise jest finish explaining to Goosey heah that Ise most gratiate to the consul foh hulpming me this far along, but I ain’t gwine no further. And I was a-telling him like a wise old-timer to dust his feets and make that boat alone befoh it miss him, foh this nigger ain’t gwine no place.”
“Ain’t going!”
Jake grinned. Banjo grinned. Ray grinned. Goosey only was glum. Jake understood Banjo too thoroughly to ask any questions. He enjoyed the situation. For a moment he felt strangely moved to throw himself in with Banjo and send Goosey back alone to the ship. But the next moment he reflected that he was no longer a wild stallion, but a draft horse in harness now with the bit in his mouth and the crupper under his tail, and﻿—that he liked it.
The taxicab slowly trailing them, the boys crossed down the street and into the Seamen’s Bar, where they stood at the counter, *à l’Américaine*, for the final drink together.
“When is you coming back to look us over?” Jake asked Ray.
“When the train puts me off,” said Ray. “I like this rolling along, stopping anywhere I’m put off or thrown off. Like Banjo. I may get off to see you one a these days if the train pass your way.”
“Well, when youse tired a rowling, if evah a broad evacuate you on any a them Gawd’s own beach, you point you’ nose straight foh Harlem. And if it is even in the middle of the night you get theah, we’ll put out that elevator runner that lodging with us and make room to take you on.”
They drove from the Joliette square down the docks to the ship, where they said goodbye. As Goosey went up the gangplank after Jake, Banjo called out again:
“Go’-by, Gawd blimey you, Goosey, and don’t fohgit what I done told you. Put it in you’ flute and blow it.”
Banjo and Ray wandered casually along the docks. Workmen were busy completing the big new American warehouse. The hand trucks were noisy on the paving stones with the shifting of boxes and barrels and the loading and unloading of ships. The eternal harvest of the world on the docks. African hard wood, African rubber, African ivory, African skins. Asia’s gifts of crisp fragrant leaves and the fabled old spices with grain and oil and iron. All floated through the oceans into this warm Western harbor where, waiting to be floated back again, were the Occident’s gifts. Immense crates, barrels, cases of automobiles, pianos, player-pianos, furniture; sandpapered, spliced, and varnished wood; calico print, artificial silk; pretty shoes and boots; French wines, British whiskeys, and a thousand little salesmen-made goods. Composite essence of the soil of all lands.
Commerce! Of all words the most magical. The timbre, color, form, the strength and grandeur of it. Triumphant over all human and natural obstacles, sublime yet forever going hand in hand with the bitch, Bawdy. In all relationships, between nations, between individuals, between little peoples and big peoples, progressive and primitive, the two lovers spread and flourish together as if one were the inevitable complement of the other.
Ray was wondering if it could have been otherwise﻿—if it were madness to imagine the gorgeous concourse of civilization, past, present and to be, without these two creatures of man’s appetites spreading themselves together, when Banjo said:
“Wha’s working on you, pardner?”
“Me? Oh, just when are we going to get outa here?”
“Fed up with the ole poht, eh, scared of it gitting you now?”
“No fear. I’ve got this burg balled up with a mean hold on ’em.”
“Nuts is good dessert, pardner, but I ain’t seen no monkey antics yet.”
“You will when the exhibition is open.”
A Peninsular and Oriental boat had entered a basin farther up the docks and the boys rounded some warehouses to reach it. When they got there they found Malty and Ginger panhandling. The crew was Indian.
“Ain’t nevah nothing doing on a coolie-jabbering boat,” said Malty, deprecatingly, “but it ain’t costing us nothing noways to hang around.”
“The A‑rabs am the best of them people for a handout on a broad,” said Banjo.
There was a company of British soldiers on board and on the upper decks groups of tall, svelte, dignified Indians were conspicuous among the European passengers.
A knot of Senegalese were gathered a little way off to themselves, with their eyes on the galley. Three Indian boys of the beach were signaling to the Indian cooks against the railing above. The cooks seemed unheeding, looking down unsympathetically on the dark rabble beneath them. At last one of them went to the kitchen, returning with a paper packet which he threw down to the three Indian boys. The packet burst, scattering a mess of curried food in the dust. With nervous eagerness the boys seized the packet and scraped up the food from the ground.
The knot of Senegalese began stirring with excitement as their eyes turned the other way from the boat and saw a little cart rumble by them. It bore two scavenger-like whites and came to a halt near the gangway. They had come to get the garbage of the great liner, that was not dumped overboard, but brought into port and sold for the feeding of pigs.
Kitchen boys, two to each can, toted the garbage down the gangplank to dump it in the cart. The rank stuff was rushed and raided by the hungry black men. Out of the slime, the guts of game and poultry, the peelings of vegetables, they fished up pieces of ham, mutton, beef, poultry, and tore savagely at them with their teeth. They fought against one another for the best pieces. One mighty fellow sent a rival sprawling on his back from a can and dominated it until he had extracted some precious knuckles of bones with flesh upon them. Another brought up a decomposed rat which he dashed into the water, and wiping his hand on the sand, dived back again into the can. There were also two white men in the rush. A small Southern European was worsted in the struggle and knocked down, while a big Swede, with the appearance of a great mass of hard mildewed putty, held his own.
“Look at the niggers! Look at the niggers!” the passengers on deck cried, and some of them went and got cameras to photograph the scene.
Once when Ray was badly broke he had gone with Bugsy to sell an American suit and shirt to a young West African called Cuffee. Many of them, British and French black boys, clubbed together in a big room that took up half of a floor, for which each paid two francs a day. They were cooking when Ray got there; the smell of the stuff was good and he was hungry. They offered him some, but Bugsy whispered to him not to eat, because he had seen them picking over the garbage of the docks.
The Africans did not understand the art of panhandling as did the American and West Indian Negroes. When they could get no work on the docks they would not beg food of any ship that was not manned by their own countrymen speaking their language. Seamen who came in with money would help their fellows ashore. But outside of their own primitive circle the African boys were helpless.
“Ain’t you ashamed a you’ race?” Banjo asked Ray.
“Why you think? We’ve been down to the garbage line ourselves.”
“Not to eat it, though. I’d sooner do some’n’ inlegal and ketch jail.”
“It’s just a difference a stomach,” said Ray. “Some stomachs are different from others.” He remembered the time he had worked as a waiter in hotels and how the feeding of certain of the guests was always an interesting spectacle for him. They were those pink-eared, purple-veined, respectable pillars of society who in a refined atmosphere of service always stirred up in him an impression of obscenity. Their bellies seemed to him like coarse sacks that needed only to be filled up and rammed down with a multitude of foodstuffs.
It was a long way from them to these stranded and lost black creatures of colonization who ate garbage to appease the insistent demands of the belly. At night they would go to the African Bar and dance it away.
“Taloufa is right heah with us again,” said Malty.
“Taloufa back in this burg?” exclaimed Banjo.
“You betchu he sure is. And ef you got anything foh helping him, git it ready, for he ain’t nothing this time more’n a plumb broke nigger.”
The boys found Taloufa at the Seamen’s Bar in Joliette, with his guitar, and a bow of colored ribbons decorating it, broke but unbroken. He was talking to an Indian, a thin, gray-haired man.
“I thought you were in England,” said Ray.
“Wouldn’t let me in,” replied Taloufa.
“How you mean wouldn’t let you in?”
From a set of papers in his pocketbook Taloufa extracted a slip and handed it to Ray.
The paper bore Taloufa’s name and fingerprint and read:

===Chinese===

一支送葬队伍正蜿蜒穿过沟区。死的不是司机朋友（the chauffeur），而是一名警察。他在司机朋友之前一天，被一个刚出狱的沟区居民开枪打死。沟区的人说，那是个复仇的故事。葬礼很盛大。全城的大官员都到了，或者派了代表：黑胡子、灰头发的男人们，一身黑衣，挂满勋章、绶带、奖章。顶要紧的几位还在遗体上头英勇地致了悼词，赞扬警队的勇武和德操。
庄严葬礼（Obseques solennelles）。
全体警力倾巢出动。忠于职守的同僚们卖力地为送葬官员和浩大的人群开道。灵车和随行的马车盖满花圈，堆得满满的鲜花。从沟区上方山上的教堂出发，那支哀伤的大军缓慢地、铺张地、庄严地开进沟区，一路的姑娘们个个画十字，掎客个个脱帽。
行进路线正对着雷坐的地方——非洲酒吧的露天座。他不想行礼，也不肯因为不行礼而惹眼——这场表演假得发臭，比鳄鱼那淫秽的尸体还叫人恶心——他起身进了屋，把背影留给这支又阴又滑稽的队伍。
等那支高贵的队伍走出老远、出了沟区，雷动身去若利耶特找班卓和古西，跟他们握别。可在无赖广场他碰见了古西，古西找了一早上班卓。他手里拿着杰克（Jake）那条船的船长开的领事函——他们就搭那条船回国。可班卓不见了。昨晚大吃大玩之后，他没回旅馆。古西把这一带熟识的格子窟全跑了一遍，哪儿也没有班卓。
"只有一个办法：回若利耶特，在旅馆等他，"雷出主意，"到时候他不露面，你就只好一个人走。"
他们回若利耶特的旅馆，坐在露天座上，就着两瓶啤酒等。船开的钟点眼看要到了，急性子的古西急得坐也坐不住，班卓才悠哉游哉、摇摇晃晃地跸了过来。
"老天爷，伙计，拿出点美国劲头来，别这么'非洲'，"古西喊道，"你不知道咱们现在得按白人的钟点走了？你以为船长会等咱们？"
"别激动，黄小子。你走你的，别带俺。俺哪儿也不去。"
"不去了！"古西喊，"领事给你付了食宿，又给了你一张免费回国的舱位？你开什么玩笑。你记得孤独布鲁斯吗？"
孤独布鲁斯到底还是从这一幕里消失了。等船给他找好了，他人没了。警察不可能又把他抓走，因为他手里有给他豁免的证件。没人知道他去了哪儿。
"记你自己吧，你，"班卓说，"俺拿定了主意，就不想你了，也不想孤独，也不想什么领事。马赛有的这种领事，上帝名下多的是；这位林肯·阿格里帕，外号班卓的，脑袋里的把戏比猴子还多。"
古西一脸茫然，又怕一个人上路。班卓突然撩挑子把他吓着了，觉得自己被骗走了靠山。他的下嘴唇委屈地眢拉下来。
"那我看我只好一个人回去了，"他说，"我病得差点儿死，要不是领事帮我，我这会儿早跟巴格西一样进了墓园。我要回家了。"
"这回是真回去了，嗯？"班卓咧嘴笑出声，"再不敢叫另一个船长自己追自己了。你发誓之后，就回你那"众蛇合众国"去了。你为种族哭丧哭了那么久，我就知道你要绷不住。我是流氓窟的孩子，你说得对；可我的搭档把脑子埋进泥里了，咱们唱的也不是上帝自家的布鲁斯——"
"这跟我回去不回去没有半点关系，"古西说，"我的看法还是我的看法。我知道我的种族在这个白人的世界里要顶的是什么，你不知道，雷有那个天分也不肯知道。我知道我从什么底下逃出来的，要是我在这里都闯不出——"
"闯不出俺鼻子尖！"班卓喊起来，"红皮黑鬼，你什么都闯不出，只闯得出你生来该干的那一号。你是偶然被捌到这儿的。一小股法国运气把你送到内地。可一撒开你自己，你就趴了，因为你身上没有一点自己撑自己的料。明白吗？你就带着你那一小盆法国土，回你那个属于你的众蛇合众国去吧。"
"将来你也会听到我的消息，"古西说，"总有一天，你会听说我在为我的种族演讲，给他们讲自由的土壤。"
古西带着一种祈祷的姿势，举起了他那件神圣的纪念品。
"你是想，我们根本不在乎种族，嗯？"班卓忽然正色对着古西，"听着，古西。你见过私刑吗？"
"没有。"
"我看你没有。我见过一回，在迪克西。被私刑的是我亲弟弟。正当他从男孩往男人身上长、生命的汁液正熟的年纪，一个粉红的诱惑缠着他不放，缠到他被逮住、被拖上了绳子那条路。你也没打过仗，对吧？"
"没打过。"
"我知道。你太小。我打了，因为我正好够岁数。我参军那会儿在加拿大，记得一个哥们儿骂我傻瓜。他说只有叫他去迪克西替他们收拾穷白佬，他才肯打仗。可我照样参了军，打完了那场仗，因为我就是疯了心想换个地方。世界也是。世界的一半把另一半活活弄死了。可世界并没有为此一直哭丧下去。没有。世界在用爵士乐把它忘掉。"
"除了那些血腥政客。"雷说。
"他们不跟咱们一个班，搭档。是啊。世界就这么接着走它那甜甜的天然的生活爵士。俺呢，也就跟着世界一路爵士下去。世界转呀转，俺就跟着它转。俺不像你似的什么都戒了。什么众蛇合众国，什么"漂白蛇国"——这一世里黑人走到哪儿都得顶着它，刚果最密的林子里也一样。俺知道，在上帝这颗大球上，有一座白人恶行的大山。白人踩过的每一寸土地上，黑人都碰得见那座山。可咱们黑人也不是天使。俺琢磨着，俺要是下到刚果的灌木林里，一不留神，连那些食人佬也要打俺屁股的主意；俺要是逃到顶北的极点，护不住自己，冰虫也能把俺冻僵了压碎。俺不是你那种偶然造成的黑鬼，古西。俺是道地生就的跑路黑鬼，俺懂生活，懂怎么顺着它自然地受用。该打俺就打，该干俺就干，犯懒俺就歇着，俺什么时候都在爱，因为生活那罐蜜就是俺的中间名。
"你有两本书上的字儿，古西，可它们只把你变成了更大一号的榆木脑袋。你不知道什么时候该用它，什么时候该把它叠好收起来，像赴完大人物晚会收起礼服那样。你要学的还多着呢，古西小子。你就回你那众蛇合众国去，叫他们往你身上打一针蛇毒智慧，把你脑子里的鹅蠢抽掉一点儿。"
街头一群闲磕牙的人中间响起喇叭的嘟嘟声，一辆出租汽车冲开人群，一个急转停在旅馆前。杰克从车里跳了出来。
"俺起了个念头，干脆来接你们这帮伙计，"他说，"昨晚那么痛快一场之后，俺一琢磨，你们兴许赖在自己那层皮里太舒坦，舍不得脱下来换那身不义的海变了。所以俺连人带车都来了，保险你们落不下。"
"你真是顶忠实的哥们儿。"班卓咧嘴笑，"可俺刚跟这位古西讲明白：领事把俺送到这一步，俺领情；可俺不再往前走了。俺还像老江湖一样劝他：掉掸脚，赶在那条船误了他之前一个人上去，因为俺这个黑鬼哪儿也不去。"
"不去了！"
杰克咧嘴。班卓咧嘴。雷咧嘴。只有古西阴着。杰克太懂班卓了，问都懒得问。他挺受用这个局面。有一阵他莫名地心动，想把自己也搭进去跟班卓走，让古西一个人回船。可下一阵他想起来：自己早已不是野种马，是一匹上了套的役马，嚼子勒在嘴里，兜带扣在尾下，而且——他受用。
出租车慢悠悠地跟着，几个人穿过街，进了海员酒吧，站在柜台前，美式做派，喝了最后一轮。
"你什么时候回来看我们？"杰克问雷。
"火车把我放下的地方。"雷说，"我就爱这么一路滚着，把我放哪儿我停哪儿，扔哪儿我落哪儿。跟班卓一样。哪天火车打你们那条道过，我兴许下去瞧瞧你。"
"好，等你滚够了，要是哪个娘们把你甩在哪片上帝的海滩上，你就把鼻子直直对准哈莱姆（Harlem）。哪怕半夜三更到，咱们也把跟咱们搭铺的那个开电梯的撵出去，腾地方收你。"
他们从若利耶特广场下码头到船边，在那儿道了别。古西跟着杰克上了跳板，班卓又喊：
"回见，上帝保佑你，古西，别忘了俺跟你说的。把它塞进你的笛子里吹出来。"
班卓和雷在码头上闲逛。工人们正忙着完工那座新的美国大仓库。搬运车在铺路石上轱辘轱辘地响，箱子桶子挪来挪去，船装了卸、卸了装。码头上世界永远的收成。非洲的硬木、非洲的橡胶、非洲的象牙、非洲的毛皮。亚洲的馈赠：脆生生的香叶，传说中的老香料，还有谷物、油和铁。全都漂过大洋，进这座温暖的西方港湾；等在这儿、等着再漂回去的，是西方的馈赠。大板条箱、桶、汽车箱、钢琴、自动钢琴、家具；砂光、拼接、上漆的木料；印花布、人造丝；漂亮的靴鞋；法国葡萄酒、英国威士忌，还有一千种推销员造出来的小货。万国土地的复合精华。
商业！一切词里头顶有魔力的一个。它的音色、颜色、形状，它的气魄和盛大。战胜一切人为的天然的重障，崇高，却又永远跟婊子"淫荡"手拉手。在一切关系里——国与国、人与人、小民与大民、进步的与原始的——这对情人双双铺展、双双兴旺，仿佛谁是谁命里的另一半。
雷正想着，事情能不能不是这样——想象文明的盛大会流，过去、现在、将来，没有这两个人的胃口造出来的活物双双铺展其间，算不算发疯——班卓开口了：
"琢磨啥呢，搭档？"
"我？哦，咱们什么时候离开这儿？"
"腻了这个老港口了，嗯，怕它这回抓住你？"
"不怕。我早把这座城攥在手心里了。"
"坚果是好甜点，搭档，可俺还没见着猴子把戏。"
"展览一开门你就见着了。"
一条半岛东方公司的船进了上游的一个船坞，两人绕过几座仓库走过去。到那儿，他们看见莫尔蒂和金杰正在讨钱。船员是印度人。
"苦力话的船上一向没戏，"莫尔蒂不抱指望地说，"可反正闲待着也不费什么。"
"论娘们，那一路人里顶肯施舍的是阿-拉伯人。"班卓说。
船上有一连英国兵，上层甲板上，一群高高、苗条、气度庄重的印度人在欧洲乘客中间很打眼。
一小伙塞内加尔人独自站开一点，眼睛盯着厨房。三个印度海滩小子正朝栏杆上头的印度厨子打信号。厨子们好像没理会，冷冷地俯视着脚下黑压压的贱民。末了其中一个进了厨房，回来时拿着一个纸包，朝那三个印度小子丢下来。纸包摔散了，一摊咖喱饭撒进尘土。小子们急切切地扑上去抓起纸包，从地上把饭刮起来。
那一小伙塞内加尔人骚动起来，眼睛转向船的另一头：一辆小推车轰隆隆从他们身边过去。车上坐着两个拾荒模样的人，在跳板口停住。他们是来收大邮轮的垃圾的——垃圾不倒下海，运进港卖掉喂猪。
厨房小子两人一罐，把垃圾罐抬下跳板倒进车里。那臭烘烘的东西立刻被饥饿的黑人们抢扒一空。他们从泔水、野味和家禽的下水、菜皮里，捞出火腿、羊肉、牛肉、禽肉块，用牙野蛮地撕扯。他们为最好的几块互相争打。一个大个子把对手从罐子上仰面推下去，独霸一罐，直到捞出几根带着肉的宝贵的骨节。另一个捞出一只腐烂的老鼠，甩进水里，把手往沙子上蹭了蹭，又扎回罐里。抢的人堆里还有两个白人。一个南欧小个子在争抢中落了下风，被打翻在地；一个瑞典大个子，看着像一大团发霉的硬油灰，守住了自己的地盘。
"瞧那些黑鬼！瞧那些黑鬼！"甲板上的乘客喊，有几个还去取了照相机来拍这场面。
有一回雷穷得没办法，跟巴格西去把一套美国西装和衬衫卖给一个叫卡菲（Cuffee）的西非青年。他们好多人，英国法国的黑小子，合住一间占了半层楼的大屋子，一人一天出两法郎。雷到的时候他们正做饭，那东西的香味很好，他也饿了。他们请他吃，巴格西悄悄叫他别吃，因为他见过他们在码头上扒垃圾。
非洲人不懂美国和西印度黑人那套讨饭的艺术。码头上找不着活儿的时候，不是本国人掌舵、说自己话的船，他们连饭都不去讨。带钱进港的水手会接济岸上的同乡。可出了他们自己那个原始的圈子，非洲小子们就没了抓手。
"你不为你这一族害臊吗？"班卓问雷。
"凭什么？咱们自己也在垃圾线上待过。"
"可没吃过。俺宁可干点犯法的，蹲班房。"
"不过是胃口的差别，"雷说，"有的胃跟有的胃不一样。"他想起自己在旅馆当侍者的那阵子，某些客人的吃相对他永远是一场好看的把戏。就是那种粉红耳朵、紫血管、社会体面支柱的人物，在优雅服务的氛围里，老叫他生出一种猥贞的印象。他们的肚皮在他看来像粗麻袋，只消往里塞、往下捣，塞进一大堆吃食就成。
从那种人到这里这些搁浅的、迷失的、吃垃圾填肚子的殖民化黑色生灵，路远得很。到了夜里，他们会去非洲酒吧，把这一切跳掉。
"塔卢法又回到咱们这儿来了。"莫尔蒂说。
"塔卢法回这破城了？"班卓喊。
"可不，真回来了。你要有啥帮他的，赶紧备好吧，这回他可是彻头彻尾一个光蛋黑鬼了。"
他们在若利耶特的海员酒吧找到塔卢法，带着他的吉他，琴上扎着一束彩带，破产了，可没趴下。他正跟一个印度人说话——一个瘦瘦的、头发花白的男人。
"我以为你在英国呢。"雷说。
"不让俺进。"塔卢法答。
"怎么叫不让进？"
塔卢法从皮夹里一沓证件中抽出一张条子，递给雷。
纸上印着塔卢法的名字和指印，写着：

===Original===

> “The above-named is permitted to land at this port on condition that he proceeds to London in charge of an official of the Shipping Federation, obtains document of identity at the Home Office, and visa (if required), and leaves the United Kingdom at the earliest opportunity.
> (Signed) —﻿—﻿—

Immigration Officer.”

===Chinese===

> "上述人名者准予在本港登岸，条件是：他须在航运联合会一名官员押送下前往伦敦，向内政部领取身份证件及（如需）签证，并尽早离开联合王国。
> （签字）————
> 移民官"

===Original===

When Taloufa arrived in England, the authorities would not permit him to land, but wanted him to go home direct to West Africa. Taloufa did not want to go there. Christian missionaries had educated him out of his native life. A Christian European had uplifted him out of and away from his people and his home. His memory of his past was vague. He did not know what had become of his family.
He tried to convince the authorities that he had a right to land in England. He had friends in Limehouse and in Cardiff. He had even a little property in the shape of a trunk and suitcase and clothes that he had left behind when he failed to return from his last American voyage. Nevertheless, he was permitted to land only to see about his affairs and under supervision.
Colored subjects were not wanted in Britain.
This was the chief topic of serious talk among colored seamen in all the ports. Black and brown men being sent back to West Africa, East Africa, the Arabian coast, and India, showed one another their papers and held sharp and bitter discussions in the rough cafés of Joliette and the Vieux Port.
The majority of the papers were distinguished by the official phrase: Nationality Doubtful.
Colored seamen who had lived their lives in the great careless tradition, and had lost their papers in low-down places to touts, holdup men, and passport fabricators, and were unable or too ignorant to show exact proof of their birthplace, were furnished with the new “Nationality Doubtful” papers. West Africans, East Africans, South Africans, West Indians, Arabs, and Indians﻿—they were all mixed up together. Some of the Indians and Arabs were being given a free trip back to their lands. Others, especially the Negroes, had chosen to stop off in French ports, where the regulations were less stringent. They were agreed that the British authorities were using every device to get all the colored seamen out of Britain and keep them out, so that white men should have their jobs.
Taloufa, under supervision, had crossed from England to Havre, had gone to Paris and, his money exhausted, had come to Marseilles to get a ship in any way he could. The Indian conversing with him was a unique case. Gray-haired, with a fine, thin, ancient, patient face, he was brown and brittle like a reed. He had left India as a ship’s boy when he was so small that he could not recall anything of his people or his home. He had been a steward on English ships for years, before and all during the war.
One day, he said, he came in from a voyage and the medical officer for the local Seamen’s Union put him on the sick list and took him off his ship. He said he was not ill, but he knew that the union officials were replacing colored seamen with white by any means. He went to a reputable private doctor and received a certificate attesting that he was not ill. He took it to the local official of his union, but that official ignored him. He had already put a white man in the Indian’s place as steward. In a fit of anger the Indian foolishly tore up his union card and left the local office.
Weeks and months passed and he did not get another job. One day he was persuaded to take a place on a boat that was going out to stay in service in the East. But when he reached Marseilles, where the crew was to sign on, the steward changed his mind about going to the Far East on a “Nationality Doubtful” paper. Then he came up against the fact that he could not get back into England where he had lived for over forty years. He was six weeks on the beach in Marseilles. He had a pile of foolscap correspondence with the British Home Office. He was a “Nationality Doubtful” man with no place to go.
This was the way of civilization with the colored man, especially the black. The happenings of the past few weeks from the beating up of the beach boys by the police to the story of Taloufa’s experiences, were, to Ray, all of a piece. A clear and eloquent exhibition of the universal attitude, which, though the method varied, was little different anywhere.
When the police inspector said to Ray that the strong arm of the law was against Negroes because they were all criminals, he really did not mean just that. For he knew that the big and terror-striking criminals were not Negroes. What he unconsciously meant was that the police were strong-armed against the happy irresponsibility of the Negro in the face of civilization.
For civilization had gone out among these native, earthy people, had despoiled them of their primitive soil, had uprooted, enchained, transported, and transformed them to labor under its laws, and yet lacked the spirit to tolerate them within its walls.
That this primitive child, this kinky-headed, big-laughing black boy of the world, did not go down and disappear under the serried crush of trampling white feet; that he managed to remain on the scene, not worldly-wise, not “getting there,” yet not machine-made, nor poor-in-spirit like the regimented creatures of civilization, was baffling to civilized understanding. Before the grim, pale rider-down of souls he went his careless way with a primitive hoofing and a grin.
Thus he became a challenge to the clubbers of helpless vagabonds﻿—to the despised, underpaid protectors of property and its high personages. He was a challenge of civilization itself. He was the red rag to the mighty-bellowing, all-trampling civilized bull.
Looking down in a bull ring, you are fascinated by the gay rag. You may even forget the man watching the bull go after the elusive color that makes him mad. The rag seems more than the man. If the bull win it, he horns it, tramples it, sniffs it, paws it﻿—baffled.
As the rag is to the bull, so is the composite voice of the Negro﻿—speech, song and laughter﻿—to a bawdy world. More exasperating, indeed, than the Negro’s being himself is his primitive color in a world where everything is being reduced to a familiar formula, this remains strange and elusive.
---
